- [`research/flyingpixel-twin`](flyingpixel-twin) — single-ray lidar twin vs a finite beam with merged pulses at a depth edge: ghost-range band exactly `2Φ⁻¹(1−ε/D)` beam widths (2.56 at ε/D=0.1, none at ε≥D/2), shifts and narrows for unequal brightness, matches a scan simulation to 0.3%, and a 3-of-5 persistence filter still passes 80–99% of ghosts.
- [`research/footprint-twin`](footprint-twin) — point/circle/calibrated-inflation footprint twin vs a rectangular robot with heading error in a narrow gap: exact pass probability `2Φ(θ*/σ)−1`, circle twins are step functions, a 95%-calibrated inflation is 0.17 m too small at 3× the heading error, 10-gap missions succeed 0.60 where the twin says 1.
- [`research/corridor-twin`](corridor-twin) — scan matching in a corridor: along-track information is exactly zero without door frames, 1.6–6× a room's along-track sd with them; a filter fed the room twin's covariance has NEES 432–1535 over a blind span, and the exact covariance holds only inside a ~0.2 m basin. Stylised.
- [`research/particle-twin`](particle-twin) — Gaussian-sensor twin vs outliers for a bootstrap particle filter: exact ESS fraction `c₀e^{−ay²}`, the mean ESS barely moves (0.671 vs 0.645) while P(ESS<1%) rises 120× (2.5e-4 → 3.1e-2), a twin-sized N=528 fails 4.4% of real steps, and posterior-mean error is 45–421× the twin's.
- [`research/ransac-twin`](ransac-twin) — RANSAC iteration budget from a fixed-inlier-fraction twin (17 for 99%) vs scenes with beta-binomial inlier counts: exact failure 1.3–30% at that budget, 99% needs 19–458 iterations or is unreachable for κ≤2; stylised, line fitting only.
- [`research/sag-twin`](sag-twin) — battery twin with no internal resistance vs a real sagging cell: endurance exactly `(1+√(1−u))/2` of the twin's (0.5 at the fold), 9–94% of charge stranded at the voltage cutoff, a burst of 0.5 fails below 51% charge where the twin's floor is 0 (a flat 20% reserve covers only a third of the full-charge limit), and a constant-derate refit errs +12% to +473% off its fit power. Stylised.
- [`research/chip-toolchain`](chip-toolchain) — reproducible open RTL flow (Yosys, Icarus, Verilator): gate count and depth for adders, a multiplier and a FIFO, SAT equivalence of two adders plus a broken-adder control. Gate counts, not silicon.
- [`research/chip-rtl-probe`](chip-rtl-probe) — what lint, random simulation and a Yosys equivalence proof each catch, on 12 small RTL tasks (companion to `chip-rtl-loop`); mutation probe shows 30 of 389 rejected bugs are caught only by the formal proof. Session-written attempts, not an LLM benchmark.
- [`research/chip-rtl-loop`](chip-rtl-loop) — lint, simulation and formal-equivalence feedback for Verilog repair on nine small tasks; 6/9 pass at round 0 and 9/9 after one round, with the session as proposer (anecdotal, not an LLM benchmark).
- [`research/chip-repair-baseline`](chip-repair-baseline) — no-model mutation-repair baseline for a scripted Verilog checker (compile, lint, simulation, SAT miter) on ten small tasks: 9/20 one-fault cases repaired in 60 evaluations; the session agent passed all ten first try, so the feedback loop is unmeasured. Preliminary.
- [`research/chip-ppa-loop`](chip-ppa-loop) — equivalence-gated area/depth rewrites of a 16-bit adder and 6x6 multiplier against a no-model parameter sweep; best adder 0.85x the reference cells×depth, tied by the sweep, no multiplier gain (stylised, gate counts).
- [`research/chip-scaling`](chip-scaling) — how generic-gate cells and depth scale with bit width for seven arithmetic blocks: adder, comparator and mux are near-linear, the multiplier quadratic (exponent 2.19), and adder depth grows ~2&times;W (stylised, generic gates).
- [`research/chip-constmul`](chip-constmul) — fixed-weight multipliers for all 255 8-bit constants: Yosys's `x * K` tracks binary shift-add (Pearson 0.97 with set bits), and a canonical-signed-digit form has 33% fewer generic-gate cells at similar depth (every form proved equivalent).
- [`research/chip-mcm`](chip-mcm) — a bank of fixed-weight multipliers sharing sub-sums (multiple-constant multiplication): 9% fewer generic-gate cells than per-constant signed-digit shift-add at 2 constants and about 20% fewer at 8 and 16 (43% fewer than Yosys's `*`), every form proved equivalent; random constants, not trained weights.
- [`research/chip-exact4`](chip-exact4) — how far above the SAT-proven minimum gate count Yosys/ABC lands on 4-input logic: the default flow is exactly optimal on only 34% of sampled functions (mean 1.19 gates above, max 4), and a heavier script is worse.
- [`research/chip-cutheadroom`](chip-cutheadroom) — how many gates an exact 4-input rewriter could still remove from real Yosys/ABC netlists: none in 28 of 30 runs (1 gate in two), even where the default script beats plain mapping by 25 to 42%, so the flow's gains are not local.
- [`research/chip-abc-sweep`](chip-abc-sweep) — sweeps five ABC scripts over five adder and eight multiplier structures (all proved equivalent): the script moves cells&times;depth by up to 1.75&times; for one adder, while all eight multiplier structures score identically (stylised, generic-gate).
- [`research/chip-abc-order`](chip-abc-order) — whether equivalent Verilog that differs only in wire names, statement order or the order of added operands synthesises to the same size: names and statement order never change cells&times;depth, operand order does for one carry-select adder (up to 1.43&times;, a 17% better result than the earlier sweep's best; stylised).
- [`research/chip-abc-orderpass`](chip-abc-orderpass) — finds the Yosys pass behind `chip-abc-order`'s operand-order effect: `opt_merge` shares `a+b` between the two carry-select sums only when both are written with `a` and `b` first (4 of 36 operand orders); removing it widens the spread (up to 3.2&times;), so it was hiding variation. One adder, stylised.
- [`research/chip-abc-orderblocks`](chip-abc-orderblocks) — tests whether carry-select blocks share `a+b` independently under mixed per-chain operand orders: the set of sharing blocks predicts Yosys's adder count in 400 of 400 random variants and fixes the cells x depth score (8 sets, one score each per ABC script); the cost is not additive across blocks (stylised, Yosys 0.33).
- [`research/chip-sharesign`](chip-sharesign) — tests whether letting Yosys share a common `a + b` shrinks adders: fewer cells in 54 of 55 design/script pairs but often deeper; stylised, Yosys 0.33.
- [`research/chip-cse-style`](chip-cse-style) — writing the shared `a + b` sum of a carry-select adder by hand (explicitly, or as an incrementer) gives the same netlist Yosys already finds; attaching the constant to `b` first defeats the sharing and is 17-22% smaller under ABC's optimising scripts (stylised, Yosys 0.33).
- [`research/chip-ppa-script`](chip-ppa-script) — re-scores the `chip-ppa-loop` rewrites and sweep under five ABC scripts with every mapped netlist proved equivalent: the adder rewrite gain survives (0.80&ndash;0.86&times; the reference per script) but the multiplier's whole gain is the script (stylised, generic-gate).
- [`research/chip-ppa-logic`](chip-ppa-logic) — equivalence-gated rewrites of 16-bit popcount, 16-bit leading-zero count and 8-bit |a&minus;b| against a no-model sweep: popcount 0.98&times;, |a&minus;b| 0.86&times;, but on leading-zero count the sweep (0.56&times;) beats the session-written rewrites (0.80&times;) in 168 of 200 equal-budget draws (stylised, gate counts).
- [`research/chip-logic-script`](chip-logic-script) — re-scores `chip-ppa-logic`'s popcount, leading-zero-count and |a&minus;b| candidates under five ABC scripts with every mapped netlist proved equivalent: the clz16 sweep-versus-rewrite verdict flips with the script (best 0.35&times; the reference); stylised, generic gates.
- [`research/chip-ppa-mac`](chip-ppa-mac) — a fused 4&times;4 multiply-accumulate: no written rewrite beats `a*b+c` under the default or three of five ABC scripts, but carry-save forms win by up to 9% cells&times;depth under `dch` and `resyn2` (stylised, generic-gate).
- [`research/chip-ppa-sky130mac`](chip-ppa-sky130mac) — the `chip-ppa-mac` carry-save MAC rewrites mapped to SKY130 HD cells: the plain `a*b+c` under its best script is the overall best, the generic-gate score ranks candidates only weakly like cell areaxdelay (Spearman 0.20; stylised, ABC delay estimate).
- [`research/chip-scriptsearch`](chip-scriptsearch) — an exhaustive 800-point table of SKY130 areaxdelay over ABC pass sequences for the MAC (optimum 11% below the best earlier script; needle-like landscape), with random search, hill climbing and session-written proposals compared at equal budget (stylised, preliminary).
- [`research/accel-kws`](accel-kws) — keyword-spotting accelerator: quantised MLP on Speech Commands, serial-MAC RTL bit-exact against an integer reference, accuracy vs cycles, cells and modelled energy over width and weight bits (stylised).
- [`research/accel-ttfit`](accel-ttfit) — a streaming keyword-spotting engine with no weight memory, exact against an integer reference and synthesised to SKY130 on 32 geometries (6,300 to 24,500 um2); 16 hidden units at 8-bit weights is the most accurate (0.616) that fits one assumed 1x1 Tiny Tapeout tile at 70% utilisation, 64 units at 4 bits (0.738) needs two. Stylised: synthesis only.
- [`research/accel-ttretrain`](accel-ttretrain) — all 96 retrained keyword-spotting models fit the Tiny Tapeout engine's 16-bit bias and 20-bit accumulator limits (worst use 43% and 30%), accuracy through the tile contract equals the reference, and 32 trained models run bit-exact in RTL.
- [`research/accel-codesign`](accel-codesign) — model-first vs joint choice of model and MAC lane count under a latency deadline, on the 40 measured `accel-kws` models; accepting 2 validation points less cuts engine area 31–42% (stylised, table-based, cycles for more than one lane analytic).
- [`research/accel-nsga`](accel-nsga) — NSGA-II against random search at equal budgets on a 3,840-design keyword-spotting accelerator space with exact ground truth; NSGA-II finds 54 to 89% of the exact front at 20.8% of the space against 19 to 21% for random search, though hypervolume is 0.97 to 1.00 for both. Stylised.
- [`research/accel-nsgatune`](accel-nsgatune) — sweeps NSGA-II's population, mutation rate and crossover on the `accel-nsga` space; mutation 0.02 to 0.1 finds about 0.38 and 0.71 to 0.77 of the exact front at 400 designs, against 0.28 and 0.59 at `accel-nsga`'s setting and 0.13 and 0.12 for random search (stylised).
- [`research/accel-dataflow`](accel-dataflow) — weight-, output-, input-stationary and reduction-tree dataflows of a P-lane MAC array on the 40 `accel-kws` models, from a cycle-by-cycle schedule simulator that matches closed forms exactly and the P = 1 RTL cycles; output-stationary wins on SRAM energy at batch 4 until an input buffer is allowed, then input-mapped flows win at 32 lanes (stylised, assumed energy constants, no RTL for more than one lane).
- [`research/accel-dataflowbuf`](accel-dataflowbuf) — how large a bounded LRU activation buffer must be before output-stationary stops being the best dataflow for the 40 `accel-kws` models at batch 4: 32, 64 and 128 words at 8, 16 and 32 lanes (for 10 to 30 of 40 models, input-stationary wins), and not at all at batch 1; buffer-read energy is assumed and no area is charged (stylised).
- [`research/accel-plane`](accel-plane) — P-lane output-stationary and reduction-tree matrix-vector engines in Verilog, bit-exact against NumPy at 1 to 32 lanes, with RTL cycles equal to `accel-dataflow`'s schedule model plus a constant 2 or 3 at every point, tails included (stylised, generic-gate area, batch 1, no memories).
- [`research/accel-planebatch`](accel-planebatch) — output- and weight-stationary batched RTL engines at batch 1 to 16: bit-exact, cycles and operand-fetch counts equal `accel-dataflow`'s schedule model, and weight-stationary's saved weight fetches cost up to 4x the generic gates (stylised, P = 8, no memories).
- [`research/accel-fetchprice`](accel-fetchprice) — breakeven price ratios from accel-planebatch's RTL counts: register-file weight-stationary beats output-stationary only if an idle flop costs under ~1% of a weight fetch per cycle at batch 4 (falling ~1/B); no energy constant measured (stylised).
- [`research/accel-pdkprice`](accel-pdkprice) — places accel-fetchprice's breakevens against SKY130 liberty energies (idle flop 46 fJ per cycle; OpenRAM macro 10 to 90 pJ per access): with weights packed into SRAM words register-file weight-stationary loses to output-stationary at nearly every batch; partial sums in SRAM lose everywhere. Library values, not measurements.
- [`research/accel-gatedprice`](accel-gatedprice) — prices clock-gated register-file weight-stationary scheduling from SKY130 and GF180 liberty files: an idle clock gate costs 0.86 of an idle flop (SKY130; 0.99 to 1.02 in GF180), so one gate per 24-bit accumulator cuts the idle cost about 28-fold and flips register-file WS from losing to winning against packed SRAM weight fetches in 35 of 64 cells, in all but one cell at 24 flops per gate. Library values, not measurements.
- [`research/accel-gatedrtl`](accel-gatedrtl) — the clock-gated register-file weight-stationary engine in RTL and SKY130 synthesis: bit-exact, same accumulator writes as output-stationary, clock-pin energy 1.0 to 1.07x OS with a gate per set, but area only 7 to 18 % below ungated WS and 1.2 to 5.3x OS (stylised).
- [`research/accel-gatedlanes`](accel-gatedlanes) — the gated weight-stationary engine's lane count swept from 2 to 16 (32 ran out of synthesis memory): bit-exact, clock-pin energy 1.01 to 1.07x OS with a gate per set at every P, area 1.6 to 1.9x OS at B = 4 and flat from P = 4, so the verdict depends on the batch, not the lanes (stylised).
- [`research/accel-gatedmux`](accel-gatedmux) — why the gated weight-stationary engine needed so much synthesis memory: one variable-index read of the accumulator bus; an AND-OR read mux gives bit-exact, same-cycle engines 17 to 28 % smaller, gated WS 1.5x OS area at B = 4 and 2.2x at B = 8 (was 1.6 to 1.9x and 2.9 to 3.0x), and lets P = 64 synthesise (stylised).
- [`research/accel-ihpprice`](accel-ihpprice) — second-node verdict for clock-gated register-file weight-stationary scheduling from IHP SG13G2 liberty files (idle flop 34 fJ, clock gate 0.75 of a flop, ten SRAM macros with separate read energies): one gate per accumulator makes WS beat OS in all 120 cells, but ungated WS also wins 33 of 60 packed cells because IHP's SRAM costs 72 to 497 flop-cycles per weight against SKY130's 18 to 62. Library values, not measurements.
- [`research/accel-planemux`](accel-planemux) — does `accel-gatedmux`'s accumulator-read bug also inflate `accel-planebatch`'s WS area? No: the rewritten engine is bit-exact with identical counts and its area is within -3 % to +12 % with no trend (WS/OS 1.62 vs 1.66 at B = 4); the earlier verdict stands.
- [`research/accel-planefpga`](accel-planefpga) — accel-plane's output-stationary and reduction-tree engines at 1 to 32 lanes through Yosys and nextpnr-ice40 on an iCE40 UP5K: output-stationary at 16 lanes is fastest that fits (10.2 us per 64x70 product, 2,942 of 5,280 cells); DSP mapping cuts logic 13 to 41% but fails to place beyond 8 lanes. Stylised, synthesis only.
- [`research/accel-surrogate`](accel-surrogate) — can a quantised MLP surrogate of an FE cantilever beat the Euler-Bernoulli vs Timoshenko gap (0.09 % median), and would an accelerator pay? No: float error is 1.7 % (18x the gap), 6-bit QAT stays within 0.3 points of float, and the surrogate only beats the solver above ~1,600 simulator flops. Stylised.
- [`research/accel-surrogatelock`](accel-surrogatelock) — with a locking-free Q6 finite-element simulator, how much surrogate error can a design loop tolerate? At 64x8 the surrogate adds 0.5 to 0.8 points to the FE's 2.3 % error; with no safety margin a min-mass screen breaks the limit in 24 to 33 % of cases, with a 5 to 10 % margin 2 to 9 %. Stylised.
- [`research/accel-surrogateloop`](accel-surrogateloop) — does a quantised surrogate make a design search cheaper in FE solves than searching with the simulator alone? At 150 FE evaluations a surrogate-assisted loop is 2.3 to 2.8 % from a best-known design against 6 to 8 % for an FE-only evolution strategy and 14 to 20 % for random search (float and 6-bit surrogates indistinguishable; stylised, 8 seeds).
- [`research/accel-surrogate2d`](accel-surrogate2d) — when the beam simulator is a 2D plane-stress FE model, does a quantised MLP surrogate (and an accelerator) pay? The energy break-even is met (34x at 32x4 mesh, 319x at 64x8, assumed constants), and the surrogate's 2 % error against its FE is small beside the FE's own 9 % discretisation error; but the locking Q4 simulator is itself inaccurate. Stylised.
- [`research/accel-policy`](accel-policy) — can a small behaviour-cloned control network meet the control-rate deadline on a microcontroller or a P-lane accelerator for a two-link arm? Stylised and preliminary: the int8 policies track 300+ times worse than their teacher, so policy accuracy, not hardware speed, is the limit; the latency model (assumed constants) shows hardware matters only above about 10 kHz.
- [`research/accel-sparse`](accel-sparse) — pruning, int4/int8, ternary and binary MLPs on scikit-learn digits (substitute task) with a modelled-energy engine: when does the accuracy slack let the hardware drop multipliers? Stylised; at 1–2 points tolerance, pruned int8 still beats multiplier-free.
- [`research/accel-kwssparse`](accel-kwssparse) — `accel-sparse`'s pruning, int4/int8, ternary and binary sweep re-run on real Speech Commands MFCC features: no multiplier-free design is within 5 points of float32 (ternary 19 down), where digits said 1; int8 pruned 50% is the cheapest within 2 points. Stylised, untuned negative.
- [`research/accel-lut`](accel-lut) — weight-sharing product-table inference against int8 MAC engines on the digits MLP: negative at this size, break-even only for wide layers with cheap table reads. Stylised, modelled energy.
- [`research/accel-lutft`](accel-lutft) — fine-tuning the shared values of `accel-lut`'s product-table engine: rescues K=2 in the 75%-pruned digits MLP (14% under int8 energy only at an assumed cheap table read), not the dense network. Stylised, modelled energy.
- [`research/accel-lutwide`](accel-lutwide) — product-table inference on trained wide digits MLPs (32, 128, 512 hidden): at best 14% under int8 energy, only with an assumed cheap table read. Stylised, modelled energy.
- [`research/accel-lutrtl`](accel-lutrtl) — the product-table engine and a serial MAC engine in Verilog, bit-exact against NumPy and synthesised with Yosys: negative, the table needs an accumulator per output (stylised)
- [`research/accel-fpga`](accel-fpga) — the `accel-kws` serial-MAC engine through Yosys and nextpnr-ice40 on UP5K and HX8K for 15 geometries (random weights, synthesis only): all fit in at most 22% of the UP5K's logic, weights land in block RAM, Fmax 15–22 MHz on UP5K. Stylised; no board, no power.
- [`research/chip-accel`](chip-accel) — parameterised MAC-lane accelerator for an 8-8-4 quantised MLP, bit- and cycle-exact against NumPy in Icarus; lanes x bit-width sweep puts the cells-times-cycles minimum at 4 lanes (stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-pnr`](chip-pnr) — synthesis-only area and delay versus placed-and-routed OpenROAD numbers on SKY130 for 13 small designs (stylised, global-route estimates).
- [`research/chip-systolic`](chip-systolic) — R x C output-stationary systolic MLP engine, bit- and cycle-exact against NumPy; at matched MACs it loses to `chip-accel`'s MAC lanes on cells-times-cycles (about 1.6x to 1.8x) but wins on latency (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-batch`](chip-systolic-batch) — batch-size sweep of the systolic MLP engine (R = 4, 8, 16): the array's cells-times-cycles disadvantage against `chip-accel`'s MAC lanes widens from 1.6x to 2.9x because its area grows with the batch (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-small`](chip-systolic-small) — small reused systolic arrays (R = 1, 2, 4 rows) against `chip-accel`'s MAC lanes: even the best, 1x4, has 1.35x the cells-times-cycles per vector (65702 vs 48546) at fan-in 8 (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-fanin`](chip-systolic-fanin) — fan-in sweep (F = 8, 16, 32) of small systolic arrays against `chip-accel`'s MAC lanes: the best array costs 1.36x, 1.25x and 1.26x the cells-times-cycles per vector of the best MAC engine, so the gap narrows but does not close (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/eda-harness`](eda-harness) — unattended design loop (lint, bit-exact RTL sim, gate-netlist check, Yosys synthesis, hash cache, JSONL log, pluggable proposers) run on `chip-accel`'s 40-design grid: synthesis and the gate check are 99% of wall time (stylised, generic-gate counts, no place and route).
- [`research/eda-gridxfer`](eda-gridxfer) — does a space-filling grid order beat the harness's lexicographic one on other families? Lexicographic is arbitrary (worst on MAC, best on FIR); farthest-point beat random from about 6 evaluations on every table, and `eda-harness` now offers it as an option (stylised, two 36-design families).
- [`research/eda-gridorder`](eda-gridorder) — does the order a grid walks a design space matter? The harness's lexicographic grid trails every random order to 30 evaluations; Halton reaches 90% of the front in 7 evaluations against 17 for random (stylised, one table, deterministic orders are single draws).
- [`research/eda-proposers`](eda-proposers) — random, grid, annealing, GA, NSGA-II and TPE at equal budgets by Pareto hypervolume on an 80-design logged space: TPE and NSGA-II lead random from about 16 to 30 evaluations, annealing trails it (stylised, tiny space, untuned).
- [`research/eda-fidelity`](eda-fidelity) — a three-rung fidelity ladder (analytic model, RTL simulation, synthesis) over 40 logged designs: the free rung ranks the objective at Spearman 0.74, and successive halving finds the optimum at 20 s of tool time against 205 s to synthesise everything (stylised, toy design family, no place and route).
- [`research/eda-surrogate`](eda-surrogate) — a cell-count surrogate from cheap word-level RTL features over 133 designs in four generator families: 3.9% median error on families it saw (non-negative linear model), 11% to 210% on a held-out family with rank agreement staying at Spearman 0.75 to 1.00, and it cuts syntheses to find a near-best design in a 36-design pool from 12.4 to 1-5 (stylised, synthesis-only target, no place and route).
- [`research/eda-adapt`](eda-adapt) — few-shot adaptation of the `eda-surrogate` cell-count model to an unseen design family: one synthesised design from the new family cuts the linear model's median error from 49% to 18%, training on the new family alone beats pooling from about three designs, boosted trees need about 12 (stylised, synthesis-only target, no place and route).
- [`research/eda-adaptpick`](eda-adaptpick) — which few designs of a new family to synthesise when adapting a cell-count surrogate: a farthest-point spread in feature space roughly halves the linear model's error against random draws at 5 designs (5.3% against 8.5%), the smallest designs are worst, and upweighting new points helps linear and ridge but not boosted trees (stylised, synthesis-only target).
- [`research/eda-adaptfixed`](eda-adaptfixed) — `eda-adaptpick`'s adaptation-design choosers re-scored on one fixed test set per family (4 families x 5 splits): the farthest-point spread still beats random draws for the linear model at every k (4.8% against 6.0% at 5 designs, w = 5) but wins in only 65% of cells, the smallest-first rule stays worst, and the earlier 'halves the error' margin shrinks to 20-45% at 5 to 8 designs.
- [`research/eda-screen`](eda-screen) — a cell-count surrogate used as a screen in the design-search loop: 4 spread syntheses plus a log-linear fit reach 0.990 of the true front's hypervolume at 12 syntheses against 0.860 for random and 0.878 for TPE, of which about +0.05 comes from free cycles/error objectives (stylised, one 80-design space).
- [`research/eda-refpoint`](eda-refpoint) — TPE stays best under five hypervolume reference points, recall, IGD+ and epsilon, but its lead over random at budget 30 ranges from +0.045 to +0.002 with the reference point (stylised, one 80-design space).
- [`research/eda-noisy`](eda-noisy) — with Gaussian error on the objectives a proposer sees, TPE's budget-30 lead over random falls from +0.037 to +0.006 (gone at error 0.8 of the objective range), NSGA-II and the genetic method lose theirs by 0.1, annealing is never better (stylised, synthetic noise, one 80-design space).
- [`research/eda-cheapaware`](eda-cheapaware) — a random search that uses the free cycles/error objectives reaches 0.901 of the true front's hypervolume at 12 syntheses (random 0.860, screen 0.990), so +0.04 of the screen's lead is the free objectives and +0.09 the cell-count surrogate (stylised, one 80-design space).
- [`research/eda-screenxfer`](eda-screenxfer) — a Pareto screen whose cell-count model was trained only on other design families reaches 0.94 to 0.98 of the true front's hypervolume with 3 Yosys syntheses (random about 0.65), though the model's absolute error is large; ranking is what matters (stylised, MAC and FIR spaces of 27 designs).
- [`research/eda-verify`](eda-verify) — mutation study of random, coverage-directed and bounded-formal RTL checks on the MAC accelerator: 64 random vectors kill 99% of killable mutants, formal 71% (timeouts), at ~50x the CPU cost (stylised).
- [`research/eda-checkorder`](eda-checkorder) — replays the eda-verify mutation log to cost check cascades: random simulation before formal cuts CPU by 54% with more kills; the order among cheap stages barely matters (stylised re-analysis).
- [`research/eda-earlystop`](eda-earlystop) — replays the eda-verify log with a time cap on formal: a 170 s cap loses no kill and saves 41% of cascade CPU but forfeits every equivalence proof; a 230 s cap keeps 15 of 16 proofs and saves 23% (stylised re-analysis).
- [`research/eda-equivpredict`](eda-equivpredict) — predicts from the mutated source line which simulation survivors formal can prove equivalent: nearest-line prediction cuts formal CPU 55% and finds 14 of 16 proofs on one design (stylised, 27 mutants).
- [`research/eda-equivinterval`](eda-equivinterval) — puts a line-clustered interval on eda-equivpredict's 93%: 25 of 27 survivors right, 95% CI 76.5–100% over 11 distinct lines, permutation p = 0.012 (stylised, one design).
- [`research/eda-equivxfer`](eda-equivxfer) — tests whether an identical post-synthesis netlist predicts formal equivalence on a second design (systolic array) as well as the MAC engine: right 3 of 3 times but finds only 3 of 28 proved equivalents (stylised, two designs).
- [`research/eda-conefeat`](eda-conefeat) — tests whether Yosys netlist features (cell-count change, miter size) predict which eda-verify survivors are provably equivalent: none beats the 59.3% majority class under leave-one-line-out, against 92.6% for source line (negative, stylised, one design).
- [`research/eda-conehash`](eda-conehash) — fan-in cone features (changed sinks, cone gates and depth, registers reached) from hashed gate-level netlists vs which eda-verify survivors are provably equivalent: none beats the 59.3% majority class under leave-one-line-out (best 13 of 27; negative, stylised, one design).
- [`research/eda-flowtune`](eda-flowtune) — all 400 ABC pass sequences on eight small SKY130-mapped designs: one design's best recipe scores a mean 1.31× the other's optimum, and a transferred warm start helps only one design (stylised, toy designs, no place and route).
- [`research/eda-detectprob`](eda-detectprob) — per-vector detection probabilities of the eda-verify mutants: a geometric model fits the random-vector miss rate within 0.005, predicts 45 vectors for a 1% miss, and 1,280 clean vectors cannot rule out a bug rarer than 0.0023 per vector (stylised re-analysis).
- [`research/eda-deepdraw`](eda-deepdraw) — 20,000 further random vectors catch none of the 25 eda-verify survivors, so none contradicts a formal equivalence proof; a random-visible bug among the 9 formal timeouts has per-vector detection probability below 1.4e-4.
- [`research/chip-systolic-edge`](chip-systolic-edge) — extends the fan-in sweep past its edges (C = 16, P = 32): the optima move inward and the best array still costs 1.25x (F = 16) and 1.20x (F = 32) the cells-times-cycles per vector of the best MAC engine (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-hidden`](chip-systolic-hidden) — fixes the hidden width at 8 and sweeps the input fan-in F = 8, 16, 32: the best MAC engine still beats the best systolic array, by 1.35x, 1.22x and 1.20x in cells-times-cycles per vector (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-mac-lanes`](chip-mac-lanes) — builds and RTL-checks every MAC-lane count P = 1 to 20 on the F-16-4 network (F = 16, 32): the best P is 8 and 16, no non-power-of-two wins, and P above 16 only adds cells (stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-hidewide`](chip-systolic-hidewide) — widens the hidden layer to 16 (F = 16, 32) and lets the MAC engine reach P = 16: the best MAC engine still beats the best systolic array, by 1.30x and 1.27x in cells-times-cycles per vector, and at F = 32 its best lane count is again the largest tried (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-p32`](chip-systolic-p32) — extends the hidden-16 comparison to MAC lanes up to P = 32 and arrays up to 4x8 with every design simulated: the best MAC engine still wins, by 1.30x and 1.28x in cells-times-cycles per vector; P = 32 adds no cycles over P = 16 (negative, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-systolic-abcrobust`](chip-systolic-abcrobust) — re-scores `chip-systolic-p32`'s 20 designs under five ABC scripts: the best MAC engine beats the best systolic array in all ten F x script cases, by 1.28x to 1.60x (negative for arrays, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-macp32cells`](chip-macp32cells) — re-synthesises the 16- and 32-lane MAC engines (same cycles) with three seeds and three Yosys flows: the sign of the cell-count gap depends on the flow, with swings of 1-10%, so `chip-systolic-p32`'s 0.7% ranking is not reliable (artefact-sensitive, stylised, generic-gate counts).
- [`research/chip-p32netlist`](chip-p32netlist) — counts cells of the 16- and 32-lane MAC engines after the word-level, bit-level and abc stages: the 32-lane engine is bigger at the word level, about 500 cells smaller after techmap, so the `chip-macp32cells` gap is set before abc (localised, mechanism unexplained, stylised).
- [`research/chip-p32passes`](chip-p32passes) — runs the Yosys `fine` passes one at a time on the 16- and 32-lane MAC engines: the P = 32 versus P = 16 sign flips (+2,100 to about -120 cells) at the first `opt -fast` after techmap, before any abc, and `chip-p32netlist`'s pre-abc label is corrected (Yosys 0.33's `-run :abc` includes `abc -fast`). Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-sequence`](robofab-sequence) — assembly-order planning for a printed gearbox: exact subset DP, collision-only admits 10.6% of orders but support checking leaves 0.077%, the collision-only optimum is infeasible, greedy disassembly needs 4 direction changes vs 1 optimal. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-settle`](robofab-settle) — MuJoCo settle tests of all 2,048 partial gearbox assemblies vs the quasi-static support rule: 95.6% agreement, 81 mutual-support false accepts and 10 bore-capture false rejects, valid-order count unchanged at 30,760. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-insert`](robofab-insert) — peg-in-hole search policies tuned by CEM in MuJoCo at three fidelity levels or with domain randomisation: tuned on the simplest level 0.91 success there but 0.53 / 0.26 on a stiffer-contact / held-out level; training on the stiffer level or with randomisation gives 0.43–0.46 held-out and cannot be ranked with 5 seeds. Stylised, sim-to-sim.
- [`research/robofab-program`](robofab-program) — session-written pick-and-place programs checked in a kinematic SCARA twin at three fidelities against a held-out reference: of 69 reference-failing injected-bug mutants the levels reject 38, 46 and 60 with no false alarms; misses are unmodelled effects (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-cell`](robofab-cell) — discrete-event twin of an arm, printers and an inspection station: printers have the highest utilisation at every size, yet at 6 printers an extra arm saves 10.9 min vs 2.3 for an extra inspection station; dispatch rules trade makespan for flow time; a mean-value twin is optimistic. Stylised, illustrative parameters.
- [`research/robofab-cell-bound`](robofab-cell-bound) — discrete-event twin of an arm, 3D printers and an inspection station: a utilisation bound names the bottleneck (printers to 10, the arm from 12, ~12.7 good parts/h) and the simulation sits 10–11% below it near the switch; a 25% faster arm lifts 11.34 to 12.68 parts/h at 12 printers, a second inspector nothing (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-cell-mva`](robofab-cell-mva) — exact mean value analysis of the arm, printers and inspection network against a discrete-event twin: a few per cent off for exponential times, up to 11% low for less variable ones, better than the utilisation bound in most real-semantics rows, and it overshoots saturation at high work in process. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-cv`](robofab-cell-cv) — coefficient-of-variation sweep and a measured printer stall share for the arm-printer-inspection queueing model: MVA's error crosses zero near cv 1 while the utilisation bound stays high, and held-printer capacity times (1 − stall) reproduces the saturated rate at 4 and 8 printers. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-monitor`](robofab-cell-monitor) — is a print-failure camera worth it in a robot-arm printer cell? Blind printing against abort-on-failure: +6 to 10% good parts per hour at 2 to 10 printers, none once the arm limits the cell; break-even false-stop rate. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-detector`](robofab-cell-detector) — where should a print-failure detector's alarm threshold go in a robot-arm printer cell? A look every 2 min makes a z = 2 threshold 19 to 23% worse than blind printing; the best z is 3 to 3.5, worth +6% (d' = 2) to +11% (d' = 4). Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-fescsweep`](robofab-cell-fescsweep) — where does the flow-equivalent-server decomposition of a robot-printer cell err as the inspector speed varies? Error is 0 for fast and slow inspectors but peaks at −2% to −5% just below the knee where inspector and subsystem rates match, ten times the earlier 0.3% mean. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-fesc`](robofab-cell-fesc) — can a flow-equivalent-server decomposition predict a robot-printer cell without enumerating its states? Mean error 0.3% (max 1.6%) against the exact Markov chain in 42 settings, where plain MVA is off by up to 20%. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-ctmc`](robofab-cell-ctmc) — is the error of queueing analysis of a robot-printer cell from print-time variability or from structure? An exact Markov chain says structure: cv 1 to 0.33 moves throughput by at most 0.71%, while plain MVA overshoots up to 20% beyond the printer count.
- [`research/robofab-cell-held`](robofab-cell-held) — does adding load and unload time to the printer's demand fix MVA of the robot-printer cell? Mixed: it cuts the error at saturation (2.9% against 8.2%) but raises it elsewhere (7.3% against 3.6%), so overall plain MVA is better (4.7% against 6.2%). Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-stall`](robofab-cell-stall) — does adding the arm's queueing delay (a fixed point, no fitted parameter) to the held printer's demand fix MVA of the robot-printer cell? No: it underpredicts in all 32 settings and is worse overall (7.9% mean error against 6.2% for held-demand and 4.7% for plain MVA). Negative result. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-cell-blocking`](robofab-cell-blocking) — can a blocking-aware correction to MVA predict the held-printer robot cell across loads? A fitted stall helps only at saturation (1.4% error) and worsens other loads (8.5%); keeping latency apart from capacity gives 3.6% against plain MVA's 4.7% on the design grid, but only ties plain MVA (3.4% against 3.5%) on a held-out job mix. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-selfparts`](robofab-selfparts) — can a parallel-jaw arm assemble its own toy gripper? A complete order exists only once the jaw opens to the widest part (30 mm); a top-down-only arm accepts 120 of 720 orderings against 384 with sideways moves; pad thickness changes nothing (negative). Stylised boxes.
- [`research/robofab-bootstrap`](robofab-bootstrap) — can a printed gripper assemble its own next generation? With fixed-size jaw pads a gripper builds a copy only at scale &ge; pad thickness in mm (4 mm pads: scale 4); a stroke cap of S mm needs 30&middot;pad &le; S; seeds of 130 and 140 mm sustain a chain, 60 and 100 mm decay. Largely algebraic given an assumed opening; stylised.
- [`research/robofab-reach`](robofab-reach) — how long an arm does it take to assemble its own toy gripper? A planar two-link arm needs 60–140 mm links as the tray moves 40–400 mm away, and a 90° elbow limit more than doubles it (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-reachorder`](robofab-reachorder) — does the assembly order change the arm needed to build its own gripper? Across all 384 valid orders the shortest arm is identical in every setting tried (negative result, stylised).
- [`research/robofab-grasp`](robofab-grasp) — can friction hold each printed part of the toy gripper? The simulated minimum jaw force matches the m·g/(2μ) bound to within 5% over 33 cases and is at most 0.1 N, so the jaw opening, not friction, is the constraint (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-armaccel`](robofab-armaccel) — does arm torque or jaw friction limit how fast an arm carries a printed part? With 0.12 m links and 5 N m the carries take 0.06–0.10 s and the cover needs 1.25 N per jaw (21× static); grip binds below about 0.5 N (stylised, bound extrapolated beyond 40 m/s²).
- [`research/robofab-armbang`](robofab-armbang) — how much carry time does a time-optimal move save over min-jerk, and what does it cost the jaws? On the `robofab-armaccel` arm the six-part total falls by 21 to 29% while the jaw-force bound rises 1.2 to 2.4 times (stylised, assumed parameters).
- [`research/robofab-jawcap`](robofab-jawcap) — how fast can an arm carry a printed part when the jaw force is capped as well as the joint torque? On the `robofab-armbang` arm the time-optimal move still beats min-jerk by 17 to 43% under caps of 1.25 to 10 times the static hold force, and a tight cap makes the torque limit irrelevant (stylised, assumed parameters).
- [`research/robofab-jointspeed`](robofab-jointspeed) — does a joint-speed limit erase the time-optimal carry's advantage over min-jerk? On the `robofab-jawcap` arm it grows it: 28 to 46% shorter under speed limits of 6 to 1 rad/s, tending to the 47% set by the min-jerk profile shape (stylised, assumed parameters).
- [`research/robofab-trapezoid`](robofab-trapezoid) — is the time-optimal carry's speed-limited advantage just the min-jerk shape? Against a tuned trapezoidal-velocity move it is only 0.4 to 2.9% at speed limits of 2 and 1 rad/s (46% over min-jerk), and 8.7 to 22.5% with no speed limit (stylised, assumed parameters).
- [`research/robofab-scurve`](robofab-scurve) — does rounding the trapezoid's corners (a jerk-limited S-curve) change the time-optimal carry's edge? An S-curve is never faster than the trapezoid and costs 0.0 to 2.0% (half-jerk) or 0.0 to 7.0% (full-jerk) at speed limits of 2 and 1 rad/s, but 1.4 to 33.7% with no speed limit (stylised, assumed parameters).
- [`research/robofab-scurvefine`](robofab-scurvefine) — checks `robofab-scurve` on a finer ramp grid: sampling a 0.2% ramp is needed or the trapezoid wrongly beats the time-optimal solver; with it the time-optimal edge at 2 and 1 rad/s is −0.3 to +0.9%, below the solver's own ~0.5% node-count error (stylised, negative on the old figure).
- [`research/robofab-edgeconv`](robofab-edgeconv) — re-solves the time-optimal carry at 400, 1600 and 6400 nodes in all 12 speed-limited rows of `robofab-scurvefine`: once converged the edge over a tuned trapezoid is positive everywhere, +0.1 to +0.9% (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-rampopt`](robofab-rampopt) — golden-section tuning of the trapezoid's ramp fraction shrinks the time-optimal carry's edge to +0.05 to +0.28% (still positive in all 12 rows; stylised).
- [`research/robofab-toppconv`](robofab-toppconv) — re-solves the time-optimal carry of `robofab-scurvefine` at 400 to 25600 nodes in all 12 rows: converged to 0.02% by 6400 nodes, the 400-node answer was up to 0.5% pessimistic, and the converged edge over a ramp-resolved trapezoid is 0.11 to 0.88% in every row; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/robofab-motorcurve`](robofab-motorcurve) — re-solves the nine torque- and jaw-bound `robofab-torquebound` rows with a motor torque-speed line (no-load speed 4 to 20 rad/s): the time-optimal carry stays faster than the tuned trapezoid in all 45 cells, but its edge ranges 3.5 to 28.6% (9.0 to 22.9% under a constant limit), shrinking where a jaw cap binds and growing where only torque does. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-motorconv`](robofab-motorconv) — repeats the `robofab-motorcurve` motor-line rows with the time-optimal solver at 400, 1600 and 6400 nodes: the edge over the tuned trapezoid moves by at most 0.28 points from 1600 to 6400 (the 0.08 m, k = 2, w0 = 4 row rises from 4.98 to 5.26%), and the missing `cover` profile was a node-count limit. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-peakcheck`](robofab-peakcheck) — re-checks the nine torque- and jaw-bound `robofab-torquebound` rows with a dense trapezoid peak check (10,000 whole-move samples, 1,000 per ramp, both sides of the acceleration corners): trapezoid totals move by under 0.16% and the time-optimal edge stays 9.5 to 23.0%, so the large edge does not rest on unseen trapezoid peaks. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-mujocotorque`](robofab-mujocotorque) — re-checks the 108 trapezoid and time-optimal carry profiles of the `robofab-torquebound` rows against MuJoCo inverse dynamics: the hand-written two-link torques agree to 1.8e-5 N m, peaks are within 0.73% of the 5 N m limit (stylised, joint torques only).
- [`research/robofab-torquebound`](robofab-torquebound) — re-checks the torque- and jaw-bound rows of `robofab-scurve` (no speed limit) with a converged solver and denser trapezoid ramp sampling: the time-optimal carry is still 9.4 to 23.0% faster than the tuned trapezoid in all 9 rows, and the edge is stable to solver node count (under 0.1%) and rises by at most 0.4 points with denser sampling. Stylised.
- [`research/robofab-graspmove`](robofab-graspmove) — how hard must the jaws squeeze while the arm moves? Simulated minimum force matches the m·sqrt((g+a)²)/(2μ) bound within 7% over 77 cases and is at most 0.3 N at 40 m/s²; a contact time constant that passes the static test reports slip at any force once the jaws accelerate (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-graspfast`](robofab-graspfast) — does the jaw-force bound still hold at arm accelerations of 100 to 800 m/s²? With fine MuJoCo contact settings the minimum force is 0.89–1.02× the bound over 39 cases; the earlier settings fail above 200 m/s² (stylised, prescribed acceleration, no arm).
- [`research/robofab-solrefmap`](robofab-solrefmap) — how fine must MuJoCo contact settings be for a moving grasp? On all seven toy-gripper parts, `solref` 0.2 ms and below reproduce the jaw-force bound (0.98–1.04×) at 100–800 m/s²; 0.5 ms fails at 400 and above, and the adequate value falls with acceleration (stylised, upward load only).
- [`research/robofab-solreftimestep`](robofab-solreftimestep) — is it `solref` or the timestep that breaks a moving MuJoCo grasp? On a 24-cell grid only 6 cells hold; a coarse step always fails, a finer step does not rescue a marginal `solref`, and `solref` 0.1 ms holds at steps of 0.1 ms and below (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-solrefsafe`](robofab-solrefsafe) — does MuJoCo's `refsafe` clamp break a moving grasp at a coarse timestep? Switching it off changes 7 of 32 grid cells' outcomes (11 hold on, 10 off), 10 runs go unstable, and the non-monotone timestep behaviour survives (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-graspoffset`](robofab-graspoffset) — does an off-centre centre of mass need a harder grasp? Ball-pad elliptic-cone bound matches MuJoCo within 1.1% (5× force at 20 mm offset); flat-pad MuJoCo lies between an equal-split and a best-split corner-contact bound (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-liftload`](robofab-liftload) — does the friction-grasp bound survive lift acceleration and an off-centre centre of mass? Scaled bound matches MuJoCo within 6% over 66 cases; the torque bracket for an off-centre bar is inconclusive and depends on the rotation tolerance (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-sliprate`](robofab-sliprate) — is the off-centre bar result in `robofab-liftload` a grasp effect or solver creep? The bar creeps at about 3.7 deg/s from 0.3 to 3 N in MuJoCo, with a rate set by contact settings, so the earlier verdict reflects the simulator (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-slipcrit`](robofab-slipcrit) — does a rate-based "holds" criterion make a MuJoCo grasp verdict independent of solver settings? No: the minimum holding force still varies 33x to 400x or more across contact settings, like a 1 degree tolerance (negative, stylised).
- [`research/robofab-slipthresh`](robofab-slipthresh) — is any rate threshold settings-robust for a MuJoCo "holds" verdict? Spread falls with the allowed tip speed and is 1.6 only at a loose 20 mm/s (0.5 s, 10 mm); 242.7 to 400 at 2 mm/s or less (stylised, preliminary).
- [`research/robofab-slipmu`](robofab-slipmu) — does the loose 20 mm/s "holds" threshold stay settings-robust at other friction and under lift? It is the only threshold under 5x spread in all six conditions (1.6 to 4.5), every other is at least 20x (stylised, preliminary).
- [`research/robofab-barimpratio`](robofab-barimpratio) — does a stiffer friction cone make the held-bar result threshold-free? Creep falls about as 1/`impratio` (3.66 to 0.004 deg/s); from `impratio` 10 the 1 and 5 degree forces agree within 26%, and MuJoCo needs 0.67 to 1.17 times the old torque bracket's lower bound (stylised).
- [`research/robofab-tolstack`](robofab-tolstack) — how much clearance does a printed two-pin cover need to assemble, and how wasteful is worst-case stacking? At position sd 0.10 mm and size sd 0.05 mm, 99% yield needs 0.62 mm diametral clearance and a ±3σ worst-case stack asks for 2.0 mm (stylised, assumed process spreads).
- [`research/ambient-pipeline`](ambient-pipeline) — CPU voice pipeline (Silero VAD, Whisper tiny.en, Qwen2.5-0.5B, Piper) on synthetic speech: per-stage latency/RTF/memory and a reply-latency budget of 0.9–1.5 s at 200–800 ms endpoint hangover, ~0.7 s of it compute. Server CPU, not a phone.
- [`research/ambient-cluster`](ambient-cluster) — when moments of need cluster in time, does a cooldown, boost or back-off help an always-on assistant at equal annoyance: an episode-aware boost gains up to 0.05 recall under strong clustering, a cooldown always loses recall, back-off is a wash; stylised simulation.
- [`research/ambient-interject`](ambient-interject) — when an always-on assistant should speak: wake, keyword, threshold and learned policies at equal annoyance, and what breaks when the simulator's room differs from the real one
- [`research/ambient-asrnoise`](ambient-asrnoise) — how much speech-recognition error hurts an always-on assistant's trigger policies at equal annoyance: from word error rate 0 to 0.5 a two-word wake phrase loses 74% of its recall and a transcript-reading threshold 76%, an acoustic-only score about 54%, and the transcript policy falls behind the acoustic one at WER 0.3 to 0.4 if its separation shrinks as (1-WER)^2 (stylised).
- [`research/ambient-memory`](ambient-memory) — what an on-device assistant should keep from a week of simulated transcripts: BM25 retrieval, extractive summaries and byte-limited retention policies (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-android`](ambient-android) — testable Kotlin core of an always-on listening service (re-framing, energy VAD, utterance segmenter, headset-drop policy; 7 tests pass under a shim) plus an unbuilt Android foreground-service sketch; no SDK, no device numbers
- [`research/ambient-glance`](ambient-glance) — when a glanceable display beats speech for an always-on assistant: a stylised utility model over noise, company, free eyes and message length, with sensitivity and twin-gap checks
- [`research/ambient-urgency`](ambient-urgency) — urgency classes and a chime-plus-glance mode added to the glance-vs-speech utility model for an always-on assistant: a stylised model with sensitivity and twin-gap checks
- [`research/ambient-speculate`](ambient-speculate) — how much reply latency starting the ASR/LLM/TTS chain during the end-of-speech hangover hides, from the measured `ambient-pipeline` stage times and an assumed within-turn pause model (hold-until-endpoint vs eager; extra runs per turn)
- [`research/ambient-semend`](ambient-semend) — can a sentence-completeness cue let an always-on assistant stop waiting so long before it replies: a stylised two-hangover endpointer cuts the mean wait from 913 to 574 ms at a 10% cut-off budget if no pause sits at a complete point, but only to 837 ms if half do; cue and pauses assumed.
- [`research/ambient-rechime`](ambient-rechime) — how many repeated earbud tones before a glance are worth their annoyance, by urgency, tone gap and masking, in the `ambient-urgency` utility model (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-misread`](ambient-misread) — how accurate an urgency label must be before an always-on assistant should act on it: naive, Bayesian and label-ignoring policies in the `ambient-urgency` utility model (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-confest`](ambient-confest) — how many audited messages an assistant needs to estimate a noisy urgency label's confusion matrix and prior well enough to act on it: about 50 at moderate accuracy, with smoothing helping at tiny audit sets (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-noisyaudit`](ambient-noisyaudit) — what a noisy human auditor does to an assistant that learns its urgency label's reliability from audits: taking audit labels as truth biases the estimate and loses 8% to 69% of the label's gain at a 0.9 to 0.7 auditor, and correcting for a known auditor accuracy recovers it (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-goldaudit`](ambient-goldaudit) — how many gold-labelled audits replace noisy ones when the auditor's accuracy is unknown: 25 gold audits match 1000 noisy ones read as truth, and a joint EM using 10 gold plus 200 noisy audits nearly reaches the true-parameter policy (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-auditbudget`](ambient-auditbudget) — how to split a fixed audit budget between gold and noisy audits when gold costs 3 to 30 times more: all-noisy is worst or near-worst in all 12 cells, 25% on gold with a joint EM fit is within 0.0009 of the best, and all-gold loses when gold is expensive (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-dsaudit`](ambient-dsaudit) — can the assistant learn its urgency label's reliability from two noisy auditors with no accuracy known (Dawid-Skene), and what does a wrong assumed auditor accuracy cost: over-trusting the auditor loses 31% of a 0.9 label's gain, and Dawid-Skene reaches within 0.0005 of the oracle at n = 1000 (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-dscorr`](ambient-dscorr) — what does Dawid-Skene do when the two auditors share their errors: it over-trusts them (auditor accuracy read as 0.909 for a true 0.8 at half shared) and loses part of its edge over trusting one auditor, falling to equal it when the second auditor is a copy, never below it (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-dshard`](ambient-dshard) — what if the auditors only share errors on hard messages: Dawid-Skene's cost is much smaller than with copying (policy utility 0.4060 vs 0.4066 at 30% hard messages), reaching the naive estimator only in an extreme cell, and a hardness-aware EM recovers the classifier but cannot identify the hard share (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-third`](ambient-third) — does a third auditor identify the hard-message share: yes at the population level (P(hard) 0.300 from informed starts), only loosely at n=1000 (sd 0.07), and in the extreme cell plain Dawid-Skene with three auditors falls below the naive estimator (0.3912 vs 0.3992) while the hardness-aware EM does not (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-multistart`](ambient-multistart) — does a likelihood-ranked multi-start make hardness-aware Dawid-Skene robust to a poor start: at the population level yes (P(hard) 0.300, vs 0.500 and a lower likelihood from a poor single start), at finite n it matches one informed start, and where hardness is absent the hard share is not identified (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-startcount`](ambient-startcount) — how many starts does a likelihood-ranked multi-start need, and are grid starts better than random ones: one start is nearly enough with strong hardness or n = 1000, 4 starts reach the best optimum in most cells, more help only in the weak-hardness and null cells, and policy utility barely moves (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-hlrt`](ambient-hlrt) — can a likelihood-ratio test tell no hard messages from some: chi-square cutoffs reject 35-72% of null data sets, a parametric bootstrap holds its level and detects h = 0.3 at n = 1000 but not h = 0.1 (stylised).
- [`research/ambient-hlrtlevel`](ambient-hlrtlevel) — does the bootstrap likelihood-ratio test of no hard messages hold its level: 200 null draws per n give rejection 0.040-0.055 at nominal 0.05, all intervals containing 0.05 (stylised).
- [`research/ambient-hlrtmis`](ambient-hlrtmis) — does the no-hard-messages bootstrap test keep its level when the three auditors differ in accuracy: 100 h = 0 draws per cell give rejection 0.00-0.10 at nominal 0.05, no inflation seen (stylised).
- [`research/ambient-bandit`](ambient-bandit) — can an always-on assistant learn when to speak from accept/dismiss feedback alone: greedy, epsilon-greedy and Thompson online policies vs an offline oracle, mostly negative (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-ips`](ambient-ips) — does inverse-propensity weighting fix an assistant learning from its own accept/dismiss feedback: mostly negative, exploration cost dominates (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-fullinfo`](ambient-fullinfo) — does selection bias hurt an assistant learning only from windows where it spoke: yes, a lot in this world; a full-information fit reaches the oracle, weighting recovers part (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-labelmatch`](ambient-labelmatch) — is the self-selected-feedback gap bias or label volume: mostly bias (a random sample of equal size nearly reaches the oracle); clipped weights help only with an informed prior (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-dr`](ambient-dr) — does a doubly robust estimator beat IPS on an assistant's self-selected feedback: no, a stylised negative result; its pseudo-labels carry 1/ε residuals and are worse than the biased fit except with an informed prior.
- [`research/ambient-drclip`](ambient-drclip) — does capping the inverse-propensity correction rescue doubly robust learning from an assistant's own feedback: with an informed prior yes (the collapse at ε = 0.002 goes from 0.26 to about 12 utility/h), with an uninformed prior no; stylised, simulated only.
- [`research/ambient-crossfit`](ambient-crossfit) — does fitting the doubly robust outcome model out-of-sample (2-fold cross-fitting) help the capped correction: no, a negative result; differences are within seed noise or worse, and the unclipped collapse at ε = 0.002 persists (1.53 vs 0.26 utility/h); stylised, simulated only.
- [`research/ambient-capcv`](ambient-capcv) — can the capped doubly robust cap be chosen by 4-fold IPS validation on the log: with an informed prior it matches the best fixed cap within seed noise, with an uninformed prior it does not rescue learning; stylised, simulated only.
- [`research/ambient-drvalid`](ambient-drvalid) — a doubly robust validation score does not pick the DR cap better than IPS with the same 4 folds (equal with an informed prior, mixed with the uninformed one); 8 random folds with the DR score is best in all three uninformed cells but still far below the label-matched control (stylised).
- [`research/ambient-drablate`](ambient-drablate) — ablating the three changes in `ambient-drvalid` (score, fold count, fold type): in the uninformed cells random folds help more than the DR score (IPS on 8 random folds is as good as DR on 8), no factor wins everywhere, and every selector stays far below the label-matched control (stylised, negative).
- [`research/ambient-drrepeat`](ambient-drrepeat) — repeated random fold assignments for cap selection in the ambient-assistant world (follow-up to `ambient-drablate`): averaging 5 assignments helps the IPS-score selector at ε = 0.02 (3.5 → 5.3 utility/h) and nothing else beyond noise; stylised, simulated only.
- [`research/ambient-exploreonly`](ambient-exploreonly) — fitting only on the windows an assistant explored at random fixes the uninformed-prior failure of learning from its own feedback (11.99 vs −13.08 utility/h at ε = 0.02), at an exploration cost (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-gatesimd`](ambient-gatesimd) — do a dual-MAC energy loop and a divide-free threshold make the Cortex-M4 voice gate cheaper: 17-37% fewer instructions with the block-minimum floor, slower without it, RAM doubled; decisions unchanged (emulator counts, synthetic audio)
- [`research/ambient-gatem4`](ambient-gatem4) — what does the adaptive voice gate cost on a Cortex-M4? `ambient-gatefw`'s C gate built with clang for Thumb-2 and run in an emulator: bit-exact, about 250 bytes of code, 3237 instructions per frame exact and 2096 with the block-10 floor, because the energy sum dominates. Instructions, not cycles; stylised.
- [`research/ambient-gatefw`](ambient-gatefw) — can a noise-adaptive voice gate fit in about 120 bytes of RAM on a pendant microcontroller? C port of `ambient-gateroc`'s adaptive energy gate, bit-exact with the Python reference; a block-minimum noise floor cuts state from 1020 to 120 bytes and comparisons per frame from 249 to 24, moving modelled battery life by at most 0.07 h. Synthetic audio, host build only.
- [`research/ambient-falsewake`](ambient-falsewake) — how many false wakes a day does an always-on trigger get when its frame scores are correlated? Exact grid-chain rates for "m frames in a row" on AR(1) scores, checked by Monte Carlo: a threshold set by the independent-frames rule gives 613 / 4,793 / 20,993 wakes a day (not 1) for m = 2 / 3 / 5 at phi = 0.9, while a single-frame threshold is nearly unaffected. Stylised.
- [`research/ambient-wakeburst`](ambient-wakeburst) — how bursty are an always-on trigger's false-wake counts? On AR(1) frame scores the Fano factor is near 1 (1.05–1.5), but a 5% window-to-window drift in noise scale lifts it to about 9 for m = 3 (38 at 10%); a 10 s lockout cuts it to 2.5 but also cuts the mean by a third. Stylised.
- [`research/ambient-driftwindow`](ambient-driftwindow) — how does the Fano factor of false-wake counts grow with the counting window when the noise level drifts for hours? At 5% persistent drift (about 3 h correlation time) it grows from 1.9 at 100 s to about 150 at 28 h, against a flat 1.9 for independent drift. Stylised; the drift is assumed.
- [`research/ratelimit-burst`](ratelimit-burst) — how far over its limit can a rate limiter be pushed in a true rolling window? Adversarial search over fixed window, sliding log, sliding-window counter, token bucket and leaky bucket: only the sliding log holds the limit; the sliding counter admits up to about 1.5 L and the buckets nearly 2 L, and under Poisson overload at 1.5x load 39-47% of rolling windows exceed the limit for fixed window and buckets. Stylised.
- [`research/ambient-preroll`](ambient-preroll) — how much pre-roll buffer does an energy-gated audio pendant need to stop clipping the first word? Synthetic audio: no gain in quiet rooms, in the loudest room onset recall 0.866 → 0.980 → 0.999 at 0, 5 and 25 buffered frames (0, 3.1, 15.6 KB) for 90 → 81 → 70 modelled hours; a too-deaf gate is not rescued (stylised)
- [`research/ambient-gateroc`](ambient-gateroc) — does a fixed energy-gate threshold survive different rooms on an always-on audio pendant? Synthetic audio, 20 seeds x 4 background levels: any fixed threshold that keeps speech recall >= 0.9 leaves the gate open all the time in the loudest room (57.6 h, no gating gain), while one adaptive threshold (4x a 5 s running-minimum floor) gives 82-90 h at recall 0.89-1.00 in all four; stylised, nothing measured on hardware
- [`research/ambient-wearable`](ambient-wearable) — what limits battery life in a pendant-style always-on audio wearable: stylised nRF52840-class power model (mic floor ~170 h; speech-gated ~105 h vs 59 h always streaming) plus a 74-byte VAD gate that compiles for Cortex-M4; no hardware measured
- [`research/selfrep-closure`](selfrep-closure) — part, mass and cost closure of the RepRap Prusa Mendel (iteration 2) for three seed shops: printer alone makes 18 of 51 part lines (35%), plus router and laser 43%, assumed-mass closure 22%; casting helps only with brass bushings. Mass and cost inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-nextmachine`](selfrep-nextmachine) — which machine a printer-only seed shop should add next under a budget: greedy closure-per-price vs exhaustive search; same choice on the real 3-machine instance, optimal in 72–94% of 500 synthetic instances. Machine prices assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-growth`](selfrep-growth) — discrete-event and fluid growth of a replicating printer shop with imported vitamins: doubling time falls roughly as 1/budget until machine time binds (about 53 h in the simulation), failed builds cost about 1/(1-f), and closure helps most when imports bind. All inputs assumed; Lackner and Wendt comparison not done. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-reinvest`](selfrep-reinvest) — optimal split of a replicating printer shop's time between copies and goods: copy flat out, then switch one e-folding time (about 50 h) before the horizon; constant splits get 9–27% of that value, smooth ramps no better. Cash-limited cases switch about 50 h from the end. All inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-materials`](selfrep-materials) — mass and energy balance of plastic and aluminium recycling for one RepRap copy (stylised, assumed process parameters): vitamins stay about 75% of imported mass, recycling the shop's own scrap cuts imports a median 3%, and only retired-machine plastic changes the plastic import much.
- [`research/selfrep-recyclers`](selfrep-recyclers) — payback of recycling machines with their own assumed part lists (stylised): about 100 printer copies on the shop's own scrap, 12–31 if retired machines' plastic is recycled.
- [`research/selfrep-costpayback`](selfrep-costpayback) — recycler payback priced instead of weighed (stylised, assumed prices): plastic pair 98–111 printer copies on own scrap, furnace 203, 10–26 with retired plastic.
- [`research/selfrep-satmarket`](selfrep-satmarket) — reinvestment of a replicating printer shop when the goods market saturates, checked by dynamic programming: bang-bang is optimal with linear revenue, but with market capacity below about 10^4 machine-hours per hour the optimum ramps the copy share down over 90–300 h and bang-bang loses 4.5–7%. All inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-singulararc`](selfrep-singulararc) — closed-form optimal copy share for a replicating printer shop with a saturating goods market (Pontryagin singular arc: goods output grows linearly, last 1/k hours all goods); earns 99.96–100% of the dynamic-programming policy's value. Necessary conditions only; all inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-wearout`](selfrep-wearout) — fleet a replicating printer shop sustains when machines wear out (stylised, assumed inputs): short-lived machines die out from one seed; recycling gains need a correction for builds cut short.
- [`research/selfrep-repairpay`](selfrep-repairpay) — does paying for spares at service start rescue the long repair queue of a printer shop (stylised, assumed inputs): yes, an unbounded queue then beats never repairing (488 against 458 at one bench) and queue caps stop mattering.
- [`research/selfrep-repairlabour`](selfrep-repairlabour) — repair labour as a limit on a repairing printer shop (stylised, assumed inputs): with few repair benches an unbounded queue of broken machines is dead stock and does worse than never repairing; capping the queue recovers most of the gain.
- [`research/selfrep-repair`](selfrep-repair) — repair versus replacement in a replicating printer shop (stylised, assumed inputs): repair wins iff its cost share is below its success probability, but only if spares are held back from new builds.
- [`research/selfrep-spares`](selfrep-spares) — how much making its own spare parts cuts a printer shop's repair cost (stylised, assumed inputs): the printer alone makes none of the wear parts, so in-house spares barely move the fleet; the breakdown rate matters more.
- [`research/selfrep-sparehours`](selfrep-sparehours) — does charging printed spares their print hours erase the printed-bushing gain (stylised, assumed inputs): the fleet ignores waits up to ~500 h per repair because it is import-limited, and the gain was within noise anyway.
- [`research/selfrep-extinction`](selfrep-extinction) — exact mean extinction time of a wearing-out printer fleet as a birth-death chain (stylised, assumed inputs): explains the die-outs in kind, within about 35% only after a post-hoc fix.
- [`research/selfrep-extinction-kits`](selfrep-extinction-kits) — exact Markov-chain test of why a lone replicating printer dies out (stylised, assumed inputs): about a fifth die before a fleet forms at 500 h mean life; wear-out lives remove it.
- [`research/selfrep-extinction-qsd`](selfrep-extinction-qsd) — quasi-stationary approximation of how long a small replicating-printer fleet lasts (stylised, assumed inputs): within 5% of the exact mean time once a correction for early die-out is applied, poor for the shortest lives.
- [`research/selfrep-reconcile`](selfrep-reconcile) — reconciles the two exact extinction chains of the lone printer shop (stylised, assumed inputs): the fleet-size chain is 1.5 to 7 times longer; adding the kit wait fixes small fleets but over-corrects large ones.
- [`research/selfrep-redesign`](selfrep-redesign) — which assumed part substitutions (printed bushings and nuts, wooden rods) cut the Prusa Mendel's vitamins most under a penalty budget: all seven take closure from 43/22/16% to 60/36/48% (lines/mass/cost); best set depends on the assumed penalties. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-rodbeam`](selfrep-rodbeam) — beam model of the Prusa Mendel's smooth rods: a wooden rod sags 20 times a steel one at 8 mm and needs about 17 mm to match it, and `selfrep-redesign`'s assumed dowel mass was twice the geometric one (stylised).
- [`research/selfrep-rodmodes`](selfrep-rodmodes) — carriage natural frequency on the Prusa Mendel's smooth rods: a vibration target, not a sag limit, sets the rod diameter; hardwood needs about twice steel's diameter (stylised).
- [`research/selfrep-alurod`](selfrep-alurod) — aluminium rods in the printer redesign: matching a steel rod's sag takes 10.4 mm aluminium (fits the assumed 12 mm holders) against 16.9 mm hardwood (does not), at 39.5% vs 37.8% mass closure (stylised).
- [`research/selfrep-alufreq`](selfrep-alufreq) — sizing printer rods to a first-mode frequency target instead of a sag spec: at 0.3 kg aluminium reaches 40 Hz with 10.0 mm (fits the assumed 12 mm holders) where hardwood needs 16.0 mm (stylised).
- [`research/selfrep-alufring`](selfrep-alufring) — rod diameter from an acceleration and ringing budget: at 3 m/s² with abrupt steps steel needs 7.3 mm, aluminium 9.6 mm, hardwood 15.5 mm; a 20 ms force ramp cuts aluminium at 5 m/s² from 10.9 to 8.9 mm (stylised).
- [`research/selfrep-sensitivity`](selfrep-sensitivity) — which assumed input of the replicating-printer growth model moves its doubling time (Sobol indices, stylised, assumed ranges): budget and vitamin cost together carry ~90% of the variance when imports bind; when machine time binds, failure, print rate, budget and price are comparable.
- [`research/selfrep-coverscale`](selfrep-coverscale) — does greedy substitution search still work past seven items (synthetic instances, assumed generator): 96–100% of the exact optimum on average but optimal in only 33–95 of 100 instances; 64 random evaluations fall to about 89% at 24 items.
- [`research/selfrep-alusearch`](selfrep-alusearch) — with an aluminium rod option priced by sag, the penalty-budget search of the printer redesign picks the 12 mm aluminium rod and reaches 40.0% mass closure at budget 2.0 against 33.1% with the wooden rod (41.4% against 35.8% at full budget); the penalty curve is assumed and the metric favours heavy stock; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/selfrep-stockmass`](selfrep-stockmass) — scoring the printer redesign by the mass the shop must still import (vitamins plus raw stock) instead of closure: the best design imports 5.85 kg against 6.72 kg for the baseline, while the closure-maximising 12 mm aluminium pick imports 6.79 kg (stylised; yield and prices assumed).
- [`research/selfrep-learnfirst`](selfrep-learnfirst) — which imported printer part to learn to make first (stylised, assumed inputs): by mass or cost the NEMA17 motor (+22 and +26 points of closure, first in 90% and 76% of draws with masses and costs scaled 0.5x to 2x), by unit count the M8 nut; by lines every vitamin ties.
- [`research/selfrep-capability`](selfrep-capability) — which process capabilities (lathe, winding, heat treatment, ...) a printer-replicating shop should learn when one capability unlocks several imported parts (stylised, assumed efforts): a lathe is the best first buy by mass (+31 points for effort 4), winding plus stamping by cost (+30 for effort 8), and exact capability sets beat part-by-part picking by up to 10 points, but the choice is fragile to the assumed efforts.
- [`research/selfrep-capsequence`](selfrep-capsequence) — in what order a printer-replicating shop should learn its process capabilities (stylised, assumed efforts): the exact best order over all 362,880 beats walking the budget frontier (54 to 73% of the optimum over 24 weeks); a gain-per-week bundle rule comes within 0 to 6%.
- [`research/selfrep-terminal`](selfrep-terminal) — a resale value `s` per machine at the horizon shortens the goods phase from one e-folding time `1/k` to `1/k − s/a`; at `s ≥ a/k` the shop never switches. Grid optimum matches the closed form. All inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/selfrep-terminalmarket`](selfrep-terminalmarket) — a resale value `s` per machine with a saturating goods market (DP): at capacity c = 1000 machine-hours per hour the copy phase runs to the horizon once s is 12.5 (a quarter of a/k), and the policy that ignores s earns 14% of the optimum; the DP loses to bang-bang by under 1% where the fleet is large, so it is a lower bound there; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/selfrep-dpgrid`](selfrep-dpgrid) — grid-convergence check of the terminal-value DP: the policy's value is stable to about 0.15% from 800 to 1,600 grid points while the table value rises from 8–13% (400 points) to 2–3% (1,600) below it; a stylised fluid model, preliminary.
- [`research/selfrep-exactdp`](selfrep-exactdp) — an interpolation-free DP (the table equals its own policy's value to 1e-14): with no resale value and a saturating market a three-phase policy beats the best single switch by 4.7% (c = 1e4) to 7.1% (c = 1e3), while a resale value of 50 makes one switch optimal; a stylised fluid model, preliminary.
- [`research/selfrep-resalecurve`](selfrep-resalecurve) — a resale price for the finished fleet that falls with market depth `m` (stylised): with an unsaturated goods market the switch moves from 574 h (linear) to 549 h (m = 1e3) and a single switch matches the DP; with a saturating goods market the DP beats the best switch by up to 14%.
- [`research/selfrep-judgement`](selfrep-judgement) — how much the RepRap closure numbers depend on which parts a shop is judged able to make (stylised, assumed inputs): buying all optimistic lines cuts mass closure from 22% to 17%; making the motors in-house would add 22 points.
- [`research/selfrep-partpool`](selfrep-partpool) — does sharing part jobs across printers beat one copy per printer (stylised, assumed inputs): a pool serving the oldest copy first doubles in 43.2 h against 51.1 h with no import limit, about half the gap to the fluid 34.7 h; with imports binding all policies agree.
- [`research/selfrep-platepack`](selfrep-platepack) — how many parts per print plate double a replicating printer shop fastest (stylised, assumed inputs): with no changeover one part per plate wins (43.2 h doubling), with 0.25 h per plate four parts (46.4 h), with 0.5 to 1 h eleven parts; pooled fleets stay 7.6 to 15.5% ahead of one copy per printer.
- [`research/simdesign-bracket`](simdesign-bracket) — parametric L-bracket sized by 2D finite elements (scikit-fem) for minimum mass under a stress limit: differential evolution 19.6 g vs random 25.6 g vs grid 31.4 g at equal budget, exported as STL with a printability check. Assumed material inputs. Stylised.
- [`research/simdesign-topopt`](simdesign-topopt) — Python reproduction of Sigmund's 99-line SIMP topology optimisation (cantilever, half-MBB; filter removes 11.5% checkerboards) with the density field exported as a mirrored, extruded STL; stylised 2D compliance only.
- [`research/simdesign-loadrobust`](simdesign-loadrobust) — how much stiffness a topology-optimised cantilever loses when the real load direction differs from the modelled one: 1.57× at 60° and 2.58× at horizontal for the nominal design; a multi-load design at 0, ±60° cuts the worst case to 2.00× for 0.6% nominal cost; stylised 2D, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-heaviside`](simdesign-heaviside) — density filter and Heaviside projection added to the SIMP code: the optimiser's compliance matches the thresholded part's to 0.1% (sensitivity filter: 10% off) but the parts are equally stiff within 0.9%; β continuation needed (β=64 from the start: 344.8 vs 192.1); a minimum-length-scale check found no advantage (negative); stylised 2D.
- [`research/simdesign-robust`](simdesign-robust) — does a topology-optimised beam survive a 0.2 threshold error? The plain Heaviside design disconnects when eroded on all three meshes; the robust eroded/blueprint/dilated design stays at 224–226 (14–16% above nominal) for 0.8–3.4% more nominal compliance, but shows no minimum-length-scale gain (negative; stylised).
- [`research/simdesign-robustradius`](simdesign-robustradius) — does the filter radius or the threshold gap set a robust beam's minimum feature size? A gap of 0.1 already stops the erosion failure (eroded compliance 255–276 against 187–200 nominal; plain design disconnects), but solid feature size is 3 px at both radius 4 and 6 (negative; stylised, one mesh).
- [`research/simdesign-robustmesh`](simdesign-robustmesh) — does the minimum feature follow the filter radius once the mesh is refined? No: at 180×60 it is 5 px at radii of 6, 9 and 12 px; across meshes it grows with the grid (1, 3, 5 px), not the radius (negative; two poor 60×20 robust runs reported; stylised).
- [`research/simdesign-robustmove`](simdesign-robustmove) — does the move limit decide whether a robust topology optimisation survives its β steps? Yes here: at 0.2 five of nine cases failed (blueprint compliance up to 4× the best), at 0.1 and 0.05 none (stylised; mechanism not tested).
- [`research/simdesign-robustadapt`](simdesign-robustadapt) — can a move limit that halves after a compliance spike rescue a robust topology optimisation? Partly: fixed 0.2 failed 5 of 9 cases, fixed 0.05 none, the adaptive rule 2 of 9, because it reacts after the damaging step (stylised, one run per cell).
- [`research/simdesign-retry`](simdesign-retry) — does rolling back a robust topology-optimisation step that spikes compliance and redoing it at half the move beat shrinking the move afterwards? Mostly: fixed 0.2 failed 5 of 9 cases, adaptive 2, retry 1, fixed 0.1/0.05 none, but retry never beat fixed 0.05 (stylised, one run per cell).
- [`research/simdesign-stuck`](simdesign-stuck) — can penalty continuation free SIMP starts stuck in a different local optimum? No: it sends all 5 stuck starts of 20 to one design (c 204.01) worse than the 202.83 three reach by running longer at p = 3. Stylised.
- [`research/simdesign-startsconverge`](simdesign-startsconverge) — is the start-dependence of fixed-penalty SIMP just slow convergence? Over 1200 iterations the median of 20 random starts reaches the uniform-start design (0.0% of elements differ, from 3.9% at 150), but the compliance spread only falls 1.43% → 0.85% (stylised, 2D, one mesh).
- [`research/simdesign-starts`](simdesign-starts) — how much does a SIMP topology optimisation depend on its starting density field? With a fixed penalty, final compliance spreads 1.4–2.8% over 30 random starts and 2–23% of elements change state; with penalty continuation the spread is 0–0.4% (stylised, 2D, 150 iterations).
- [`research/simdesign-contlength`](simdesign-contlength) — how short can SIMP penalty continuation be and still remove the start dependence? A ramp of 25 iterations or fewer leaves much of it (worst start differs in 16–23% of elements, 27–31% for a fixed penalty); 50 iterations cuts compliance spread to 0.01–0.23%, and a 112-iteration ramp is most repeatable but ends at a worse compliance (stylised, 2D, 150 iterations).
- [`research/simdesign-fidelity`](simdesign-fidelity) — cantilever sizing with Euler–Bernoulli, Timoshenko, 2D and 3D finite elements: stress agrees everywhere, deflection differs; cheap models land within ~5% of the 3D depth, unsafe when narrow and stubby (Euler–Bernoulli) and oversized when wide (plane stress). Stylised, assumed material.
- [`research/simdesign-llm`](simdesign-llm) — session-agent design proposals vs CMA-ES, Bayesian optimisation and random search on a bracket sized by finite elements, 40 evaluations each: agent 23.0 g vs 32–39 g baseline medians (best known 22.4 g); the agent had prior knowledge, n = 1, not an LLM benchmark. Stylised.
- [`research/simdesign-dfm`](simdesign-dfm) — manufacturability rules (FDM, CNC routing, laser cutting, sand casting) added to the sized L-bracket: the mass price of each rule set against the same material with no rules (casting +43% from its even-section rule, CNC +24% from plate stock, laser and FDM ~0); designs overshoot the stress limit by 17–26% on a fine mesh (stylised, assumed rule values).
- [`research/simdesign-holes`](simdesign-holes) — tension plate with a required bolt hole sized under net-section, Kt = 3 and finite-element stress models and per-process hole rules (stylised).
