- [`research/simdesign-processcover`](simdesign-processcover) — share of a sampled bracket family each shop process can make under the `simdesign-dfm` rules: geometry alone adds nothing beyond FDM (negative), with a tip-load requirement the processes separate (FDM alone 30%, +casting +laser 41%) and the best next machine depends on budget and part size, where greedy sometimes loses 1–2 points to exhaustive search (stylised, assumed rules).
- [`research/simdesign-coverweight`](simdesign-coverweight) — does weighting parts by mass change which shop process, and which machine, matters? With a tip load FDM covers 0.30 of parts by count and 0.58 by mass, the laser-versus-CNC order flips, and at budgets 600–800 exhaustive search picks the laser cutter by count but the casting setup by mass (one seed). Stylised, assumed rules and prices, simulated only.
- [`research/simdesign-flipseeds`](simdesign-flipseeds) — do the mass-weighting machine-choice flips of `simdesign-coverweight` survive across seeds? Over 20 seeds, `log` flips from the laser cutter (count) to the casting setup (mass) in 20/20 at budgets 600–800, `large` only at 800, `small` never; the 1100–1400 flips are near-ties (≤0.26 points). Stylised, assumed rules and prices, simulated only.
- [`research/simdesign-pricebands`](simdesign-pricebands) — how wide are the budget bands where mass weighting changes the machine choice when prices are uncertain? With ±20% independent price noise the `log` flip band widens from budgets 600–800 to 500–1000 but softens at its edges (about one half), and `large`'s single nominal flip at 800 shows up in only 44% of price draws. Stylised, assumed rules and prices, simulated only.
- [`research/simdesign-meshmargin`](simdesign-meshmargin) — re-runs `simdesign-dfm` with a stress knock-down and a finer search mesh, checking designs at h = 0.7 mm: the price ordering (casting ~1.4, CNC ~1.23, FDM 1.0) survives the mesh, the laser-cutting price does not (1.01–1.22, noisy), and a 1.3 margin leaves designs at 0.88–1.02 of the limit (stylised, assumed rule values).
- [`research/simdesign-patchstress`](simdesign-patchstress) — compares peak and patch-averaged von Mises stress under mesh refinement on the L-bracket: the peak rises 18–41% from h = 2.5 to 0.55 mm while a 3 mm patch average moves 1–6%, but it reads only 0.45–0.69 of the peak so needs its own limit (stylised, assumed values).
- [`research/simdesign-kirsch`](simdesign-kirsch) — checks the finite-element peak stress at a circular hole against Kirsch's exact 3× concentration: the element-centroid peak sits 27% (coarse) to 1.1% (fine) below it and closes only at first order, while hole-edge vertex values are within 1% (stylised).
- [`research/simdesign-patchsearch`](simdesign-patchsearch) — repeats the process-rule bracket search with a 3 mm patch-averaged stress instead of the peak: the ordering of mass prices (casting > CNC > laser ≈ FDM) holds, the laser price spread across seeds shrinks from 0.16 to under 0.005, and designs overshoot their limit by 2–12% on a fine mesh against 19–34% for the peak (stylised, assumed values, uncalibrated limit).
- [`research/simdesign-filletfloor`](simdesign-filletfloor) — tests whether a near-sharp fillet floor explains the laser-cutting price scatter seen in `simdesign-patchsearch`: it does not (peak-stress designs already use 7.6–14.3 mm fillets, so floors of 0.2 and 1 mm give identical designs); the patch-stress price is steady (sd 0.006–0.011) at every floor, and the cause of the peak scatter is not identified (negative, five seeds, stylised).
- [`research/simdesign-gaugebudget`](simdesign-gaugebudget) — follow-up to `simdesign-filletfloor`: the peak-stress price scatter of laser-cutting rules (sd 0.063) sits in the 2 mm gauge sub-search, not the fillet floor; a four-times search budget cuts it to 0.025 but not monotonically, so several nearby optima are plausible but not shown (five seeds, stylised).
- [`research/simdesign-holepatch`](simdesign-holepatch) — patch-averaged stress for a bolt hole in a tension strip: nearly settled under mesh refinement already, matches Kirsch's disc averages, needs its own limit (stylised).
- [`research/simdesign-gaugescatter`](simdesign-gaugescatter) — the peak-stress price scatter of laser-cutting rules is a missed search at the narrow 2 mm gauge (1 seed in 20), not a cost of the rules; stylised, mesh effects untested.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugerestart`](simdesign-gaugerestart) — a second DE restart or a doubled population removes the missed 2 mm sub-search (0 misses in 40 runs against 1 in 20 at baseline) and shifts results to the lighter of two mass groups at about twice the cost; stylised, 2.5 mm search mesh only.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugebasins`](simdesign-gaugebasins) — the two gauge-2 mass groups differ in web width w (about 14.3–14.9 against 15.2–15.6 mm), not fillet radius, and the lighter group matches ragged isolated feasible pockets on the 2.5 mm mesh rather than a second broad basin; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-pocketmesh`](simdesign-pocketmesh) — the isolated feasible pockets of the gauge-2 bracket scan (web width below 15.4 mm) vanish when the mesh is refined from 2.5 to 1.5 mm and the smallest feasible width rises by about 1 mm; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-meshconverge`](simdesign-meshconverge) — on meshes of 1.0 and 0.5 mm no width in the usable 15.0–16.0 mm window of the gauge-2 bracket scan is feasible (one point at 0.75 mm), and the lowest peak stress rises 11–14% from 1.5 to 0.5 mm; negative, stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-widerweb`](simdesign-widerweb) — with the assumed slenderness limit raised from 8 to 12.5, gauge-2 bracket webs of 16–17 mm and wider are feasible at h = 1.5, 1.0 and 0.75 mm; refinement from 1.5 to 1.0 mm moves the lightest feasible width up 0.5 mm (about 1 g, 3%); stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-edgescan`](simdesign-edgescan) — a 0.1 mm scan of the feasible edge of the gauge-2 bracket web (nine mesh/radius cases): peak stress is not monotone in w, with residual jitter of 0.9–2.4 MPa about a quadratic trend, the same size as the 0.1–1.7 MPa gap to the 100 MPa limit just below each edge, so the edge is a band of about 0.5 mm and the earlier refinement shift is not separated from mesh noise; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-bucklebound`](simdesign-bucklebound) — replacing the w/b ≤ 8 ceiling with a closed-form buckling-factor floor makes the 1.5 mm laser-cut gauge feasible (31.0 g, about 13% lighter than the best 2 mm design) and changes nothing at 2 and 3 mm; at 1 mm the minimum-wall rule, not buckling, blocks every design; stylised, unvalidated factor, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-lbuckle`](simdesign-lbuckle) — the whole 1.5 mm L-bracket as one 3D body clears the buckling floor under the design load (factor 4.01 vs closed-form 3.47) but not with the load reversed (2.25, below 3); sharp corner, ideal sheet, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-revwidth`](simdesign-revwidth) — widening the 1.5 mm L-bracket web from 18.4 to about 22.6 mm lifts the reversed-load buckling factor from 2.27 to the floor of 3, at +5.9 g (+19%) plate mass; buckling only, sharp corner, ideal sheet, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-revgauge`](simdesign-revgauge) — at the reversed-load buckling floor of 3, a 2 mm sheet needs a web of about 11.8 mm and weighs 27.5 g against 36.6 g for 1.5 mm at 22.6 mm; 2.5 mm gives 20.5 g; buckling only, one element layer through the thickness, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugelayers`](simdesign-gaugelayers) — more element layers through the thickness (1 to 4) move the L-bracket's reversed-load buckling factor by at most 1.2% and the width needed for the floor of 3 by at most 0.07 mm at 1.5 to 3.0 mm gauges, so the thicker-sheet designs of `simdesign-revgauge` stand; coarse in-plane mesh, sharp corner, ideal sheet, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-fwdcheck`](simdesign-fwdcheck) — under the forward 100 N load the four reversed-load buckling-limited L-brackets all clear buckling (factor 4.7 to 5.6), but only the 1.5 mm design also meets the 100 MPa and 4 mm limits; the 2.5 and 3.0 mm designs fail by 2 to 8 times, and the 2.0 mm design passes only on the coarse mesh (95.7 vs 144.9 MPa finer). Stylised.
- [`research/simdesign-minwall`](simdesign-minwall) — relaxing the 1.2 mm minimum wall of the laser-cut bracket: it does not open 1.0 mm sheet by itself (the buckling floor binds); with floor 1 and wall ≤ 1.0 mm a 24.8 g design appears (unvalidated closed form).
- [`research/simdesign-buckle15`](simdesign-buckle15) — a through-thickness-converged 3D eigenbuckling model agrees with the closed-form buckling factor of the lightest 1.5 mm bracket design to under 1% (3.49 vs 3.47, so it clears the floor of 3); the horizontal-arm formula is about 18% conservative; one design, ideal bar, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-webbuckle`](simdesign-webbuckle) — a closed-form lateral-torsional buckling check of the laser-cut bracket arms (stylised, unvalidated by FE): at 2 mm gauge the governing safety factor is 6.9–9.0 for w = 16–20 mm, so the widths of `simdesign-widerweb` are not buckling-limited, and the factor rises with w, opposite to the w/b ≤ 8 rule; at 1 mm the old rule admits designs below 1.
- [`research/simdesign-webshell`](simdesign-webshell) — a 3D scikit-fem eigenbuckling check of the bracket-arm closed forms (stylised, mesh not converged): within about 1% for the governing vertical arm, conservative by 6–29% for the horizontal arm at the 2 mm gauge.
- [`research/simdesign-volscale`](simdesign-volscale) — how much stiffer a thresholded topology-optimised part is than a uniformly thinned plate across volume fractions (stylised): 26–31% lower compliance at v = 0.3–0.5, shrinking to 14% at 0.8, and disconnected designs below that on coarse grids.
- [`research/simdesign-shellconverge`](simdesign-shellconverge) — mesh refinement of the 3D eigenbuckling check of the bracket arms (stylised): refining length, width and thickness lowers every FE/closed-form ratio by 0.9–1.3 points, so the governing vertical arm agrees with the closed form to within 1% (ratio 0.99–1.00 at the finest mesh).
- [`research/simdesign-shellaspect`](simdesign-shellaspect) — how the 3D eigenbuckling model compares with the slender-beam closed form as a bar gets longer (stylised): the finite-element load is 14.5%, 7.4%, 3.8% and 2.1% above it at l/w = 3, 6, 12, 24.
- [`research/simdesign-shellorder`](simdesign-shellorder) — observed convergence order of the 3D eigenbuckling model on a short bar (stylised): three uniform meshes give order ≈ 2.6 and a Richardson limit FE/closed-form ratio 1.137, so the gap to the closed form is not discretisation error.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugepockets`](simdesign-gaugepockets) — the isolated feasible pockets below w = 15 mm at the 2 mm laser-cut gauge vanish at 2.0 and 1.5 mm element size, and the smallest feasible w moves up (14.4 to 15.6 mm at r = 14 mm); stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugemesh`](simdesign-gaugemesh) — the narrow 2 mm laser-cut bracket region is a mesh effect: all 39 designs feasible at h = 2.5 mm fail the stress limit at h ≤ 1.5 mm (peak 1.09–1.27× higher, still rising at 0.75 mm); stylised, negative for the search mesh.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugepatch`](simdesign-gaugepatch) — a 3 mm patch-averaged stress converges with the mesh (≤2.2% change from h = 1.0 to 0.75 mm) where the centroid peak does not, and all 39 gauge-2 designs stay feasible; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-gaugefine`](simdesign-gaugefine) — under a 3 mm patch-averaged stress rule the least-mass laser-cut bracket is the same on a 2.5 mm and a 1.0 mm mesh (gauge 2: 32.31 vs 32.33 g), while the centroid-peak rule leaves no feasible gauge-2 design on the fine mesh; the patch value hardly sees the fillet radius; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-gradedtip`](simdesign-gradedtip) — does the L-bracket's peak stress at the fillet tangent point converge under graded mesh refinement? Yes for four designs: it flattens at 1.09–1.14× its 1.5 mm value (0.0–1.1% change over the last 4× refinement down to 0.025 mm), so the 2 mm region's mesh dependence is a resolution effect; the 16/15/2 design is marginally over 100 MPa when converged; stylised 2D, no reference solution.
- [`research/simdesign-rank`](simdesign-rank) — do cheap beam and 2D models rank cantilever designs like 3D finite elements? Yes on 3 seeds of 80 designs: Kendall τ 0.987–0.996 and the 3D-best design always ranked first, even though single-design merit is off by up to 14%; stubby sections agree least; stylised, one load case.
- [`research/simdesign-buckleguard`](simdesign-buckleguard) — replacing the w/b ≤ 8 slenderness rule of the laser-cut bracket with a lateral-torsional buckling factor ≥ 3 lets the optimiser use the 1.5 mm gauge at w ≈ 18.7 mm, 31.3 g instead of 35.9 g (about 12% lighter), and the guard does not bind at that optimum; the result depends on the assumed factor (no feasible design for factors ≥ 5 at that gauge); stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/simdesign-loadbind`](simdesign-loadbind) — sweeping tip load (50–400 N) and arm length (60–120 mm) of the laser-cut bracket: the buckling-factor ≥ 3 guard binds only at 120 mm arms (unguarded optimum has factor 1.9; 69 g vs 59 g), while the old w/b ≤ 8 rule costs 14–25% in mass in five of six cases (stylised, two seeds).
- [`research/garment-teardown`](garment-teardown) — bottom-up cost model for a lined coat applied to two reported designer prices: modelled factory cost is under 15% of price, the assumed markup chain does not close the gap, and a $20–50 replica needs very cheap cloth and labour. All inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/garment-pattern-fit`](garment-pattern-fit) — position-based cloth drape scores a flat skirt-tube pattern on two synthetic elliptical bodies by strain and waist ease; the best waist is near the body's waist perimeter and a pattern fitted to one body fits the other clearly worse. Stylised; strains depend on mesh and solver.
- [`research/garment-nesting`](garment-nesting) — marker making as strip packing: on 20 random 8-box markers, shelf heuristics leave 17–20% extra length vs the exact optimum, annealing 0.5%; on two stylised garments, polygon nesting beats box packing at equal budget. Stylised; no seam allowance or multi-ply.
- [`research/garment-markercost`](garment-markercost) — a 1 cm seam allowance cut the stylised shirt's marker efficiency from 0.69 to 0.63 and the skirt's from 0.71 to 0.51; mixing three sizes in one marker shortened it 9%; swapped into the cost model, trench cloth rises from $26 to $33–41 at $7/m². Stylised; sensitivity, not a coat measurement.
- [`research/garment-cutplan`](garment-cutplan) — cutting-order planning for a 3-size stylised shirt: laying several garments per marker saves 9–11% of cloth over one per marker; mixing sizes adds 0.4–3.7% in sample, but re-nesting the chosen markers with fresh seeds leaves +0.09%, so it is within nesting noise. Stylised; invented shapes.
- [`research/garment-nestcost`](garment-nestcost) — nesting a stylised coat's 10 shell and 5 lining pieces with a 1.5 cm seam allowance gives marker efficiencies of 0.68–0.73 (shell) and 0.59–0.68 (lining) at three cloth widths, below the fixed 0.80 in `garment-teardown`; its trench FOB moves +9% to +15% at the teardown's own areas. Stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/garment-sizeset`](garment-sizeset) — how many sizes fit a population? On a stylised synthetic bust/hip population (+-4 cm), an exact size run beats a regular grid by 12 to 25 points at correlation 0.9 but not on fresh people at correlation 0; greedy is within 3.7 points of optimal (stylised, preliminary).
- [`research/garment-sizestretch`](garment-sizestretch) — how many sizes does a stretch fabric save? On a stylised synthetic bust/hip population, 10% usable stretch cuts the sizes needed for 80% fit from 6 to more than 8 down to 2 or 3; an idealised upper bound (preliminary).
- [`research/garment-thermal`](garment-thermal) — Fanger/ISO PMV and a two-node model extended with fabric infrared transmittance, emissivity and solar absorptance: indoors, transmittance 0→0.8 is worth ~2.4 °C of comfort at 28 °C; in sun, absorptance dominates. Stylised; assumed fabric properties, no reference for the optical extension.
- [`research/garment-twonodecheck`](garment-twonodecheck) — cross-checks `garment-thermal`'s two-node model against `pythermalcomfort` over 108 conditions: core temperature within 0.25 °C, skin temperature mean 0.6 °C (up to 2.1 °C) apart; a comparison of implementations, not of people.
- [`research/garment-twonodealign`](garment-twonodealign) — switches nine design choices one at a time between `garment-thermal`'s two-node model and `pythermalcomfort`'s: variable skin mass fraction, convection coefficient and vasodilation gain explain most of the 0.58 °C skin-temperature gap; a comparison of implementations, not of people.
- [`research/garment-twonodeoptics`](garment-twonodeoptics) — adds `garment-thermal`'s fabric optics (infrared transmittance, emissivity, solar absorptance) to the switchable two-node model and re-runs the comparisons under `pythermalcomfort`'s choices: property rankings agree in all four scenarios, sizes within 3% at 28 °C but up to ~2× apart in cool air and in sun; a comparison of implementations, not of people.
- [`research/garment-twonodesteady`](garment-twonodesteady) — the fabric-optics comparison of `garment-twonodeoptics` run to steady state instead of one hour: the cool-room disagreement between design choices mostly vanishes (stylised).
- [`research/garment-twonodegate`](garment-twonodegate) — the closed-to-open bounds of an infrared-gating textile from `garment-thermal` hold under both two-node choice sets and at steady state: opening the gate cools skin by 0.20–0.25 C at 31 C and keeps it 1.4–1.5 C cooler at 16 C; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/garment-twonodeswitch`](garment-twonodeswitch) — a skin-temperature-triggered infrared-gating textile with hysteresis and actuation lag settles to a fixed closed or open state under constant rooms (no chatter, lag only delays the start); gains need changing conditions; stylised, preliminary, mostly null.
- [`research/garment-twonodestep`](garment-twonodestep) — under a single step in room temperature, a thermostat-driven infrared-gating textile beats both fixed states after a step up and tracks an oracle schedule closely (stylised).
- [`research/garment-twonodemet`](garment-twonodemet) — a step in metabolic rate (rest to walking) instead of room temperature for the thermostat infrared-gating textile: at 24 C the gate beats both fixed states under one choice set and ties fixed closed under the other, at 28 C staying open is as good as any policy, and a 10 min lag erases the gain after walking stops; stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/garment-twonodeheld`](garment-twonodeheld) — tuning the thermostat infrared-gating band on training rooms and testing on held-out rooms: a narrow band just below 33.7 C carries over (about 0.01 to 0.06 C better than the untuned band) but chatters, up to 24 switches in 180 min (stylised).
- [`research/garment-twonodelag`](garment-twonodelag) — retuning the thermostat infrared-gating band with an actuation lag (0 to 10 min) and a cost per switch: lag cuts the gain over never switching from 0.23 to 0.15 C (stylised), a switch penalty widens the band and stops the chatter, and under one choice set the tuned band can lose to the untuned one.
- [`research/garment-twonodeprop`](garment-twonodeprop) — a proportional infrared-gating transmittance against the tuned on/off thermostat gate: a negative result, within 0.010 C in every cell because the tuned ramp collapses to nearly on/off, and a softer ramp costs 0.02 to 0.07 C (stylised).
- [`research/garment-twonodepen`](garment-twonodepen) — prices actuation in equal units (one full close-open swing = 1) and asks when a proportional infrared gate beats the on/off gate: it wins in 52 of 80 held-out cells, by tenths of a degree at most, and the gain reverses in the outside rooms at the highest penalty (stylised, preliminary).
- [`research/garment-twonodewide`](garment-twonodewide) — retunes both infrared gates on a wider set of rooms: the proportional gate's outside-room reversal is partly a training-range artefact
- [`research/garment-twonodeperson`](garment-twonodeperson) — tunes both infrared gates on one person and clothing level and scores them on other clothing and body sizes; clothing matters, body size barely does
- [`research/garment-twonodeopt`](garment-twonodeopt) — a grid search for the best open/closed switching schedule under a room-temperature step shows one toggle suffices and the thermostat gate sits within 0.02 °C of it after a step up, but up to 0.11 °C after a step down (stylised).
- [`research/garment-comfortcost`](garment-comfortcost) — joins `garment-thermal` comfort shifts to `garment-teardown` costs: only one fabric treatment helps in each scenario, levers combine sub-additively, and at assumed premiums a degree costs $0.5 to $19 on a replica trench but $3 to $114 on a designer one. Stylised; premiums assumed.
- [`research/garment-wearmix`](garment-wearmix) — weights `garment-comfortcost`'s fabric levers by wearing time and adds a gated infrared lever: a fixed infrared-transparent shell pays only with a warm-room majority (above 75.5% of time against the cool room), white never harms, and the gate is chosen only when it costs about the fixed shell's premium or less. Stylised; premiums and wearing shares assumed.
- [`research/garment-costperwear`](garment-costperwear) — how much longer must a $2,850 coat last than a $56 replica to win on cost per wear? About 46× with no resale and equal care; needs a median life near 3,100 wears to win half the time. Lifetimes, care and resale assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/garment-learning`](garment-learning) — how many home-sewn replica coats until labour cost settles? On a Wright learning curve (assumed first coat 4× an experienced operator, rate 0.85) it takes 20 coats to get within 2× of factory time; the first coat stays under $50 only if your time is valued at ≤$1.45/h. Learning parameters assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/garment-wearpair`](garment-wearpair) — is a second garment better than a dearer single one? A shell-and-layer pair beats the best single garment at the same budget in 54 of 66 wearing mixes at $50 and $100 (mean +0.49 C and +1.02 C) once thin cloth is removed; the apparent $20 gain is an artefact of thin cloth's negative assumed cost. Stylised; premiums and wearing shares assumed.
- [`research/garment-wearwrong`](garment-wearwrong) — how often can a wearer pick the wrong garment before a second garment stops paying? With random wrong picks the pair's edge over one garment holds (+0.57 C at 30% error, $100); if every mistake is the worst option it falls from +0.55 to +0.09 C (stylised).
- [`research/garment-assist`](garment-assist) — one-joint ankle exosuit model: active assist cuts modelled whole-body cost 3–14% (gain 0.1–0.5) but a few kg of garment mass erases it; break-even mass ∝ 1/(mass sensitivity). Stylised; assumed constants, no OpenSim/MyoSuite.
- [`research/garment-assistpower`](garment-assistpower) — adds a motor and battery mass model to the ankle exosuit: at an assumed 8 N m/kg the motor (about 8 kg) makes a gain-0.3 suit raise cost 0.2–1.9%; the sign flips between 8 and 20 N m/kg, battery hours matter little. Stylised; assumed constants.
- [`research/garment-assistloc`](garment-assistloc) — moves the exosuit's motor and battery from shank to waist: the waist layout wins once a shank kg costs more than 1.03–1.39× a waist kg (assumed ratio, swept); at ratio 3 and 8 N m/kg shank +20.7% vs waist +3.3%. Stylised.
- [`research/garment-bowdenbend`](garment-bowdenbend) — replaces the assumed Bowden efficiency with the capstan law `exp(-mu*theta)`: at ratio 3 and mu 0.1 the waist layout beats the shank layout until the cable bends through about 31 rad, so friction loss alone does not decide the layout (hysteresis not modelled). Stylised.
- [`research/garment-protective`](garment-protective) — Cunniff-style scaling for nylon, aramid and UHMWPE: at equal weight UHMWPE gives 1.49× aramid's V50 and needs 0.20–0.67× the areal density depending on an unknown exponent; a 3 mm, 1 kg suit-like panel falls short of a 4 kg/m² aramid reference except under optimistic assumptions. Stylised; recalled formula, assumed properties and prices, no protection claim.
- [`research/garment-smart`](garment-smart) — activity recognition from simulated chest/thigh/wrist IMUs and knee/elbow strain: two sensors reach ~99% and all five 99.9% on easy synthetic classes, but a clean-trained model falls to 45% under 1 rad garment slip (94% when trained with slip). Stylised; invented signals, no real dataset.
- [`research/garment-optics`](garment-optics) — RF-shielding weave model: 54–87 dB sheet shielding but the aperture bound caps it (36 dB at 2.4 GHz for 1 mm pitch, ~15 dB at 28 GHz); yarn breakage tracks 1/(1−2f) and conduction fails near 50% broken. Stylised; plane wave, ideal crossings, RF part only.
- [`research/garment-contactres`](garment-contactres) — contact resistance at yarn crossings in the RF-shielding weave: no effect on an intact fabric, but with 20% of yarns broken a contact equal to one segment raises sheet resistance 22% and ten segments 2.3×. Stylised; contact values are a sweep, not measured.
- [`research/garment-flexdamage`](garment-flexdamage) — correlated damage in the RF-shielding weave: worn contacts next to breaks (rc×10) raise the median sheet resistance at 20% broken from 2.05 to 3.03 segments; a fold band across the current direction at the same mean breakage leaves 57 of 60 draws open, while a fold along it helps. Stylised; wear multiplier is a sweep, not measured.
- [`research/garment-thincolor`](garment-thincolor) — thin-film transfer-matrix structural colour on a nylon-like substrate: 6-pair stacks reach 99% reflectance with 2–3 g/m² of oxide but blue-shift 48 ΔE by 60° incidence, and 10% layer-thickness error moves hue by ΔE≈10. Stylised; flat substrate, no dispersion.
- [`research/garment-stackopt`](garment-stackopt) — optimising 8-layer thin-film stacks for low angle shift at fixed colour: Nelder–Mead restarts cut mean ΔE76 shift from 12.4 to 1.4 (red) and 25.9 to 5.6 (blue) at 3000 evaluations, beating random search and differential evolution, at the cost of chroma and ~1.8–2.5× oxide mass. Stylised; flat substrate, no tolerance.
- [`research/garment-stackrobust`](garment-stackrobust) — thickness-error robustness of the `garment-stackopt` angle-stable stacks: the nominal optimum (J 1.4) degrades to 24.9 at 3% layer error and loses to the quarter-wave stack; a sampled robust objective at equal cost recovers part of it (red, 3%: 11.7 vs 13.2 quarter-wave) but not colour drift. Stylised; independent Gaussian errors, 10 seeds.
- [`research/garment-stackcvar`](garment-stackcvar) — negative follow-up to `garment-stackrobust`: a CVaR (worst-quarter) training objective and a correlated rate-drift error model do not rescue the optimised angle-stable stacks; none beats the quarter-wave stack (red, iid error: mean J 14.1 vs best optimised 16.5). CVaR trims Nelder-Mead's 90th-percentile cost (47.1 to 43.1) at the price of the mean. Stylised; no coating measured.
- [`research/garment-stackpareto`](garment-stackpareto) — shift against chroma and oxide mass for the `garment-stackopt` 8-layer coating: on a grid of chroma floors and mass caps, the best red-target shift is 0.68 at a 4.0 g/m² cap but 2.71 at 3.0 and no feasible run at 2.0 or below (floor C* 40); mass binds before chroma. Stylised; 5 seeds per cell, one optimiser.
- [`research/garment-stacklayers`](garment-stacklayers) — layer-count sweep (2 to 12) of the constrained structural-colour coating: six to ten layers reach best angle shifts under 1 dE at C* 40 and 4 g/m2 where 2 and 4 layers stay near 3.4, but 12 layers did not search well at the same budget (stylised, 8 seeds).
- [`research/osrobot-specialise`](osrobot-specialise) — when does a purpose-built gantry beat a general two-link arm at tray pick-and-place? At 2 mm tolerance the arm wins only between 0.36 and 0.99 m of work area, and the band depends on assumed costs (stylised).
- [`research/osrobot-morph`](osrobot-morph) — does co-designing an arm's link split and controller beat tuning the controller on a fixed body? Co-design gained 1.9% in held-out move time (0.283 against 0.289 s), within three-seed noise; tuning the controller mattered about 37%. Stylised, preliminary, mostly negative.
- [`research/osrobot-lab`](osrobot-lab) — can a small twin of a liquid-handling bench catch protocol errors before they run, and how much pipetting error does a serial dilution tolerate? It flags 366 of 522 injected faults against 100 for a syntax-only baseline, and an assumed error curve gives a final-well CV of 0.187 at 10 µL and 0.035 at 100 µL transfers (stylised, circular fault set).
- [`research/osrobot-labreal`](osrobot-labreal) — does the real Opentrons simulator catch the faults the stdlib liquid-handling twin catches? On the same 522 seeded faults it raises on 270 and the twin flags 366; everything the simulator catches the twin catches too, while tip reuse, a 5 µL dispense and a half-empty source pass the simulator. Mostly policy differences; the fault set was written for the twin (stylised).
- [`research/osrobot-lint`](osrobot-lint) — can a stdlib static linter catch the lab-protocol faults the Opentrons simulator accepts? It flags 362 of the 522 seeded faults to the simulator's 270, adding reused tips, tips left on, sub-minimum volumes and carry-over; stylised, partly circular.
- [`research/osrobot-bodysearch`](osrobot-bodysearch) — does searching a planar arm's joint count, links and mount, with its controller tuned, beat a fixed three-link body at equal cost? On a shelf task with a tool-down constraint, 0.95 held-out success against 0.26; on a long position-only reach, no gain (stylised, kinematic).
- [`research/osrobot-uav`](osrobot-uav) — how heavy must a purpose-built UAV for targeted observation be, and when is a hover-capable airframe worth its weight? At 3 km out and 20 min on station a fixed wing closes at 1.27 kg against 2.14 kg for a quadrotor and 2.08 kg for a VTOL wing, and the quadrotor stops closing at 45 min (stylised, assumed constants, no flight stack run).
- [`research/osrobot-uavwind`](osrobot-uavwind) — does a steady wind change which purpose-built observation UAV is lightest? At 3 km and 20 min on station the fixed wing stays lightest from 0 to 18 m/s (1.27 to 1.50 kg) while the quadrotor goes 2.14 to 7.0 kg; the multirotor's still-air win at 1 min on station ends near 10 m/s. Stylised, steady known wind only.
- [`research/osrobot-uavgust`](osrobot-uavgust) — how much heavier must an observation UAV be if the wind is uncertain? Designing to the 95th-percentile of wind ~ N(8, 3) m/s costs the fixed wing +3.7% and the multirotor +11.7% mass, and a bigger battery reserve buys about the same reliability for about the same mass; stylised, one steady wind per mission.
- [`research/osrobot-uavnogo`](osrobot-uavnogo) — if an observation UAV launches only when the forecast wind is under a limit, how much does forecast error bring failures back? A perfect forecast turns every failure into a scrub (fixed wing, wind ~ N(5, 4) m/s: 4.6% scrubbed at the 95th-percentile limit); a 2 m/s error gives 6.8% failures at the median limit but 1.1% at the 95th. Stylised, one steady wind per mission.
- [`research/osrobot-uavretry`](osrobot-uavretry) — if a scrubbed observation-UAV launch can be retried later the same day, how much does persistent wind limit the gain, and do retries bring failures back? Fixed wing, median-wind limit, perfect forecast: eight slots complete 99.7% of days with independent wind but 71.5% with persistence 0.95; with a 2 m/s forecast error eight slots raise failures from 6.8% to 13.6% of days (23.7% at persistence 0.95). Stylised.
- [`research/osrobot-uavvalue`](osrobot-uavvalue) — what is a UAV launch wind limit worth in money, and should the launch threshold sit below the design limit when the forecast is wrong? With an assumed expensive airframe and a 2 m/s forecast error the best threshold sits 1 to 6 m/s below the design limit, worth up to $284 per attempt, with failures at or below 0.1%; with a cheap airframe the best design covers nearly every wind. Stylised, assumed prices.
- [`research/osrobot-field`](osrobot-field) — for a weeding robot with a cheap camera, is it better to treat weeds as it drives or to scout first? Scouting then touring killed the same share (0.399 against 0.401 at 1 m/s) and was slower in all 23 paired rows; lane overlap repays a weak sensor (0.40 to 0.86 killed) at 3.5 times the time. Stylised, constants assumed.
- [`research/osrobot-deadline`](osrobot-deadline) — when a pest window sets a deadline, is a scout-and-treater fleet or inline weeding robots cheaper? One scout cannot finish 16 fields faster than 5.3 to 32.5 h; pipeline/inline cost is 1.06 to 1.12 at 0.72 ha, 1.49 at 0.18 ha, and 0.87 to 0.93 at 1.62 ha where it can meet the deadline.
- [`research/osrobot-pipeline`](osrobot-pipeline) — if one scout drone feeds ground treaters in a pipeline, can a held fleet beat inline weeding robots? One scout keeps only 1.0 to 1.3 treaters busy; overlapping the stages cuts the held-pair penalty to 1.04 at 0.72 ha fields and 0.86 at 1.62 ha, still losing on small fields. Assumed prices; scheduling over `osrobot-pairprice`'s fits.
- [`research/osrobot-pairprice`](osrobot-pairprice) — is a scout drone plus a camera-less ground treater cheaper per hectare than one weeding robot? With assumed prices and each machine charged for its own minutes, break-even is near 0.5 ha and the pair is about 0.7 times the cost per hectare at 5 ha; held for the whole job it never wins; flips with utilisation, labour and battery life. Stylised, nothing built.
- [`research/osrobot-scoutpair`](osrobot-scoutpair) — does a fast scout platform plus a ground treater beat one weeding robot? At matched kill (0.343) a 3 m scout swath finishes in 26.6 min against about 42 min for one inline robot, but a 1 m swath takes 47.3 min; a mud-aware tour changes nothing (stylised, simulation only).
- [`research/osrobot-fieldtour`](osrobot-fieldtour) — does a better tour rescue scout-then-treat weeding? 2-opt cuts the tour from 539 m to 468 m but a field still takes 56.1 min against 51.7 min treating inline; scouting loses at every weed density tried (stylised, simulation only).
- [`research/osrobot-reconfig`](osrobot-reconfig) — when does one shared robot beat a dedicated robot per job in a pick-and-place shop? Sharing wins while the total load fits one machine and changeover is short (4 jobs, trays within 0.4 m: shared cheapest at demand 0.2 per job, dedicated from 0.3); the shared arm often cannot hold tight tolerances. Stylised, assumed costs.
- [`research/osrobot-uptime`](osrobot-uptime) — what does a shared pick-and-place robot cost once it must stay up? Spares priced per job or for every job at once: the shared fleet costs the same fraction of a robot per job when its load fits one machine (0.68 at 4 jobs), and falls to 0.34 to 0.51 when all jobs must run at once. Stylised, assumed availabilities.
- [`research/osrobot-assist`](osrobot-assist) — what sets the force on the lips when a spoon-feeding arm meets a swaying mouth: the sensor, the approach speed or a force cap? Peak force tracks approach speed (1.0 N at 0.05 m/s, 4.0 N at 0.20), a camera cuts how often the lips are touched (0.40 against 0.72 of trials) but not how hard, and latency erodes that gain. Stylised point-mass model, assumed constants.
- [`research/osrobot-dof`](osrobot-dof) — does a spoon-feeding arm need more than two joints? From 2 to 6 joints the tip mass moves only 0.63 to 0.50 kg and lip force not clearly at all, while servo cost triples (stylised; extends `osrobot-assist`).
- [`research/osrobot-mjfeed`](osrobot-mjfeed) — does the stylised feeding-arm lip-force result survive MuJoCo contact physics? The steady push matches `KP × LEAD × speed`, but the impact peak is 1.84 × higher and the share fed at most 2 N falls from 1.00 to 0.45 at 0.10 m/s (stylised; extends `osrobot-assist`).
- [`research/osrobot-lipsweep`](osrobot-lipsweep) — is the peak force of a feeding arm on the lips set by lip stiffness, the force loop or the approach lead? Lip stiffness moves the mean peak from 0.32 to 2.02 N at 0.20 m/s (stylised; extends `osrobot-assist`).
- [`research/osrobot-leannoise`](osrobot-leannoise) — if the person being fed misjudges the spoon or sees it late, can a blind feeding arm still do without a camera? A 20 mm misjudgement drops the blind arm's safe share from 1.00 to 0.60 (camera arm 0.78); stylised, extends `osrobot-lean`.
- [`research/osrobot-leanshrink`](osrobot-leanshrink) — if the person being fed judges the spoon better as it nears the mouth, how much of a camera's value does a blind arm get back? A misjudgement shrinking to zero at the lips lifts the blind arm's safe share at 40 mm from 0.47 to 0.84 and restores every feed; stylised, extends `osrobot-leannoise`.
- [`research/osrobot-lean`](osrobot-lean) — if the person being fed leans toward the spoon, can a blind feeding arm do without a camera? At 30 mm sway and 0.20 m/s the blind arm's safe share rises from 0.21 to 1.00 as the lean reaches 100 mm/s (stylised; extends `osrobot-assist`).
- [`research/osrobot-plate`](osrobot-plate) — how fast can a purpose-built robot fill a 96-well plate, and does the robot or the number of channels matter more? Eight channels cut the time by 86 to 88 % (9.5 s against 69.7 s for a gantry at 10 µL) while gantry against arm moves it 4 to 14 %, since about 76 % is fixed pipetting actions (stylised; every speed, price and accuracy figure assumed).
- [`research/swe-friction`](swe-friction) — where do agent runs say the documentation hurts? 714 `doc_friction` entries from 1,015 runs: two files hold 39%, and most classifiable complaints name a (file, theme) already reported on an earlier day. Preliminary.
- [`research/free-frictionnull`](free-frictionnull) — do documentation complaints repeat more than chance? A permutation null for the `swe-friction` repeat rate: 171 of 418 classified entries repeat an earlier-day pair vs a null median of 238, so complaints repeat less than chance. Preliminary.
- [`research/free-excitation`](free-excitation) — which test input should you use to calibrate a mass-spring-damper twin? A resonant sine gives the lowest held-out prediction error (3.1e-4 vs 1.1e-3 for a step) yet the worst Fisher condition number (527 vs 56 to 114); fitted spreads sit within 0.83 to 1.09 of the Cramér-Rao bounds. Stylised, preliminary.
- [`research/swe-ledger`](swe-ledger) — what can the run log measure about the agent loop today? Cost fields are present in at most 1% of 189 runs, a third of listed projects are follow-up families, and every recorded CI-failure count is zero. Preliminary first step of track 10.
- [`research/swe-runcost`](swe-runcost) — what does an agent run cost, and which cost the most? 355 priced runs (14 to 18 October): median $0.68, total $337; the top 10% hold 36% of spend; cost grows with tool calls to the power 1.18 (preliminary; API-equivalent estimates).
- [`research/swe-spendoutput`](swe-spendoutput) — does a bigger run buy more output? Across 157 priced run records that report adding tests, cost and tests added rank together pooled (Spearman 0.57) but weakly within research runs (0.19), and the dearest third yields the fewest tests per dollar; preliminary, tests added is a crude self-reported proxy.
- [`research/swe-costpermerge`](swe-costpermerge) — what does a run cost per merged pull request? 366 priced runs joined to `main`'s merge history: 75% name a merged PR, $1.26 per merged run against $0.94 per run, process runs about 2.4 times research's; red team never links its PR (preliminary; API-equivalent estimates).
- [`research/swe-deviations`](swe-deviations) — do agent runs that record a deviation from the brief cost more or end worse? Of 1,564 run records 203 list one; within track they take ~1.6x as long and cost ~1.2x as much, but the cost gap rests mainly on the process track, and they end partial 5 points more often. Observational, preliminary.
- [`research/swe-notests`](swe-notests) — do agent runs that add no tests end worse? 84 of 621 counted run records add none; within track they end partial 4/84 against 16/537 (+2.6 points, p = 0.23, no detectable effect) and cost less, probably because they do less. Preliminary.
- [`research/swe-callcost`](swe-callcost) — how well does a run's tool-call count predict its cost? On 882 priced run records cost is about $0.03 per call, a log-log fit has R² 0.91 and a held-out median error of 9%; the pooled slope 1.22 is partly group mixing (1.10 within Sonnet). Preliminary.
- [`research/swe-cachecost`](swe-cachecost) — where does a run's cost go? On 893 run records with per-token-kind usage, cache reads are a median 45% of the API-equivalent cost and 1-hour cache writes 41%, output only 13%; the starting context (about 60,000 tokens) is re-read on every request, which makes cost grow faster than linearly in tool calls (preliminary).
- [`research/swe-devkinds`](swe-devkinds) — which kind of recorded deviation predicts a partial outcome? Of 213 run records with a deviation, tooling failures end partial 22.6 points more often than runs with none (track-stratified p=0.001); skipped steps (+1.3) and switched tasks (+1.3) do not. Keyword classes, observational, preliminary.
- [`research/swe-fieldfill`](swe-fieldfill) — how complete are the agent run records, field by field? Of 1,362 records, cost, tokens and wall time are present on 47% (0-7% on 13 to 15 October, 61-82% since), `merged` on 4.6%, and 18.5% of `success` runs name no pull request (preliminary; presence, not correctness).
- [`research/swe-modelmix`](swe-modelmix) — does Opus pay for itself on this portfolio's runs? An Opus run costs about 8 to 9 times a Sonnet run in the same track; with 12 priced Opus runs the ledger cannot compare value per dollar, and a 50% difference in projects per run needs about 129 runs per arm (preliminary; cost only).
- [`research/swe-briefab`](swe-briefab) — did research brief v1 change how runs go? Same-days comparison of v0 (342 runs) and v1 (67) on 15 to 18 October: no detectable difference in cost, time or success; the lower v1 error rate (7.5% vs 17.0%) vanishes within unlabelled-task runs (8.3% vs 9.6%); v1 records more doc-friction entries (1.24 vs 0.72 per run, p = 0.0001). Observational.
- [`research/swe-errors`](swe-errors) — what do agent runs record going wrong? 17% of 1,027 runs record an error (24.5% of research runs); environment 28%, own mistakes 21%, collisions 17%; runs with an error end partial twice as often (12.6% vs 6.0%) and cost about twice as much. Preliminary: keyword classifier, self-reported.
- [`research/swe-errlabel`](swe-errlabel) — do the `swe-errors` keyword rules sort run-record errors correctly? On a 60-entry blind hand-labelled sample they agree on 40 (66.7%, Wilson 54.1% to 77.3%; kappa 0.59); the `ci-or-tooling` rule never wins and own-mistake recall is 55%, so the earlier shares are a lower bound for those two. Preliminary: one labeller.
- [`research/swe-errfix`](swe-errfix) — can the `swe-errors` keyword rules be improved on 30 hand labels and still help on the other 30? A two-fold held-out refit (rule order plus greedy keywords) averages 68.5% agreement with the hand labels against 66.7% for the shipped rules (+1.1 entries over 200 splits, range -4 to +7), while the same procedure scores 85% on its own 60 training entries: mostly memorisation. Preliminary.
- [`research/swe-backfill`](swe-backfill) — what does git history say about the agent loop that the run log cannot? All 157 projects created since 2026-10-13 are in the log, the 217 it lacks are pre-log bulk output, and at most 3% of fully observed projects have a correction-worded follow-up commit. Preliminary second step of track 10.
- [`research/swe-audit`](swe-audit) — do committed results reproduce? A seeded sample of 40 projects with a runnable experiment was re-run: 36 (90%, 95% Wilson 77% to 96%) matched their committed `results.txt`, 3 timed out under load and 1 needed a missing voice model. Preliminary first pass of track 10, milestone 2; it checks reproduction only, not citations. Second sample of 60 (92 distinct projects pooled): 81 reproduced (88%), no finished run differed; re-running the 5 timeouts serially reproduced all, giving 86 of 92 (93%).
- [`research/swe-value`](swe-value) — can two passes agree on what a project is worth? A three-level value rubric scored two automated ways over 408 projects agrees at chance (kappa 0.07, 95% 0.01 to 0.13), partly from a name-family flaw fixed after the fact (0.25); a blind 30-project hand sample agrees better (0.45 to 0.57, wide intervals). Preliminary, track 10 milestone 3; no correctness term.
- [`research/swe-prompt`](swe-prompt) — when can a prompt experiment stop? Fixed-n, peek-every-pair and a mixture SPRT simulated on ledger-shaped outcomes: peeking declares an identical brief different in 21% (success rate) to 40% (value) of experiments, the mixture test stays under 5% but needs far more runs than a 97% success rate allows. Stylised, no candidate brief run; track 10 milestone 6, partial.
- [`research/swe-allocate`](swe-allocate) — which track should the next run work on? Four allocation policies (uniform, greedy, bootstrap Thompson, knapsack-UCB) replayed offline on 7 tracks' scored projects: greedy and Thompson reach about 80% of the best-arm oracle against 60% for the current uniform pick, but value per line falls with order in 4 of 7 tracks, so the stationary replay overstates the gain. Stylised, offline only, no live trial; track 10 milestone 4, partial.
- [`research/swe-decay`](swe-decay) — do track-allocation gains survive when tracks run out of projects? Sequential, no-replacement replay: at 20,000 lines greedy and Thompson keep most of their lead over uniform (pass A 134 and 130 against 114), but at 5,000 lines they lose to it (24 against 30). Stylised, offline only; track 10 follow-up to swe-allocate.
- [`research/swe-bootstrap`](swe-bootstrap) — how much of the track-allocation gain is luck of the history? Block bootstrap of the sequential replay: at 20,000 lines greedy and Thompson beat uniform in 98–100% of 200 histories (intervals exclude zero), knapsack-UCB's does not; the small-budget loss does not reproduce. Stylised, offline only; follow-up to swe-decay.
- [`research/swe-warmstart`](swe-warmstart) — does a cheaper start fix the small-budget loss of smart track allocation? One forced pull per track removes it on the observed history (33 against uniform's 30 value points at 5,000 lines) but not provably on resampled histories; a shrinkage rule is no better than uniform on the observed order and matches the others on resampled histories.
- [`research/swe-cleanbroken`](swe-cleanbroken) — when research merges collide cleanly, is the result still right? None of 92 clean replays was broken (28.7% of pairs within 60 min conflict); found and removed two duplicate README bullets the build missed.
- [`research/swe-prlifetime`](swe-prlifetime) — how long do research pull requests stay open, and how often do they really collide? Median 0.9 min open (95% merged within 30 min); replayed from real creation times, 2 of 58 shared-file PRs (3.4%) conflict.
- [`research/swe-branchlife`](swe-branchlife) — does a research branch live longer than its pull request, and which shared file collides most? Median 1.1 min from first commit to merge (vs 0.9 for the PR); `research/PROJECTS.md` conflicts in 5 of 44 PRs that edit it, more than the index files (3 of 58).
- [`research/swe-siblings`](swe-siblings) — do sibling research projects ask different questions? Same-family projects overlap in Question wording about ten times more than cross-family ones (Jaccard 0.078 vs 0.008); preliminary, a crude proxy.
- [`research/swe-sharedfiles`](swe-sharedfiles) — how often do research merges collide on the shared index files? 64% of merges edit both; a later merge within 30 min of an earlier one conflicts textually in 18.6% of 59 replayed pairs (assumed branch times, preliminary).
- [`research/swe-schedule`](swe-schedule) — how many parallel agent runs duplicate each other when they start together? 12.8% of runs at four blind starts; a start gap past the claim latency or scheduler slots remove it (stylised, assumed timings).
- [`research/swe-pickmix`](swe-pickmix) — do concurrent runs pick the same track more than chance, and is the pick uniform? Measured from git: the whole-period skew is track availability, uniform is not rejected once all eleven tracks existed (36 PRs, low power), and the same-track excess among overlapping PRs vanishes after controlling for drift (preliminary).
- [`research/swe-prflow`](swe-prflow) — what do 526 closed pull requests say about how the agent loop lands work? 87.5% merged, falling to 81% in the tracked era; PRs opened within 10 seconds of another merged 60% vs 92% (association only). Preliminary third step of track 10.
- [`research/swe-nullrate`](swe-nullrate) — how often does this portfolio report a negative or mixed result? In an 80-summary hand-labelled sample, 59% of the summaries that test a claim against an alternative (43–73%) report a negative or mixed result (preliminary, one labeller).
- [`research/swe-reuse`](swe-reuse) — do later projects build on earlier ones? A reference graph of 723 READMEs: 61.7% of projects are named by at least one later project (Wilson 58% to 65%), inbound counts are very uneven (Gini 0.69) and 71% of references stay within a track; naming is an upper bound on reuse.
- [`research/swe-reccover`](swe-reccover) — do the agent run records match git? All 522 records naming a pull request in this repository name one that landed, but only 79.8% of sessions landing commits from 13 October have a record (ventures 79.2%, research 92.2%; preliminary, seven days).
- [`research/swe-ventgap`](swe-ventgap) — why do landed ventures sessions leave fewer run records? 79.6% against research 92.2%; scope, size and kind explain nothing, the ventures sub-prompts alone omit the record step, and coverage rose from 53% to 84–89% in a week (preliminary).
- [`research/swe-orgs`](swe-orgs) — which practices of engineering organisations that ship could help an agent loop? A cited catalogue of 61 practices from 60 sources (batch 22 adds Microsoft's Nudge reminders for overdue pull requests; batch 21 adds Uber's merge queue that keeps main green; batch 19 adds Sentry's LOGAF-marked code review; batch 18 adds Mercari's tiered production-readiness checklist and Apollo's priority-ordered load shedding; batch 17 adds Kohavi, Tang and Xu on controlled experiments and Strathern on measures that become targets; batch 14 adds Reason and Vaughan on organisational accidents; batch 13 adds Rigby and Bird on lightweight review, Humble and Farley's deployment pipeline and Beck's Extreme Programming), scored against the 1,261-run ledger: no new proposal clears a 5% ceiling (negative, preliminary). Ongoing, with a research log; track 10, milestone 7.
- [`research/swe-startslot`](swe-startslot) — do agent runs that start alongside other runs, or at certain hours, go worse? Raw, crowded starts look better (error recorded 9.8% vs 21.0%) but that is a day effect; shuffled within days there is no detectable difference over 660 timed records, and hour of day shows nothing (negative, preliminary, low power).
- [`research/swe-reviewdup`](swe-reviewdup) — do parallel review sessions duplicate each other? 6 of 35 review-track PRs (17.1%, Wilson 8.1–32.7%) are the second of a pair merged 0.4 to 2.5 minutes apart, and 5 of 6 pairs edited the same generated files; cost unmeasured (3 of 35 priced). Preliminary.
- [`research/mimic-contexts`](mimic-contexts) — how well do prompt-free detectors tell people from a plainly prompted LLM, and do they carry over to unseen kinds of text? Eight RAID families split by family: n-gram detector AUROC 0.93 and Binoculars-style 0.80 on held-out families, length at chance. Preliminary first step of track 11.
- [`research/mimic-loop`](mimic-loop) — can prompt search alone move a frozen 0.5B imitator past a prompted 0.5B detector? A stylised first run of the imitation game, four proposers at equal budget on two families: the starting detector is below chance, proposers cannot be ranked at this size, and length is a major confound.
- [`research/house-framing`](house-framing) — how much does advanced (OVE) framing save in a house's exterior walls? Stylised 28 × 40 ft house: 42% less wall lumber and 27% fewer pieces than conventional 2x6, whole-wall R 16.2 to 18.6, studs at 39% of capacity under 30 psf snow; the lumber is worth only $450–900 a house, so labour carries the case. Not a code check. First project of track 12.
- [`research/house-spans`](house-spans) — how far can a 2x joist, rafter, header or beam span? Open NDS sizing tool, no table copied: 2x10 SPF floor joists at 16 in span 15 ft 3 in, bending governs most floors and snow rafters, deflection most ceilings; deepest joist at 24 in uses 20–50% less lumber (vibration unchecked). Not validated against published tables. Milestone 2 of track 12.
- [`research/house-vibration`](house-vibration) — does a floor-vibration frequency limit erase the lumber saved by deep, widely spaced joists? Beam-theory first-mode frequency on the `house-spans` checks: limits up to 10 Hz change nothing, 12 Hz shortens a 2x10 at 16 in from 15 ft 3 in to 13 ft 11 in, 15 Hz moves the least-lumber 12 ft joist from 2x10 to 2x12. Joist alone, limit is a policy knob. Stylised. Track 12.
- [`research/house-composite`](house-composite) — how much of a floor-vibration penalty does the subfloor's T-beam action recover? Gamma-method partial-composite stiffness on `house-vibration`: at a 15 Hz limit a 2x10 at 16 in grows from 12 ft 6 in to 13 ft 2 in–14 ft 6 in, depending on two assumed inputs (connection efficiency and panel modulus). Stylised.
- [`research/house-continuous`](house-continuous) — does a joist lapped over a middle support span farther than a simple span? FE beam with pattern loading and NDS checks: two-span peak moment equals a simple span's, so bending-governed floors and snow rafters gain nothing; deflection-governed ceiling joists gain up to 18.5%; 1.5 in bearing on the middle support cuts a 2x10 floor joist to 11 ft 4 in. Not validated against published tables. Follow-up to `house-spans`.
- [`research/house-unequal`](house-unequal) — how far can the beam under a two-span joist sit from the middle of the house? Bending-governed floor joists lose 0.4% of total width at a short/long span ratio of 0.9 and 13% at 0.5 (the short span relieves the long one); deflection-governed ceiling joists lose about 5% per 0.1 of ratio. A 2x10 floor can have its beam 3.5 ft off the middle of a 28 ft house. Stylised; not a design.
- [`research/house-plan`](house-plan) — can a house plan be data, and which plan needs the least lumber per sq ft? Parametric one-storey plan to framing takeoff, bill of materials, room-rule checks and IFC4; best plans use 4.8 to 5.2 bf/sq ft at 640 to 1,024 sq ft with none feasible by stick framing at 1,400; local search hits the exhaustive optimum in 36 of 40 runs where random search hits none. Stylised, not a design.
- [`research/house-cost`](house-cost) — where does a new home's price go, and which levers move it most? Share-anchored model (NAHB 2024): size moves the price most (87.8 to 112.0 for 1,600 to 2,400 sq ft), then storeys, finish level and lot; a 14% framing-lumber cut is worth 0.85% of price (2.4% with labour). Index not dollars, assumed driver splits. Milestone 4 of track 12.
- [`research/house-costsens`](house-costsens) — does the house-cost lever ranking survive its assumed driver splits? Monte Carlo over the splits: floor area stays the largest lever in 96–100% of draws; storeys is the soft claim (swing 9.0–19.9 vs 14.0 baseline); the lumber result does not move. Index not dollars.
- [`research/house-sobol`](house-sobol) — which uncertain input of the house-cost model drives a house's price uncertainty? Sobol indices over every lever range, the framing share and the driver splits at once: floor area 53% of variance, storeys 19%, all framing inputs together under 1%. Index not dollars.
- [`research/house-energyprices`](house-energyprices) — does the cost-optimal home envelope change when invented fuel prices are replaced by EIA state prices? Optimum unchanged in 10 of 12 cases; 30-year saving moves by −$496 to +$549; advanced 2x6 walls still win and R-49 attics and U 0.27 windows still do not pay. Only fuel prices are sourced.
- [`research/house-hvacnpc`](house-hvacnpc) — how much dearer can a heat pump be than a gas furnace plus AC before gas wins on 30-year cost? Break-even premium $84 to $735 in the South; in Chicago to Duluth it would have to cost $5,399 to $6,698 less. No equipment prices used; stylised.
- [`research/house-coldcop`](house-coldcop) — does a temperature-dependent heat-pump COP change how much more a heat pump can cost than gas before gas wins? Seasonal COP 4.01 (Miami) to 2.30 (Duluth) under an assumed curve, so the constant 2.5 understated the South: break-even premium $156, $952, $1,979 in Miami, Houston, Atlanta and −$8,050 in Duluth; signs unchanged, size curve-dependent. Stylised.
- [`research/house-escalate`](house-escalate) — how fast must gas prices rise against electricity before a heat pump ties a gas furnace on 30-year cost? At equal installed cost the heat pump already wins in Miami, Houston and Atlanta unless gas gets cheaper; in Chicago, Minneapolis and Duluth gas must rise 4.6%, 3.7% and 3.7% a year in real terms for 30 years. Stylised.
- [`research/house-energy`](house-energy) — which envelope upgrades pay back over 30 years, by climate? A degree-day net-present-cost search of 640 packages in eight climates: advanced 2x6 framing is in every optimum (13–15% lower cost than a 2x4 baseline); better windows and R-49 attics do not pay at assumed prices. Stylised, assumed prices, not EnergyPlus.
- [`research/house-wallsystems`](house-wallsystems) — does any alternative to a wood-framed wall beat it on cost, carbon or heat loss for a small house? In a stylised model wood 2x6 is cheapest in 97% of random draws and lowest-carbon in 100%; SIP, CLT and printed walls win only on labour. Inputs are ranges from memory.
- [`research/house-market`](house-market) — what does a new home cost to build in the US and Colorado in 2026, and where does the money go? Sourced market report: median new US home $393,700 (Aug 2026, −5.8% y/y) while input costs rose 10.3%; NAHB's breakdown puts construction at 64.4% of price and foundations, framing and exterior at 26%, so a 20% cheaper structure moves the price 5.2%; manufactured homes cost ~52% of site-built per sq ft; Denver fees and plan-review delay are ~12% of a modelled $462k (lot excluded) home against 3.5% nationally.
- [`research/house-adufees`](house-adufees) — what does Denver Water's 2026 tap fee cost a 480 sq ft ADU against a house? A flat $2,170 for the ADU against $5,400 for a house charged on 2,000 sq ft, from the utility's own schedule; Denver's own sewer schedule adds $575 (shared water tap) or up to $7,055 (own tap), so about $2,745 to $9,225 in all.
- [`research/house-structure`](house-structure) — does a small wood-frame house have enough shear wall for wind and earthquakes once the openings are cut out? At 115 mph and SDS 0.50 the 28 x 40 ft house uses 19% of a gable-wall line's capacity at 6 in nailing; the openings, not the load, are what shrink it. Stylised, capacities from memory.
- [`research/house-diaphragm`](house-diaphragm) — does the half-and-half load split understate the demand on a garage-front wall? In a toy stiffness model a rigid roof diaphragm gives the garage line less than half, so the flexible split governs; stylised, torsion not modelled.
- [`research/house-torsion`](house-torsion) — how much does accidental torsion add to the garage-front wall's demand in a rigid-diaphragm wind split? Offsetting the load up to 15% of the length in `house-nailedwall`'s split raises a 22 ft-door wall from 0.36–0.59 to 0.44–0.71 demand over capacity, still under the flexible 1.05. Stylised.
- [`research/house-nailedwall`](house-nailedwall) — does the rigid-diaphragm relief for a garage-front wall survive a nailed-wall stiffness model? Four-term pier deflection (bending, shear, nail slip, hold-down) in `house-diaphragm`'s rigid split: yes; a centred 22 ft door falls from 1.05 (flexible) to 0.36–0.59 demand over capacity. Stylised.
- [`research/house-open-design`](house-open-design) — what does an open plan set for a 480 sq ft backyard cottage look like when generated from code? A 24 x 20 ft ADU composed from the track's models: drawings, bill of materials, IFC, lateral check, envelope energy (a tight envelope cuts modelled heating and cooling cost 36-37%) and a cost index. A worked example, not a buildable design; every input assumed.
- [`research/house-climatesweep`](house-climatesweep) — at what climate does each envelope upgrade start to pay? Climate as a continuous axis over `house-energy`'s model: slab-edge R-10 from 3,271 heating degree-days with gas (2,386 with a heat pump), 3 ACH50 from 5,769, and cooling alone switches on only the attic and air sealing above ~3,000 cooling degree-days. Stylised: unsourced prices, no solar gain.
- [`research/house-solar`](house-solar) — does solar gain through windows change the cheapest envelope, and is window orientation worth money? Adds first-principles solar geometry to `house-energy`: U 0.50 stays optimal in 15 of 16 cases, and orientation is worth $420–$1,740 of 30-year cost. Stylised: degree-day model, assumed prices and clearness, not EnergyPlus.
- [`research/house-overhang`](house-overhang) — does a roof overhang over south windows pay for itself, and where does it cost energy instead? Adds profile-angle shading of south beam radiation to `house-solar`; at an assumed $12 per ft of width per ft of projection no overhang is optimal in 8 climates (gross value at most $280 of 30-year NPC, negative past 1 ft in cold climates). Stylised degree-day model.
- [`research/house-dewpoint`](house-dewpoint) — where in a wall does vapor condense, and does a vapor retarder or exterior foam fix it? Steady-state Glaser check of a 2x6 wall in four assumed climates; winter condensation appears in Chicago and Duluth only, and only polyethylene clears Duluth. Stylised.
- [`research/house-breakeven`](house-breakeven) — how much extra can a tight envelope cost before it stops paying back on a 480 sq ft backyard cottage? The ADU's whole upgrade package can carry $1,409 to $3,104 of extra first cost (3% real rate, heat pump); at assumed prices only the wall switch clearly pays. Stylised.
- [`research/house-hpsize`](house-hpsize) — does a tighter envelope let a 480 sq ft backyard cottage use a smaller heat pump? The tight package cuts the design heating load about 36.5%, worth a $450 to $1,800 equipment step credit (assumed catalogue and prices) that nearly closes the payback gap of the costed upgrades in three colder climates. Stylised.
- [`research/house-coolsize`](house-coolsize) — when does cooling, not heating, set the heat pump size for a 480 sq ft backyard cottage? Adding a summer load (conduction, window solar gain, internal gains) shows cooling governs only in Houston (12 kBtu/h baseline, 9 tight); sizes and credits elsewhere are unchanged, and Houston's credit swings between $0 and $900 with SHGC and latent allowance (stylised, assumed inputs).
- [`research/house-truss`](house-truss) — how much lumber does a prefabricated roof truss save over rafters and ceiling joists? A pin-jointed W truss with NDS checks uses 40-55% less nominal lumber per sq ft of plan than 16 in rafters plus ceiling joists over 12-30 ft spans at 30 psf snow (stylised: no plates, bracing or heel details).
- [`research/house-offsite`](house-offsite) — when does building a house's wall shell in a factory beat building it on site? Panel layout, sheathing nesting (1 sheet in 33 saved) and a Monte Carlo of the shell: panelised takes 2.17 d against 1.96 d on site and costs 38% more at assumed rates; it is faster only from about 58 ft long. Closed panels (insulation, wiring, windows built in) cut the gap: cost parity at a factory labour ratio of about 0.45 and a day saved. Stylised, every rate assumed.
- [`research/house-panellift`](house-panellift) — can a crane or telehandler lift the house's wall panels? Weights of the eight `house-offsite` panels (343–559 lb open, 376–877 lb closed), best pick points and top-plate bending (4–12% of allowable with two picks): bending never limits the layout, weight and wind do. Hand-lifting needs 7–18 people; a 20 ft open panel swings 10° at about 12 mph. Stylised, every density and coefficient assumed.
- [`research/house-liftnails`](house-liftnails) — do the nails carry a hung wall panel? Sheathing-to-top-plate nails take 17–31 lb each (0.20–0.36 of capacity) if they share the load; the closed panel fails when under about 40% of the row acts, and a bare frame's stud end nails get no credit.
- [`research/house-foundation`](house-foundation) — which foundation is cheapest for a small house as the frost line deepens? At 48 in frost a frost-protected shallow foundation is $13.2k against $20.6k slab, $24.1k crawlspace and $54.9k basement in a stylised model.
- [`research/house-shellvalue`](house-shellvalue) — does a factory-built shell pay once the interest on the days it saves is counted? Alone it saves 15.5 working days (0.22% of price) and breaks even near a $629,000 house at an assumed $1,500 crane day.
- [`research/house-schedule-check`](house-schedule-check) — does an independent second schedule model agree with `house-schedule`? Same ranking (early ordering of long-lead items first, prefab only with it, handoff gap matters), different magnitudes (-11% against -29%): sizes are set by assumptions. Crew-merging does nothing. Every input assumed.
- [`research/house-orderlead`](house-orderlead) — when should a builder order long-lead materials once holding them costs money? Extends `house-schedule`: ordering ~2 lead times ahead of plan cuts the build from 214 to 182 working days and total cost from 3.34% to 2.91% of price; ordering everything on day 0 saves more days but costs more (4.00%).
- [`research/house-season`](house-season) — does the month a house build starts change how long it takes? Same Monte Carlo as `house-schedule` with a monthly weather calendar: in an assumed cold climate a December start takes 231 working days against 211 for July (~20 d); in a mild one, 4 d. Early ordering saves ~63 d in every month. Weather numbers assumed.
- [`research/house-schedule`](house-schedule) — how much do fewer handoffs, parallel trades, early ordering and a factory shell cut a house's build time? Discrete-event Monte Carlo: critical path 100 working days, baseline 214; early ordering -63 d, fewer handoffs -21, a factory shell only -6 unless ordered at day 0 (-78). Stylised, uncalibrated.
- [`research/capture-crop`](capture-crop) — how long a lens can a 360 camera fake by cropping into a reel? An 8K equirect is native only to ~24 mm-equivalent at 1080x1920 (85 mm is a 3.5x upscale), and perspective is set by distance: a stick at 0.8 m enlarges a 10 cm-nearer feature 14% vs 7.6% at an 85 mm framing distance, so portrait looks need a camera standing back. Pixel-count upper bound. First project of track 13.
- [`research/capture-kit`](capture-kit) — what should a travel creator pack to film from many angles within a weight budget and the 100 Wh battery limit? Exact search over 17,280 kits reaches all 18 band x viewpoint shots at 5 kg; a greedy packer stalls at 12 because lenses need their body (assumed device figures).
- [`research/capture-storage`](capture-storage) — how much footage does a multi-camera travel kit produce, and what limits carrying and backing it up? Four cameras make ~860 GB a day; cards weigh ~8 g but the evening copy takes 2.7 h on one reader, so a safe plan needs 6 readers and ~450 g (assumed device figures).
- [`research/capture-rig`](capture-rig) — how many cameras, and where, to film a person from every angle? A 30° gap between neighbours needs 13 cameras on a ring (15° needs 25), but depth error is set by convergence angle, not count: a pair's error falls 11× from 5° to 90° (7.3 to 0.64 mm) while 3 vs 16 ring cameras only halves it. Idealised 2-D lower bounds.
- [`research/capture-novelview`](capture-novelview) — how far apart can cameras be before a reel editor can no longer synthesise the angle between them? With exact depth a two-view warp beats cutting to the nearest camera only up to about 30° (24.8 vs 23.6 dB); beyond that holes dominate, and a bystander pulls the limit in to about 15° (ray-traced toy scene; ordering, not decibels).
- [`research/capture-stereodepth`](capture-stereodepth) — is the depth a camera rig can actually measure good enough for the in-between angle? Block-matching stereo on rendered pairs feeds the two-view warp: mean depth error 14.9% at 3 cm baseline to 2.7% at 50 cm, coverage peaks at 91%, and the result never beats cutting to the nearest camera (22.3 vs 24.8 dB at a 15° gap; exact depth 27.3 dB).
- [`research/capture-hybrid`](capture-hybrid) — can the holes and correlated errors of measured stereo depth be repaired so in-between synthesis beats cutting to the nearest camera? A hole filler and a no-ground-truth disagreement gate that falls back to the raw nearest camera lift a 15° gap from 22.3 dB to 29.2 dB against 24.8 dB for cutting (held-out seeds 29.2); with a bystander the gate loses its edge (26.1 vs 26.3 for plain filling). Toy scene.
- [`research/capture-softgate`](capture-softgate) — does a soft confidence weight beat a hard switch for repairing stereo-depth view synthesis, and does the matcher's cost help? A ramped weight on warp disagreement beats the hard gate by 0.1 to 0.5 dB in every condition (29.5 vs 29.3 dB held out at 15°) and leaves a quarter fewer seams, closing only a tenth of the oracle gap; matching cost is a worse signal than disagreement (level with hole filling, worse with a bystander). Toy scene.
- [`research/capture-follow`](capture-follow) — which platform can follow a creator through a crowd and keep the shot? A drone that ignores pedestrians frames 99.9% of the time but falls to 39% at 0.3 people/m² once it avoids them; a wheeled robot is stopped by step lines 42–47% of the walk, and a flat-floor one that ignores people passes within 0.5 m ~500 times an hour. 2-D simulation, assumed platform figures.
- [`research/capture-edit`](capture-edit) — how much of a multi-camera shoot can an automatic edit recover? At detector noise 0.3 a fixed 3 s rotation shows mean true quality 0.546, a cut-cost (Viterbi) edit 0.802 vs 0.815 on true scores, and best-score-every-frame 0.723 at 345 cuts a minute; clock sync fails first on short clips (5 s at noise 0.25: 25% over 40 ms). Synthetic quality model, no real footage.
- [`research/capture-trigger`](capture-trigger) — does recording only on a trigger save battery, and what does it miss? With 6 thirty-second moments an hour, standby with a 10 s pre-roll uses 19.4 Wh of an 80 Wh continuous day (76% less) and captures 90% of interesting seconds; deep sleep uses 7.7 Wh but captures 69%. Misses and wake latency, not the battery, set coverage. Assumed device figures, synthetic day.
- [`research/capture-wake`](capture-wake) — when a camera rig records only on a trigger, should every camera have its own, or should one wake the rest? Four standby cameras with their own triggers use 80 Wh of 320 Wh continuous but get all four angles on only 68% of interesting seconds; one sentinel waking the others costs the same and gives 90.5%, and any-camera wake gives 100% for 92 Wh but its false-alarm energy grows with detector count. Assumed device figures.
- [`research/capture-sentinel`](capture-sentinel) — where in a camera ring should the sentinel go when the subject faces a favourite direction? A sentinel facing the favourite direction misses 8.6% of moments against 34.4% for the one behind; spread cameras' misses are anti-correlated, so capture-wake's independence assumption overstates the all-miss rate by 2x on a ring of 4 and understates it 12x for a tight cluster. With the turned-face miss measured in `capture-detectors` (54 faces) in place of the assumed curve, placement matters only if the back of a head goes undetected (untested), and weak detectors miss 8 to 45% of moments even with four cameras listening.
- [`research/capture-bystander`](capture-bystander) — when does automatic blurring miss a bystander's face? On an assumed size-recall model a face detector leaves 5% of identifiable faces unblurred per frame, blurring the head of any detected body under 0.1%, and independent per-frame misses show a face in some frame of a 5 s shot 100% of the time; an offline whole-track blur leaves 0 to 5%. No real footage or detector.
- [`research/energy-home`](energy-home) — how big should a home's solar array and battery be, and at what price does the battery pay? Hourly model on synthetic weather (assumed inputs): a 6 kW array meets 35% of an 8,000 kWh/yr load, a 10 kWh battery lifts it to 59%, and the battery pays only below about $451/kWh installed at $0.05 export (assumed price $800).
- [`research/energy-tou`](energy-tou) — does time-of-use pricing make a home battery pay, and which size? On an assumed tariff with the flat tariff's average, a 10 kWh battery behind 6 kW PV breaks even at $641/kWh installed against $444 flat (assumed price $800); only 2.5 to 5 kWh clears $800, and the peak price steers the answer (`energy-home` with hourly prices).
- [`research/energy-thermal`](energy-thermal) — can a house-sized sand heat store carry summer heat into winter? A transient model: 10 MWh after 120 days needs 222 m³ (70 if lossless) and returns 26% of the electricity as heat, so a COP-4 heat pump wins unless its electricity is 15× dearer; a days-scale store at house size. Stylised.
- [`research/energy-lcos`](energy-lcos) — which storage technology is cheapest per MWh delivered, by duration? Li-ion wins to about 4 h ($116/MWh), cavern stores beyond ($109 vs Li-ion $337 at 24 h), cavern hydrogen only past ~300 h. All inputs assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/energy-lcos-mc`](energy-lcos-mc) — how robust is the cheapest storage technology by duration to uncertain costs? Monte Carlo over `energy-lcos`: Li-ion wins 96% of scenarios at 2 h but 18% at 8 h; no technology wins over 48% between 6 and 12 h; caverns win most from 24 h. Ranges assumed. Stylised.
- [`research/energy-cell`](energy-cell) — how much better can a Li-ion cell get by redesigning its electrodes, and does a smarter search help? In a PyBaMM model, thicker, more porous electrodes with more negative capacity lift energy after 100 cycles from 109 to ~143 Wh/kg (+30%); random, Bayesian and session-agent proposers end within 3% of each other. One aggressively ageing parameter set. Stylised.
- [`research/energy-density`](energy-density) — how much energy can each storage technology hold per kilogram, at the most? First-principles ceilings that reproduce five published values within 0.3% (Li-S 2,567, Li-O2 3,458 Wh/kg). No battery couple exceeds 1.65 eV per reactant atom, so light atoms set the winners (Wh/kg = 26,801 × eV/atom ÷ g/mol); flywheels top out near 0.3 eV/atom, heat and pumped hydro far lower. Active material only. First project of track 16.
- [`research/energy-volumetric`](energy-volumetric) — how much energy can each storage chemistry hold per litre, and does the ranking by weight survive? Volumetric ceilings from reactant densities: zinc-air 9,659 Wh/L with air, Li-ion 1,382, lead-acid 724, hydrogen 1,314 at 700 bar.
- [`research/energy-practical`](energy-practical) — how close do real batteries, flow cells and hydrogen tanks get to their first-principles ceilings? A Panasonic NCR18650B cell reaches 0.64 of the Li-ion reactant ceiling (243 of 380 Wh/kg), LiFePO4 0.25–0.45, lead-acid 0.21–0.24; DOE's projected 700 bar H2 system is 5.1% hydrogen by mass against 24–41% ideal. Small sourced dataset, primary and secondary rows marked. Second project of track 16.
- [`research/energy-mix`](energy-mix) — what is the cheapest mix of solar, wind, gas and storage that cuts emissions, and what does the last 5% cost? PyPSA on 2019 German hourly demand (assumed costs): $79/MWh all-gas, $113 at an 80% cut, $177 at zero emissions with 10.6 TWh of hydrogen storage; abatement cost $30/t to $1,765/t for the last 5%. Stylised.
- [`research/mimic-crossplay`](mimic-crossplay) — does the prompt-only imitation game settle or cycle? Every imitator prompt from `mimic-loop` against every detector prompt (0.5B on both sides): pure equilibria below chance (reddit 0.396, reviews 0.292), no cycles at a noise margin, and text length tracks detector accuracy. Stylised, one loop seed; model-size question open.
- [`research/mimic-dialogue`](mimic-dialogue) — does a second turn make a plainly prompted LLM easier to spot in dialogue? A prompt-free n-gram detector gets AUROC 0.93 on one imitated user turn and 0.98 on two, on 400 OpenAssistant threads with a 0.5B imitator. Stylised baseline, no prompt optimised.
- [`research/mimic-featuretransfer`](mimic-featuretransfer) — which features let a human-vs-machine detector carry over to a context family it never saw? Leave-one-family-out AUROC with train-only vectorisers: words 0.931, characters 0.916, both 0.938, function words alone 0.797, surface formatting 0.687 (below chance on abstracts and recipes), length alone 0.155 to 0.885 depending on family. Stylised: one classifier, plain-prompt RAID text, no LLM.
- [`research/mimic-anycontext-detect`](mimic-anycontext-detect) — does a prompt-free n-gram human-vs-machine detector hold up on context families it never saw? Detector side of `mimic-anycontext` with no LLM (model download was blocked): AUROC 0.934 on unseen families against 0.985 in-family, and 40 target items close most of the gap on recipes. Stylised: plain-prompt RAID text only.
- [`research/mimic-anycontext-prompt`](mimic-anycontext-prompt) — can a prompt make a 0.5B model detect machine text, and does it carry to unseen families? Negative: the prompted Qwen2.5-0.5B detector is inverted (mean AUROC 0.278 plain, 0.293 universal on held-out families, none above 0.5 on any family) while the n-gram detector gets 0.93. Stylised: one small model, 16 prompts, plain-prompt RAID text.
- [`research/mimic-twinc2st`](mimic-twinc2st) — does a classifier two-sample test catch digital-twin errors that a paired trajectory error misses? Stylised pendulum: boosted-tree AUROC 0.72 for a friction error whose RMSE is a quarter of the sensor noise, 0.92 for Gaussian-vs-Student-t noise at zero paired error. Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-mmd`](mimic-mmd) — does a kernel MMD test catch the same wrong digital twins as a classifier two-sample test? Stylised pendulum: raw-trajectory MMD is blind to a Coulomb-friction error (power 0.05 to 0.06) that boosted trees catch from n = 200, but MMD on second differences detects a +25% noise-level error at 0.97 from n = 100.
- [`research/mimic-mmdmujoco`](mimic-mmdmujoco) — does a kernel MMD test catch the same wrong MuJoCo digital twins as a classifier two-sample test?
