Stylised MuJoCo pendulum: boosted-tree C2ST power 1.00 at n = 200 for Euler at 0.02 s and a noise-shape error where MMD stays near 0.05; MMD on second differences catches a +25% noise-level error; friction errors undetected by all at n <= 200.
- [`research/mimic-twinregret`](mimic-twinregret) — does a digital twin that a classifier can tell apart from the real system also make a worse controller? Stylised pendulum: 6 of 15 wrong twins have zero controller regret at C2ST AUROC 0.58 to 0.999; the worst regret (3.8%) comes from a twin with no sensor noise and zero paired error. Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-twinswing`](mimic-twinswing) — on a swing-up task where friction matters, does a twin's detectability predict what tuning on it costs? Stylised pendulum: only the two gravity-error twins cost much (3.7% and 8.3% extra real cost); 13 others cost under 0.3% at C2ST AUROC 0.51 to 1.0. Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-twinswinggrid`](mimic-twinswinggrid) — does a finer controller grid change which wrong pendulum twins cost something on the swing-up task? No: the same two gravity-error twins cost 3.8% and 6.1%, the rest under 0.3%; detectability vs regret ρ = 0.38 (p = 0.16). Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-twingrid`](mimic-twingrid) — does a wider gain grid, or a controller that uses the twin's gravity, change which wrong pendulum twins are costly? Gravity errors stay the costly ones; giving the controller the twin's g/L made g/L −20% regret 13 times larger (0.013 to 0.177). Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-twinrand`](mimic-twinrand) — does tuning a controller on a spread of randomized twins cut the regret of tuning on one wrong twin? Stylised pendulum: it brings the no-noise twin's regret from 0.0098 to 0.00006, but a very wide range raises a doubled-noise twin's worst-seed regret 7.5-fold. Preliminary, no LLM.
- [`research/mimic-twinnoiserand`](mimic-twinnoiserand) — does randomizing only the noise level of a wrong twin keep domain randomization's rescue without its blow-up? Stylised pendulum: the no-noise twin's regret goes 0.0098 to 0 and stays small at a wide range (worst seed 0.0015 vs 0.042 for all-four), but friction and gravity errors are not helped. Preliminary, no LLM.
- [`research/mimic-twinpower`](mimic-twinpower) — how many real trajectories does a classifier test need to flag a wrong digital twin, and are the twins it flags the ones that cost a controller? Stylised pendulum: all six twins with controller regret of at least 0.001 are flagged by 80 real trajectories, and so are three that cost nothing. Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-mujocotwin`](mimic-mujocotwin) — does a classifier two-sample test see the integrator, timestep and friction gaps of a MuJoCo digital twin? Stylised pendulum: AUROC 0.98 for Euler at 0.02 s, 0.53 (null) for a paired error of 0.003 rad, 0.92 for Gaussian-vs-Student-t noise at zero paired error. Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/mimic-stack`](mimic-stack) — does adding a zero-shot perplexity score to the n-gram detector help on unseen families? The two scores are uncorrelated within class, but averaging them lifts held-out AUROC only from 0.926 to 0.935 (paired gain +0.009, interval −0.010 to +0.026); fitted stacks do no better. Preliminary, 720 held-out texts, small scorer, no LLM run.
- [`research/mimic-evade`](mimic-evade) — can cheap surface edits (typos, contractions, no chat preamble) fool the n-gram human-vs-machine detector? Most move held-out AUROC under 0.005; 2% typos cost 0.012 and keeping half the text 0.051 (from 0.927), and a refit detector wins it back.
- [`research/mimic-whitebox`](mimic-whitebox) — how many words must an imitator change to evade a word n-gram human-vs-machine detector it can read? Deleting the 20 most machine-ward word types per text takes held-out AUROC from 0.948 to chance (0.487); a refit detector recovers to 0.94-1.00.
- [`research/mimic-blackbox`](mimic-blackbox) — how many detector queries does an imitator need to evade a word n-gram human-vs-machine detector it cannot read? About 100 score queries per text, deleting the 20 best word types, take held-out AUROC from 0.948 to 0.520 (white-box 0.487); blind deletion does nothing. Stylised, no fluency check.
- [`research/mimic-retrain`](mimic-retrain) — does refitting a text detector on attacked text stop a black-box evader? It settles after one refit: fresh-attack AUROC stays near 0.81 (from 0.719) while clean AUROC falls from 0.948 to about 0.90. Stylised, no fluency check.
- [`research/mimic-genshift`](mimic-genshift) — does a prompt-free detector carry over to a generator or decoding it never saw? The `mimic-contexts` n-gram detector loses 0.01 to 0.02 AUROC on a chat model left out of the fit (0.96 to 0.98 left out) and 0.025 to 0.05 on a left-out decoding (sampling 0.936); averaging over four same-source texts lifts AUROC to 0.999. Preliminary, plain-prompt RAID text only.
- [`research/mimic-mauve`](mimic-mauve) — does MAUVE rank human-vs-machine gaps the way a text detector does? Across 20 RAID family × model cells MAUVE (lexical embedding) and a cross-fit n-gram detector agree loosely (Spearman −0.60) and disagree on recipes and news; every cell is far below the human-vs-human null (0.92 to 0.99). Preliminary, no GPT-2 features.
- [`research/mimic-surface`](mimic-surface) — how much text does a prompt-free detector need, and what does it key on? The `mimic-contexts` n-gram detector on held-out families: AUROC 0.65 at 50 characters, 0.84 at 400, 0.93 at 1500; stripping punctuation or shuffling words costs at most 0.014. Preliminary, plain-prompt RAID text only.
- [`research/mimic-threshold`](mimic-threshold) — if a detector's threshold is set for a 5% false-positive rate, does it hold on an unseen text type? Leave-one-family-out with the `mimic-contexts` n-gram detector: realised rate 0.00 to 0.54 (abstracts 0.54, books 0.18), and recipes catch 1.7% of machine text. Preliminary, plain-prompt RAID text only.
- [`research/mimic-recalib`](mimic-recalib) — how many human texts does it take to re-set a detector's threshold for a 5% false-positive rate on a new kind of text? About 80: with the plain 95th percentile k = 20 gives a mean false-positive rate of 9% (36% chance above 10%), k = 80 gives 6.1%; a conformal rule holds 4.9% but costs recall and needs 19+ texts. Stylised, RAID text, one n-gram detector.
- [`research/mimic-batch`](mimic-batch) — how many texts from one source does a weak human-vs-machine detector need before a batch verdict is reliable? Four all-machine texts give AUROC 0.997 (one text: 0.935); a quarter-machine source needs about 32; but a threshold set on 150 human texts drifts from 7% to 15% false flags at k = 32. Preliminary, RAID text, one n-gram detector.
- [`research/mimic-cursor`](mimic-cursor) — can an imitator that changes its own code fool a human-vs-bot cursor detector, and does shared feedback help? On DUX cursor strokes of 46 people, a held-out reference detector catches every final imitator at AUROC 0.99 to 1.00 in all four feedback conditions; two-way sharing gave the strongest detectors in cross-play (0.81 vs 0.70 blind, interval includes 0), and an 8x larger search stops at 0.88, the level of replaying real strokes. Preliminary.
- [`research/mimic-webcam`](mimic-webcam) — can a generator imitate a person's webcam emotion signals well enough to fool a detector? On DUX Affectiva scores of 46 people, every fixed generator and every loop-tuned imitator is caught at AUROC 0.997 or higher by a detector the loop never saw; only replaying real windows gets close to chance (0.61). Preliminary.
- [`research/mimic-webcam-tails`](mimic-webcam-tails) — does giving a webcam-signal generator real heavy tails and bursts fool a detector? No: copula and quiet/burst generators still separate from held-out people at AUROC 1.000 (8 seeds); restoring per-window level and spread brings level and spread to near chance, but extremes (0.995) still give it away; copying a real window's value set (G6) puts those at chance too, yet all-groups AUROC stays 0.998 on temporal structure.
- [`research/mimic-webcam-stitch`](mimic-webcam-stitch) — why is block-stitched real webcam signal still caught? With 50-frame blocks random stitching gives AUROC 0.887, one person's windows only 0.687, join-matched 0.796, both 0.588 (= whole-window replay 0.597); at 5 frames all are caught (8 seeds).
- [`research/free-robustpaired`](free-robustpaired) — was the paired twin test weak, or was its statistic? Stylised pendulum, Student-t noise: the mean squared residual has power ~0.1 at a 10% Coulomb error up to n = 400, while median, trimmed, absolute and Huber statistics reach 0.94–1.00 from n = 50; Gaussian noise barely cares.
- [`research/free-robusttails`](free-robusttails) — which robust paired test still catches a wrong digital twin when noise has infinite variance or whole runs go bad? Stylised pendulum, 10% Coulomb error: median and trimmed residuals reach power 1.00 under Student-t(2) cells but only 0.17&ndash;0.40 under bad runs, where a per-run median reaches 0.89&ndash;1.00; no statistic wins both.
- [`research/free-robustcombo`](free-robustcombo) — which paired test of a digital twin survives both heavy-tailed cells and whole bad runs? Stylised pendulum, 10% Coulomb error: a two-level statistic (trimmed mean of squared residuals within each run, then trimmed mean across runs) has power 0.99&ndash;1.00 at n = 50 under t(2) cells, bad runs at 0.86 and both at once, where the earlier single-level statistics each fail one case. Costs power under Gaussian noise (0.84 against 0.91).
- [`research/free-c2stpower`](free-c2stpower) — how many real trajectories does a classifier two-sample test need to reject a wrong digital twin? Stylised pendulum: for a 20% Coulomb error a paired error test has power 0.98 at 100 trajectories vs 0.25 for the C2ST (which needs ~200), but for a 10% error the paired test stalls at 0.09–0.20 up to 800 while the C2ST reaches 1.00 at 400, and for same-variance noise-shape error the paired test has power ≤0.07 at every size. Null calibration approximate (60 replicates). Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/free-citeaudit`](free-citeaudit) — audit of how many research READMEs flag a citation as unchecked: 94 of 550 (17.1%), with Dawid and Skene (1979), Zhang et al. (2019), Horowitz (2014) and Horvitz (1999) the most repeated; counts admissions only, nothing verified.
- [`research/free-secretarynoise`](free-secretarynoise) — how much noisy scoring costs the secretary problem: with n = 50 the best cutoff stays at 16–17 (19 noiseless) while P(best) falls from 0.373 to 0.145 at noise 1; cutoff rules only, simulation.
- [`research/free-sprtcert`](free-sprtcert) — how many simulator rollouts a sequential test saves when certifying a failure rate: exact dynamic programming shows Wald's SPRT uses about half the rollouts of the best fixed test at either hypothesis (79/113 vs 181 for 1% vs 5%), and 6–17% fewer at its worst point in between.
- [`research/free-intervalcov`](free-intervalcov) — which 95% upper bound on a failure rate really covers 95% at rollout sample sizes: exact coverage shows only Clopper-Pearson never undercovers (min 0.950), Wilson dips to 0.93, Jeffreys to 0.85 and Wald to 0.06–0.75; zero-failure certification of p ≤ 1% takes 299 rollouts (CP), 268 (Wilson) or 191 (Jeffreys).
- [`research/free-sprtcap`](free-sprtcap) — how long can a sequential rollout test run, and what does capping it cost? Exact: the SPRT's 99th percentile is 309 rollouts against a 79 mean (1% vs 5%; fixed test 181), 28% of runs exceed the fixed size at the midpoint rate; capping at the fixed size adds 34–38% to the error, a cap of 1.5 times it keeps the error at 0.053 to 0.057 for a mean within 2% of uncapped.
- [`research/free-secretarymargin`](free-secretarymargin) — with noisy scores, a rule that must beat the earlier best by a margin raises P(pick the true best) from 0.233 to 0.244 at noise 0.5 and 0.143 to 0.149 at noise 1 (n = 50, fresh-trial test); no gain at noise 2; simulation.
- [`research/free-jittertwin`](free-jittertwin) — does a sampled-data loop tuned on a fixed-period twin survive random sampling intervals? Uniform jitter up to ±100% never destabilises it, but missed samples do: almost-surely stable up to miss probability 0.556, mean-square stable only to 0.270 (0.05 for a deadbeat tuning); at 0.4 all 2000 simulated runs converge. Stylised scalar model.
- [`research/free-retunegain`](free-retunegain) — does retuning the gain on the sampling-interval law, instead of the fixed-period twin, remove the missed-sample penalty? Yes for this plant: the mean-square-optimal gain is stable at every miss probability (worst E g² 0.478 vs a twin-gain limit of 0.270), at the price of a slower nominal loop; a gain designed for q = 0.3 stays stable at every true q. Stylised scalar model.
- [`research/free-c2stexact`](free-c2stexact) — which significance rule gives a classifier two-sample test the right false-rejection rate on a digital twin? Stylised pendulum: cross-validated rules falsely reject 0.06 to 0.13 at a nominal 0.05, a holdout rank-sum test holds 0.04 to 0.06 at roughly twice the data cost. Preliminary, simulated only.
- [`research/free-c2stperm`](free-c2stperm) — does a permutation test on the cross-validated statistic keep its size and beat the holdout rule's power on a digital twin? Stylised pendulum: null rates 0.040 and 0.020, power 0.367 vs 0.183 for holdout at n=200 (Coulomb -10%).
- [`research/free-kfmismatch`](free-kfmismatch) — how much does a wrong process-noise guess cost a Kalman filter? Exact scalar steady state: under-guessing `q` by ×100 costs ×7.4 MSE, over-guessing by ×100 only ×1.6 (bounded by `r`), and the under-guessing filter reports 5× too-small error bars. Stylised.
- [`research/free-qmcdr`](free-qmcdr) — do low-discrepancy scenarios beat iid domain randomisation for evaluating a policy in a twin? Latin hypercubes only cut MSE 1.6–12× (rate stays `n^-0.5`); shifted Halton changes the rate for smooth returns (149× lower MSE at d=2) and keeps 4–24× on a success-rate jump, shrinking with d; the `R_d` lattice is erratic at d=5; 95% intervals from 8 randomisations cover 94–97%. Stylised.
- [`research/free-rdseeds`](free-rdseeds) — why does the `R_d` low-discrepancy lattice lose its edge at d=5 when scoring a twin policy? Not a seed fluke (exponent −0.45 ± 0.01 over six seeds) and not a smooth dimension effect (a dip between d=4 and d=6); a `√p` generator gets −0.66 at d=5. A simple resonance score does not explain it. Stylised.
- [`research/free-f32shadow`](free-f32shadow) — how long does a float32 twin of a chaotic system shadow the float64 one, and do its statistics survive? Lorenz and Rössler: float32 departs from float64 after a median 12.3 and 164 time units, like a float64 start error of about 5×10⁻⁶ (100× the float32 roundoff), but the Lyapunov exponent and time-averaged statistics agree within sampling error (stylised).
- [`research/free-f32bounce`](free-f32bounce) — does a float32 simulation of a bouncing, chaotic system lose its statistics? Sinai billiard: float32 leaves the float64 trajectory after a median 21 collisions, like a float64 start error of about one unit roundoff, and changes the event order one collision later, but the mean free path and circle-hit share agree with float64 and with Cauchy's exact value (stylised).
- [`research/benford-repo`](benford-repo) — do this repository's file sizes follow Benford's law? Not as a whole (byte MAD 0.018, nonconforming) because templated files repeat the same size thousands of times; counting each distinct size once gives acceptable conformity (MAD 0.0068).
- [`research/sensing-imuarray`](sensing-imuarray) — how many cheap gyros, fused, match one better gyro at equal cost? With independent errors the price ratio must beat `k²` (100 for a 10× quieter unit); shared error above `1/k²` means no array size ties; unequal white/bias improvement makes the answer depend on integration time. Stylised.
- [`research/sensing-imutemp`](sensing-imutemp) — does a shared temperature coefficient put a floor under an array of cheap gyros? The shared part of the tempco does not average out: in the illustrative case a 3× better gyro can no longer be matched at any array size above a 0.2 °C/min ramp over 100 s; compensation quality, not array size, decides.
- [`research/sensing-burst`](sensing-burst) — how many frames from a small sensor, merged, match one frame from a sensor with A× the pixel area? A shot-noise-limited burst needs A frames, a read-noise-limited one up to A² (formula and simulation agree); splitting the same total exposure never ties; registration blur and bracketed HDR are measured. Synthetic scenes only.
- [`research/sensing-ghost`](sensing-ghost) — what does a moving subject do to a burst merge? A plain mean ghosts at every speed and gets worse as frames are added (30.5 dB at 8 frames, 25.0 at 48), median and sigma-clip do no better for a slow subject, and a reference-guided merge keeps improving and ties a 4×-area frame at about 6 frames at 0.1 dB cost when nothing moves. Synthetic scenes only.
- [`research/sensing-ghostalign`](sensing-ghostalign) — can aligning a moving subject rescue a burst merge instead of rejecting it? Inside the subject, yes (37.0 dB against 31.5 for reject-only and 32.8 for a 4×-area frame at 8 frames), but over the whole image rejecting is as good or better (31.7 against 31.8 dB), the aligner moves 10–31% of static pixels on noise, and the revealed-background ring stays 4 dB short of the large frame. Synthetic scenes only.
- [`research/sensing-ghostring`](sensing-ghostring) — can a temporal-median background or a subject mask fix the edge ring that burst alignment leaves behind? No: four ring treatments leave it at 21.6–27.2 dB at 8 frames against 31.6–32.0 for a 4×-area frame, none beats plain aligned-plus-reject (27.0 dB), and the mask misses most of a slow subject. Synthetic scenes only.
- [`research/sensing-ghostedge`](sensing-ghostedge) — does modelling the sub-pixel coverage of a moving subject fix the edge ring? Yes, on a known shape: a layered disc-over-background model fitted from the burst lifts the ring from 27.1 to 33.1 dB at 8 frames and 2 px per frame (4×-area frame: 31.7 dB), less at slow speeds, and it is sensitive to a 0.5 px centre error. Synthetic flat disc only.
- [`research/sensing-superres`](sensing-superres) — can a burst of hand-shaken low-resolution frames recover detail that merging and upsampling cannot? At 2× in bright light a least-squares reconstruction reaches 29.5 dB at 16 frames against 24.0 for merge-and-upsample and 26.6 for merge-and-deblur, so 2.9 dB is sub-pixel information; it nearly vanishes in dim light and falls to nothing at about 0.27 px of shift error. Synthetic scenes only.
- [`research/sensing-timesync`](sensing-timesync) — how many milliseconds of camera-to-gyro time offset double a fused attitude error, and can an alignment fit recover it? About 12–14 ms at 30 deg/s motion; the fit recovers it to ~1 ms with motion and fails on a still platform. Synthetic, single axis.
- [`research/sensing-syncskew`](sensing-syncskew) — how much motion does a camera-to-gyro offset fit need, and what does clock skew do to a one-off calibration? A 20 ms offset needs about 16 deg/s rms motion to fit reliably; 1000 ppm skew leaves 0.254 deg error after a fixed-offset fit against 0.155 deg when skew is fitted too. Stylised, synthetic.
- [`research/sensing-vio`](sensing-vio) — which upgrade buys the most visual-inertial accuracy, a sharper camera or a better IMU? On a planar synthetic smoother only camera resolution moved drift (1280 px 1.2 % vs 1.7 % at 640 px); a 0.25–4× change in IMU noise did not, on 12 s paths. Stylised, not EuRoC.
- [`research/sensing-scan2cad`](sensing-scan2cad) — how accurate must a scan be to rebuild a part as parametric CAD? On synthetic plate scans, RANSAC plane and circle fits give all five dimensions within 0.2 mm for noise up to 0.2 mm, but at 0.5 mm noise the error is a bias (thickness −0.20 mm) that more points do not remove, and a 0.5% scale error alone moves an 80 mm length by 0.40 mm. Synthetic, one shape.
- [`research/sensing-recon`](sensing-recon) — how many photos does a usable 3-D reconstruction need? On a synthetic object with known cameras, 12 views on a full orbit triangulate every feature, 8 views 89% and 4 or fewer at most 13%; per-point error then depends on pixel noise and pose error, not view count. Triangulation stage only (synthetic).
- [`research/sensing-thermalfuse`](sensing-thermalfuse) — does a low-resolution thermal camera find people in darkness and smoke, and what does misalignment cost? On synthetic scenes RGB finds 36% in bright light and none in the dark or dense smoke, thermal alone 65% everywhere (capped by warm decoys), fusion 94% in bright light and the thermal level otherwise; a 4 px (one thermal pixel) displacement costs the fused detector 19 points and 6 px erases the gain. Synthetic only.
- [`research/society-robotnorms`](society-robotnorms) — how many robots does it take to change which side a crowd walks on? Robots that hold a programmed side tip a crowd once they are 16–32% of it, more when people remember more past partners (simulation matches mean field within 0.02); just short of that they make people collide up to 14× as often as adaptive robots, and placement matters more than numbers when people meet only neighbours. Stylised.
- [`research/codesign-gripper`](codesign-gripper) — which printable two-finger gripper holds eight objects for the least plastic, and can an agent beat CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation at 40 MuJoCo evaluations? Median best objective 0.991 (Bayesian opt) vs 0.979 (CMA-ES), 0.965 (random) and 0.963 (session agent, one run); the cheapest winners (0.5 g flat fingers) are also the most fragile when object offsets triple. Stylised, no learned policy.
- [`research/codesign-sar`](codesign-sar) — which cheap wheel-legged rover covers the most stylised rubble under a 120 USD cap, and can an agent beat CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation at 40 evaluations? Wheel radius at its bound plus about 0.5 N m torque reaches the ceiling (J 0.783, 69 USD); Bayesian opt and CMA-ES 0.782, session agent 0.783, random 0.716; stylised, falls never occurred.
- [`research/codesign-agri`](codesign-agri) — which three-finger pad design picks the most unbruised strawberry-like fruit under a 60 USD cap, and can an agent beat CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation at 40 evaluations? Bayesian optimisation did (median J 0.635 against the agent's 0.618); about 20% of fruit are lost to clutter collisions no pad setting changes. Stylised, assumed constants.
- [`research/codesign-logistics`](codesign-logistics) — which suction gripper picks the most of a stylised mixed tote (boxes, polybags, bottles) under an 80 USD cap, and can an agent beat CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation at 40 evaluations? The agent's best J was 0.519 against 0.478 for Bayesian optimisation (median of 20 seeds); the best design is two 18.5 mm cups at 80 kPa and 60 L/min, and a catalogue-default single 30 mm cup scores 0.075. Stylised, assumed constants.
- [`research/codesign-aero`](codesign-aero) — should a quadrotor's airframe and its learned roll controller be sized together? Yes in this stylised model: joint CMA-ES reaches 5.25° RMS roll error, equal to the grid optimum, against 7.06° for lightest-airframe-first; a controller reused across airframes is worse on 41% of them.
- [`research/codesign-aero-robust`](codesign-aero-robust) — does the best quadrotor airframe stay best under widened motor-strength, mass and lag randomisation? Mostly: top five unchanged (Spearman 0.75), but retuning gains helps the median airframe 0.91° (stylised).
- [`research/codesign-aero-joint`](codesign-aero-joint) — should airframe and gains be searched jointly under an uncertain plant? Yes in this stylised model: joint CMA-ES at 960 evaluations (5.75° median held-out, 8 seeds) beats random search and a 4800-evaluation grid-then-tune best cell (6.00°).
- [`research/codesign-aero-seeds`](codesign-aero-seeds) — is the airframe ranking from one tuning run reproducible? With wide-tuned gains yes (Spearman 0.99 over six replicates); with nominal-tuned gains it is noisier (0.84) and the earlier 0.17° regret was within noise (stylised).
- [`research/codesign-transfer`](codesign-transfer) — does the ranking of soft-fruit gripper designs survive a shift in friction, fruit, placement error and print tolerance? Yes in this model (Spearman 0.993 over 600 designs), but the nominal optimum loses 13% of its J and a robust optimum gains only about 0.015 on average; sim-to-sim, assumed ranges.
- [`research/society-plaza`](society-plaza) — do robots that keep to one side make people walk on it, or jam the corridor? In a social-force corridor, robots that adapt change nothing, but robots that hold the left cut people's speed from 0.91 to 0.39 of desired by 30% of the crowd and halve lane separation, while people move to their side only gradually (no sharp tipping, unlike the 1D model). Stylised.
- [`research/society-trust`](society-trust) — can a robot fleet's reputation survive one bad incident? In a model where trust spreads by experience and gossip, a dent always recovers because people keep meeting robots; reputation is lost only through avoidance (at a 0.30 avoid-below threshold, 20/20, 18/20 and 0/20 crowds recover under neutral, 2× and 4× negativity in gossip), and robots that explain failures recovered every time. Stylised.
- [`research/society-commons`](society-commons) — what does it cost robots to let people go first at a shared lift or narrow pavement? At load 0.9, deference cuts people's wait 6.5× (4.60 to 0.71) and raises robots' by 2.6 units; total waiting cost is conserved, so it pays only if a person's wait costs more than a robot's, and a lone defector waits far less (2.08 vs 7.65). Queueing model within 2.7% of closed forms.
- [`research/society-llmtown`](society-llmtown) — do robot agents keep the norms they state? A pluggable town harness with a deterministic stub (no language model run): a robot stating "always yield" keeps it in 1 − 0.4×pressure of meetings (1.00 to 0.60), and people's cost per meeting rises from 0.20 to 1.08. Stylised.
- [`research/society-stubborn`](society-stubborn) — how many robots does it take to change a crowd's walking side when some people never adapt? With 0, 20, 40 and 50% stubborn walkers the mean-field tipping share (memory 9) rises 0.217, 0.240, 0.283, 0.329, simulation agrees within 0.02, and committed-robot conflict costs barely change. Stylised.
- [`research/society-blocs`](society-blocs) — how many robots does it take to change a crowd's walking side when some people always walk on the robots' side? Each 5% of always-robot-side people cuts the memory-9 mean-field tipping share (0.217) by about 0.04, 25% tip a crowd with no robots, opposing blocs raise the bar, and simulation agrees within 0.01. Stylised.
- [`research/society-converts`](society-converts) — does a stubborn block that slowly converts let a smaller robot minority tip a crowd's walking side? With 40% stubborn walkers, 24% robots never tip (0 of 12) but tip all 12 at 0.1% conversion per round (median round 721).
- [`research/society-twinfidelity`](society-twinfidelity) — does the robots-in-a-corridor conclusion survive a more humanlike crowd? Adaptive robots stay free and committed robots never flip the crowd in all five simulated crowds, but the size of the slowdown (loss of 0.17–0.49 of desired speed at 30% robots) and of personal-space intrusions (0.03–0.36 of agent time) depends strongly on how humanlike the people are. Stylised.
- [`research/society-show`](society-show) — can a simulation run be turned into a watchable, honest episode? Five seeded, labelled-fiction episodes of a robot town built on `society-trust`, with turning points found by code and a narration read from the record: even-handed gossip recovers, 4× negativity locks the town out, explained failures recover, and 2× ends both ways by seed. Stylised.
- [`research/codesign-forcefresh`](codesign-forcefresh) — do agent-proposed gripper fingers beat random search, CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation when each design gets its own trained grasp force? On 40 held-out scenes all methods' best seeds reach 0.93 to 0.95 and the ranking is within noise; on 100 fresh scenes the session agent's 0.95 falls to 0.81, level with the baseline median (stylised, preliminary, mostly negative).
- [`research/hanger-fit`](hanger-fit) — what hanger shape keeps a shirt in shape, and can one hanger fit every size for a laundry-hanging robot? In a 2D cloth-drape simulation the best single fixed hanger leaves garments 2.1 cm RMS from their worn shape (5.4 cm for a child's tee), one adjustable-width hanger set per garment 0.18 cm, and a rack of four widths under 1 cm for every size; thin wire ends fold the cloth at the tip. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-fold`](hanger-fold) — should a laundry robot hang a garment or fold it, and what can it sense to tell? A stretch-set and crease-set model on five fabric archetypes: woven shirts and linen are better hung, tees, fleece and heavy knits better folded; shoulder width, mass and stretch when lifted pick the better action for 89–91% of 600 simulated garments (always fold: 59%). The split depends mostly on the assumed crease model. Stylised, assumed fabric constants.
- [`research/hanger-insert`](hanger-insert) — how does a laundry robot get a hanger into a garment, and what does it cost the neck? A swept-section model of a rigid, telescoping or folding hanger passing a neck or hem loop: rigid hangers need 43–61 cm of loop, a tee's neck stretches up to 33% and a buttoned shirt collar cannot take them at all (shirts go in at the hem, 10 s against 6 s); a hanger that collapses to a 10 cm half width needs 31 cm. The round end `hanger-fit` wants costs about 3 cm more. Stylised, assumed constants.
- [`research/hanger-cell`](hanger-cell) — how many dryers can one laundry-hanging robot serve, and what limits it? A discrete-event model of the whole cell (pick, sense, feeder, insertion, rail, fold table) fed by `hanger-insert` and `hanger-fold`: with assumed times one arm clears a 25-item load in 8.9 min (about 5 dryers), a second arm adds 22% before the fold table binds, and the five hanger designs differ by at most 3%; pick and fold times dominate. Stylised, assumed station times.
- [`research/hanger-print`](hanger-print) — can the hanger a cloth simulation designed be 3D-printed, and does it hold a garment? `hanger-fit`'s adjustable shape and four-width rack as parametric STL and OpenSCAD with a beam check: the round-ended arm has a safety factor of 40–70 in assumed PLA, a wire-like arm 0.13, and no useful width fits a 220 mm bed whole (one arm with its hub does). Stylised, assumed material constants.
- [`research/hanger-split`](hanger-split) — can a laundry hanger be printed in two halves on a 220 mm bed, and is the bolted joint strong enough? A half-lap hub joint with two M4 bolts lets every width up to XXL print on a 220 mm bed; the joint (safety factor 3.7–13) is then weaker than the arm (40–70), and printed pegs fail. Stylised, assumed material constants; nothing printed.
- [`research/hanger-pick`](hanger-pick) — how should a laundry robot pick one garment out of a tangled dryer load? In a stylised disc-pile model aiming at a visible opening takes 13.4 assumed seconds per garment against 22.7 for pinching the top and regrasping, and pushing garments aside first is slower (17.7 s); one arm then serves 151, 110 and 127 items an hour. Assumed times, not a robot.
- [`research/hanger-pickbreak`](hanger-pickbreak) — how long may a laundry robot spend finding a garment's opening before blind pinching is faster? On paired piles of the stylised pick model, aiming wins only if the detector costs under about 9 s per pick (95% interval 8.9 to 9.6, assumed times); the limit falls as regrasps get likelier to succeed or more openings are hidden. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-misaim`](hanger-misaim) — how often may a laundry robot's opening detector aim wrong before blind pinching is faster? In the stylised `hanger-pick` model each 10 points of detector error cost about 1 s of look-time budget: 9.2 s per pick when perfect, 4.5 s at 50% wrong, none when always wrong. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-cellpick`](hanger-cellpick) — how many dryers can one laundry-hanging arm serve once the pick is simulated, not assumed? A one-arm cell drops from 169 to 147 garments an hour (5.1 to 4.4 dryers) with look-then-aim picking and to 104 with blind pinching; with two arms the fold table is the limit. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-wrap`](hanger-wrap) — does a second 3D cloth model, wrapped over the arm, agree with the 2D hanger drape? On the cases both run, it picks the same best arm width (22.8 cm for a size M tee; error correlation 0.994) and adds the bend across the arm (119 deg/cm over a 0.5 cm arm, 34 over a 2 cm one), but disagrees for a heavy knit on a thin arm and is not grid-converged. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-3d`](hanger-3d) — does the 2D hanger result survive in 3D? With an arm as deep as the garment a 3D cloth sheet matches the 2D drape (within 0.05 cm for round-ended hangers) and the best width is again 1 cm over the half shoulder; a shallow arm lets the front and back panels fall 5–8 cm and raises a wooden hanger's error from 0.4 to 2.0 cm. Stylised.
- [`research/qual-correction`](qual-correction) — can you do machine learning on qualitative data with an LLM, and when can you trust the numbers? Simulated LLM coders with known error: an 89%-accurate coder (κ = 0.72) understates a 30% code at 26% with 0% interval coverage, and when it reads one group's answers less accurately the naive group gap has the wrong sign (+3.6 against −4 points, "significant" 80% of the time). Prediction-powered inference with 200 hand-coded answers per group removes the bias and narrows intervals 23–26% against hand-coding alone, but is wider below ~80% coder accuracy. Stdlib tool (κ, Krippendorff's α, PPI). Simulated, no real model.
- [`research/qual-regression`](qual-regression) — what happens to a regression coefficient when the regressor is an LLM's code? Naive OLS gives 0.74 for a true 1.00 (0% coverage) and biases a perfectly measured covariate when coder error depends on it; DSL with ~210 hand-coded items fixes both (coverage 94–97%) but narrows the coded coefficient's interval only 14%; per-class PPI fixes 3-class shares. Simulated coders.
- [`research/qual-real`](qual-real) — does the prediction-powered correction work on real hand-coded text? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments a naive-Bayes coder (kappa 0.14) is wrong on prevalence (13% against 25%, 0% coverage) and overstates a length gap six-fold; PPI repairs both but is wider than hand-coding alone. Not an LLM (no key).
- [`research/qual-hypothesis`](qual-hypothesis) — do hypotheses about what separates two sets of documents survive a held-out check? On 5,313 GoEmotions comments a word-counting proposer's effects are 7.6× too large at 100 discovery comments (1.5× at 2,000); a BH check on 3,000 held-out comments confirms almost nothing with shuffled labels, but 36–59% of confirmed hypotheses are the label's own vocabulary. Not an LLM proposer.
- [`research/codesign-diagnose`](codesign-diagnose) — does a failure summary or full rollout logs help a gripper design loop beyond the score alone? No: three scripted readers end at 0.84 to 0.86 best test success, like random search, CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation (stylised, rule-based readers, not an LLM).

- [`research/marriage-divorce-evidence`](marriage-divorce-evidence) — original U.S. marriage microdata: interpretable annual-divorce models, schooling/earnings pairings and reproducible held-out validation; no validated personal optimum.
- [`research/codesign-ceiling`](codesign-ceiling) — which grasp scenes can no printable two-finger gripper hold? 10 of 140 (7%), all spheres with the hand more than about 2 mm above their centre height; lowering only the hand holds all 10, so the 0.85 plateau in `codesign-diagnose` is scene geometry, not friction or force (stylised, found after the fact, one intervention).
- [`research/codesign-headroom`](codesign-headroom) — does diagnostic feedback help a gripper design loop once the scene generator is fixed? Still no: every method gains only 0.00 to 0.05 (best test success 0.85 to 0.90) and the failure-summary and rollout-log readers do not beat random search, CMA-ES or Bayesian optimisation (stylised, rule-based readers, not an LLM).
<!-- projects:start -->
<!-- Generated by platform/ci/research_live_links.py --write from research/<slug>/project.json `lede`. -->
- [`research/accel-cascade`](accel-cascade) — classifier-only energy of two-stage keyword-spotting cascades vs 40 single MLPs retrained on the original Speech Commands v0.02 (5 seeds, 200 models, about 41,000 cascades per seed, thresholds chosen on validation): cascades cut modelled energy by 0 to 28% at the same validation target, losing 0.3 to 1.4 test points, and the saving is under 1% of a system whose MFCC front end costs about 50 times the classifier
- [`research/accel-frontend`](accel-frontend) — an instrumented Q15 MFCC front end (512-point FFT, 40 mel bands, DCT) costs 7,532 multiplies per 20 ms frame against the 5,184-MAC `accel-kws` classifier per inference; under assumed 45 nm constants the front end is 8 to 150 times the classifier's power, and 16-bit FFT scaling decides its accuracy (synthetic frames, no Speech Commands)
- [`research/accel-frontendbfp`](accel-frontendbfp) — a shared data-chosen exponent for the 16-bit FFT in `accel-frontend`'s MFCC path cuts cepstral error to 0.016 to 0.081 of the spread at -60 to -30 dBFS (one shift per stage: 0.35 to 0.72) for about +0.5% modelled energy, but no better than 18 to 20-bit fixed schedules on loud input (synthetic frames, no Speech Commands)
- [`research/accel-horowitzcheck`](accel-horowitzcheck) — all 34 energy constants in the accel-* projects that have a counterpart in Horowitz's Fig. 1.1.9 are within 4% of it; re-costing accel-kws with the figure's array-size law (energy about the square root of size) moves energy to 0.23 to 0.81 of as built but keeps 17 of 18 front designs (Spearman 0.991)
- [`research/accel-lutsize`](accel-lutsize) — pricing every array by its own size flips accel-lut's negative result once read energy grows faster than size^0.26 (dense) or size^0.28 (pruned); Horowitz's slope is 0.5 above 8 KB but there is no point below it, so the verdict is conditional
- [`research/accel-pdkperiod`](accel-pdkperiod) — OpenRAM's clk0 figure is an average power in mW over one cycle of an unstated period, not pJ: the unpacked verdict (register-file WS wins) holds for any period above 3 ns, while the packed verdict flips between 1 and 31 ns, so accel-pdkprice's packed conclusion is conditional
- [`research/accel-sysmodel`](accel-sysmodel) — joining accel-cascade's committed results with accel-frontend's 46.8 nJ per frame: cascades' classifier-only saving of up to 28% becomes at most 0.42% of whole-system energy, while the most accurate model (about 0.75 test accuracy) costs 23 to 26 times the cheapest classifier but only 5% more system energy (modelled, not measured)
- [`research/accel-ttbig`](accel-ttbig) — Synthesising the streaming keyword-spotting engine at 96, 128, 192 and 256 hidden units (SKY130 HD, Yosys): area grows about 250 µm² per unit, so H = 128 (0.752 accuracy, 4-bit) needs a 2x2 tile (75,603 µm²) against a 1x2 (36,347 µm²) for H = 64 (0.737): +0.015 accuracy for twice the tile; H = 256 needs 4x2 at 50% utilisation, and its 8-bit layer-2 accumulator overflows 20 bits on a random model.
- [`research/accel-ttsizes`](accel-ttsizes) — Tiny Tapeout's real SKY130 tile sizes (1x1 is 161 × 111.52 µm = 17,955 µm², 0.43% below the value assumed earlier) applied to the synthesised keyword-spotting engine and the retrained models: the best 1x1 model is unchanged at 100% and 50% utilisation and flips at 70% (H = 16, 4-bit, 0.596 instead of 8-bit, 0.616, a 29 µm² miss); H = 64 with 8-bit weights (0.737) fits a 1x2 tile.
- [`research/aerolog-scaling`](aerolog-scaling) — research programme (proposal stage): per-quantity error budget for a Froude-scaled cargo aircraft model, with a stdlib scaling calculator; first-order result: at 1:10 the model needs 17 to 35% more power than Froude scaling predicts
- [`research/build-data`](build-data) — research programme: open permit feeds from Chicago, San Francisco and Austin loaded onto one schema; median permit issue lag is 0 days in all three, so duration targets are zero-inflated; no model run yet
- [`research/buildbot-carry`](buildbot-carry) — can a stock humanoid carry a stud faster than a labourer? A MuJoCo single-support step envelope: the G1 steps 0.50 m with one 2x4 and cannot carry two; the H1 steps 0.20 m with one and 0.10 m with 15 kg; neither beats a labourer who takes a bundle of four, and the model is restrictive (no falls, drops or energy scored).
- [`research/buildbot-carrycop`](buildbot-carrycop) — does moving the planned centre of pressure along the sole raise a humanoid's carry speed? Not by a constant offset: the G1's 4 kg result moves one grid cell (0.10 to 0.13 m/s) and joint torques, not the sole, set the limit.
- [`research/buildbot-carryphase`](buildbot-carryphase) — can a stock Unitree G1 or H1 carry a stud as fast as a labourer? A pendulum gait with no trunk compensation gets the G1 to 0.25 m/s unloaded and 0.10 m/s at 4 kg; a robot with one piece per trip would need 4-13 m/s to match a labourer carrying three.
- [`research/buildbot-carryramp`](buildbot-carryramp) — does a heel-to-toe sweep of the planned centre of pressure raise a humanoid's carry speed? No: one grid cell for the G1 at 4 kg and the unloaded H1, and nothing for heavier loads, because joint torques set the limit.
- [`research/buildbot-cohold`](buildbot-cohold) — can a stock Unitree G1 or H1 hold one end of a sheet while a person lifts the other? For a 22 kg sheet the person carries 11.1-11.5 kg-equivalent in a 2-4 s lift; the G1 manages up to 8 kg, the H1 a 23 kg drywall sheet; lag sets the tilt (2-6 degrees at 0.2-0.5 s).
- [`research/buildbot-cost`](buildbot-cost) — does a stock humanoid pay for itself on a house site? The break-even slowdown against a $38/h loaded carpenter is about 2.4 for a $16k Unitree G1 and 1.1 for a $90k H1; over a sweep the G1 saves money in 15% of draws, the H1 in 0.4%, and a perfect H1 would remove 0.19% of an average home's price on the taskbook scope (inputs swept, not measured).
- [`research/buildbot-custom`](buildbot-custom) — does a purpose-built nailing gantry beat a stock humanoid at fastening sheathing? A modelled two-axis bridge nails a 62-nail sheet in 61 s (6.1x hand nailing); a $100k machine needs about 150 houses a year to break even on walls (50 with roof cassettes), where the H1 saves money in 0.4% of draws (price and times swept, not sourced).
- [`research/buildbot-fasten`](buildbot-fasten) — can a stock Unitree G1 or H1 push a nailer into a wall and place nails inside a stud face? One 4x8 sheet is 62 nails, 34 with a +-3.9 mm window; the tip needs 1.24 mm scatter for a 95% sheet. The G1 pushes up to 142 N and reaches 18 of 62 nails from one stance, the H1 up to 276 N and 36 of 62 (static, peak torque, no policy).
- [`research/buildbot-flatsheet`](buildbot-flatsheet) — can a G1 humanoid nail a 4x8 sheet lying flat on a table? Not better than on a wall: pushing down at 50 N with its feet planted it holds only 30 of 693 reach cells, reaching 48 of the sheet's 62 nails in 16 stances (13 stances for all 62 on a wall), and matching hand nailing (372 s) only if each re-stance takes under 23-24 s (push, standoff and re-stance time are swept, not sourced; static IK, no walking).
- [`research/buildbot-grounded`](buildbot-grounded) — with simulated carry and nailing results in place of a swept slowdown, does a stock humanoid pay on a house site? The H1's carry slowdown is 0.41 to 1.70 against a one-stud labourer (pays in 72% of cells) and 1.63 to 6.82 against a bundle carrier (never pays), against a break-even of 1.07; the dollars are tens per house.
- [`research/buildbot-holdheat`](buildbot-holdheat) — can a stock humanoid hold a stud for a minute? G1 static joint torques re-read against a swept continuous-torque fraction: the waist is at 0.54 of peak with empty hands at the carry pose and 0.91 with a 3.8 kg stud, so below a 0.6 continuous ratio nothing is sustainable (0.6 kg at 0.6) and a stud reaches the winding limit in 0.14 thermal time constants at 1/3. A 150-pose search finds a posture (hands 0.15 m ahead, 0.7 m high) at 0.33 of peak, so the 0.91 is not the floor.
- [`research/buildbot-holdpose`](buildbot-holdpose) — does a searched arm and waist posture let a G1 hold a stud longer? In MuJoCo the stud's peak joint fraction falls from 0.91 (default) to 0.76 with the centre of pressure on the sole point and to 0.49 (best of three noisy seeds) 3 cm inside the toe edge, enough to make a stud sustainable at a continuous rating of 0.5 of peak; a 2x6 goes 0.93 to 0.68 and sheets stay above peak.
- [`research/buildbot-mjsheet`](buildbot-mjsheet) — does the hand-written flexing-sheet plant of buildbot-skills agree with MuJoCo? Pass/fail labels agree on 99.8% of 2600 episodes across 13 policies, so the sim-to-sim transfer failure (0-11% vs 81% where tuned) is not an artefact of my equations.
- [`research/buildbot-mobilebase`](buildbot-mobilebase) — does a G1 on a drive-and-lift base nail a 4x8 sheet faster than a walking G1? Yes in 22 of 24 speed settings: 54-121 s a sheet (13 stances, 12 moves of 5-63 s) against 85-119 s walking and 372 s by hand, and it matches a 61 s gantry only with a fast tip, 0.5 m/s drive and 0.2 m/s lift (speeds swept, not a chosen machine; reach unchanged from static statics).
- [`research/buildbot-nailpay`](buildbot-nailpay) — how slow must a person be at nailing a sheet of sheathing for a Unitree G1 nailing it to pay? A walking G1 (85 s a sheet) pays above 35 s of person time at 0.3 supervision, a base-mounted one above 26 to 76 s; about $110 a house, and the person's time per sheet is the unsourced unknown.
- [`research/buildbot-nailseq`](buildbot-nailseq) — does a nailer's recoil slow a G1 down when it fires a whole 62-nail sheet in sequence? Not by much: the arm is settled and back on the wall within 0.31 s at 4 N s of recoil, shorter than the 0.16 s moves only for a fast tip, so a sheet loses 0 s (2.3-2.5 s at 10 m/s2 and 4 N s) and the clean-sheet probability does not move; the stud finder still sets the budget (recoil, loop, tip speed and wall are swept, not sourced).
- [`research/buildbot-payload`](buildbot-payload) — can a stock humanoid carry building materials? MuJoCo static payload envelopes of the Unitree G1 (median 4.5 kg, waist-limited; matches Unitree's 2–3 kg rating) and H1 (median 14.2 kg, elbow-limited): the G1 can carry a 2x4 but not a 2x6, the H1 can take one end of an OSB or drywall sheet with a person and set a lightweight block, but not a whole sheet, a normal-weight block or a 60 lb bag, and neither reaches an 8 ft ceiling.
- [`research/buildbot-recoil`](buildbot-recoil) — does a framing nailer's recoil knock a stock Unitree G1 arm off the stud window? In a closed-loop MuJoCo hold, up to 4 N s of recoil moves the tip 1-4 mm (window +-3.9 mm) but 1 N s or more lifts the nosepiece off the wall for 25-140 ms; stiff loops go unstable with 10-20 ms delay (recoil, delay and wall are swept, not sourced).
- [`research/buildbot-screwline`](buildbot-screwline) — how well must a drywall-screwing robot find the framing? A 4x8 sheet (28 wall screws, 36 ceiling) needs one read per member good to 1.0 mm for 95% clean sheets, nearly the nailing figure; allowing 3 missed screws gives 1.7 mm, averaging 4 reads 2.1 mm.
- [`research/buildbot-shift`](buildbot-shift) — how many hours of an 8-hour shift can a battery-powered Unitree G1 work? One 421 Wh pack gives 4.7 h; two packs and one charger give 7.7 h; above about 300 W the charger is the limit, and pack logistics move the cost break-even only from 2.42 to 2.27 to 2.46 (draw swept, not measured).
- [`research/buildbot-skillpack`](buildbot-skillpack) — can the building-site robot skills ship as loadable files? Five JSON skill files reload and re-score exactly; a follow skill tuned on the worse of two plants passes 68%, 65% and 70% on the rigid, flexing and MuJoCo plants, no better than tuning on the flexing plant alone (72%, 69%, 71%).
- [`research/buildbot-skills`](buildbot-skills) — does a sheet-following skill tuned on a rigid-sheet simulator survive a flexing sheet with delay? It passes 81% of episodes where it was tuned and 0-11% on the held-out plant; tuning on the held-out plant gives 61-66% there.
- [`research/buildbot-stance`](buildbot-stance) — how many times must a G1 humanoid re-stand to nail one 4x8 sheet? About 13 stances at 5 platform heights: with its feet planted it holds a 3.4 kg nailer at 50 N only 0.7-1.5 m high and 0.3 m sideways, so one stance covers at best 6 of 62 nails, and the sheet matches hand nailing (372 s) only if each re-stance takes under 26-29 s (push to seat and re-stance time are swept, not sourced; no walking simulated).
- [`research/buildbot-studfind`](buildbot-studfind) — how well must a nailing robot find the studs? A 4x8 sheet needs a single stud reading good to 0.89 mm (1 sigma) for 95% clean sheets; a stud finder's 'within 1/8 in' claim gives 28% (8 of 62 nails missed) or 69%; averaging reads and pooling a straight wall help, re-measuring each nail is worst.
- [`research/buildbot-taskbook`](buildbot-taskbook) — which house-building tasks hold the crew hours, and which can a stock humanoid hold the load for? An open task book of a 28x40 ft wood-frame house: 20 tasks with cycles, masses, tolerances and crew-hours, scored against the simulated Unitree G1 and H1 payload envelopes; fastening is 31% of the hours, and the H1 passes the payload test for 53-81% of them (minutes per unit are assumptions, swept).
- [`research/buildbot-torquewall`](buildbot-torquewall) — which joint's torque limit caps a stock humanoid's carrying? For the G1 it is the waist pitch: doubling it alone takes the heaviest feasible payload from 4 to 6 kg; the H1 has no single bottleneck.
- [`research/capture-aliaswin`](capture-aliaswin) — does a longer window or a delay prior fix GCC sync's aliasing on real clips? Window length (shared audio) is the lever: 97% wrong at 1 s, 38% at 2 s, 16% at 3 s on the failing clips; a tone clip needs a prior tighter than its period.
- [`research/capture-audiosync`](capture-audiosync) — how long a window of sound syncs two drifting cameras? GCC-PHAT plus a robust line fit syncs to under 0.02 ms over an hour from -10 dB SNR with 2 s windows; a sync made once at the start drifts one frame in 833 s at 40 ppm.
- [`research/capture-detectors`](capture-detectors) — how small can a face be before a real detector misses it? YuNet found every face of 16 px or wider and half at 10 px (assumed: 20), cutting the modelled blur leak on an 8K 360 camera from 4.8% to 0.01%; YOLOX finds half of people at 23 px tall. A pilot on 22 easy faces in 128 COCO photos: an optimistic bound.
- [`research/capture-followplan`](capture-followplan) — does a predictive planner recover the framing a follow camera loses by avoiding pedestrians? Not at equal settings (drone 58% framed vs 68% for repulsion at 0.1 people/m²), but it halves-to-tenths the contacts (36 vs 161 per hour; wheeled 21 vs 326) and moves the framing/contacts front left. 2-D simulation, assumed platform figures.
- [`research/capture-followtrack`](capture-followtrack) — how much of a predictive follow camera's contact reduction survives noisy, delayed pedestrian detections? Framing is unchanged (57-61%) but a drone's contacts rise from 36 per hour with exact data to 120 at 0.3 m noise and 0.25 s delay, and 373 at 1 m and 1 s (ignoring pedestrians: 523). 2-D simulation, swept noise, perfect association assumed.
- [`research/capture-kitstore`](capture-kitstore) — what does it cost a travel filming kit to carry its footage backup? Counting storage costs 3 of 18 shots at 4 kg; the lightest kit reaching all 18 grows from 3,900 g to 4,125 g.
- [`research/capture-learngate`](capture-learngate) — can a small model trained on many random scenes pick the per-pixel blend weight better than a hand-set ramp? A one-hidden-layer network beats the best soft gate by 0.57 dB on 40 held-out scenes (all 40 ahead) and recovers 0.36 of the headroom to the best per-pixel blend.
- [`research/capture-realaudio`](capture-realaudio) — does the synthetic audio-sync threshold hold on real sound? No: on 10 real clips 2 s windows fail 53% of the time at -10 dB SNR (0% synthetic), and partial whitening instead of PHAT halves that.
- [`research/capture-realedit`](capture-realedit) — does an automatic multi-camera edit survive sync errors measured on real clips? Not as on synthetic audio: 34% of cuts jump over a frame at 0 dB SNR (64% at -10 dB) against none, depending on the clip; partial whitening cuts the 0 dB figure to 1.4%.
- [`research/capture-realkit`](capture-realkit) — what does the capture-kit packing result look like with real, sourced devices (Insta360 X4, DJI Osmo Action 4, iPhone 15 Pro Max, Sony a7C II and lenses, Anker packs)? Exact search reaches all 18 shots at 4 kg, greedy stalls at 12; the unsourced mount masses move the exact figure by up to 10 shots.
- [`research/capture-residgate`](capture-residgate) — does a clip-free residual-from-the-clock-line trim beat the PSR gate? At 0 dB a little (1.4% to 0.9% of cuts over a frame); at -10 dB no (46% vs 38% for PSR).
- [`research/capture-reverbsync`](capture-reverbsync) — in a reverberant room, does PHAT win after all? No: across 10 simulated rooms PHAT is the worst fixed weighting; gamma 0.5 cuts failures from 0.26 to 0.09, and choosing gamma per window by peak-to-sidelobe ratio helps less than just fixing it.
- [`research/capture-syncbudget`](capture-syncbudget) — how often must free-running cameras be re-synced by audio? At 40 ppm a sync lasts 1000 s for a 40 ms tolerance; a drift gate on fresh estimates helps only if you re-sync every 300 s. Synthetic audio.
- [`research/capture-syncedit`](capture-syncedit) — does a residual audio-sync error show at the cuts of an automatic edit? A sync made once leaves 50% of cuts jumping over a frame; a robust fit leaves none down to -10 dB SNR (2 s windows) and then fails outright, with 11 to 24% of cuts repeating or dropping a loud event.
- [`research/capture-syncfilter`](capture-syncfilter) — does a robust fit or a Kalman filter hold a camera clock better between audio re-syncs? On a clock rate computed from a sourced AT-cut crystal curve the plain offset hold is already fine up to 1800 s and every fit ties; only a Kalman filter tuned to an assumed 30 ppm wobble wins. Synthetic audio.
- [`research/capture-syncfine`](capture-syncfine) — does a coarse offset first let short windows sync drifting cameras? A 10 s window finds the offset, then 0.5 s windows hold within a frame from -12 dB SNR; plain 1 s windows fail to -5 dB. Synthetic audio.
- [`research/capture-syncgate`](capture-syncgate) — does a peak-sharpness gate plus settings chosen on other clips keep the edit in sync? At 0 dB yes (1.9% of cuts over a frame held-out vs 34% for PHAT); at -10 dB only partly (39% vs 64%).
- [`research/capture-syncslope`](capture-syncslope) — does holding a fitted clock slope between audio re-syncs help? At 40 ppm it cuts out-of-tolerance time from 44% to 3.7% at 1800 s intervals, but an assumed rate wobble makes a strict line gate lock out. Synthetic audio; also corrects capture-syncbudget.
- [`research/capture-trackprior`](capture-trackprior) — can a delay prior from a camera's own earlier windows replace the oracle? Not yet: 22.5% to 37% wrong on the failing clips against 0% for the oracle, and an early alias becomes an hour-long lock-in.
- [`research/capture-whichwrong`](capture-whichwrong) — does telling the blend model which of two sources is wrong help, or does it need to choose between them? New occlusion and cross-check features add 0.03 dB; a per-pixel source weight adds 0.47 dB (40 of 40 test configs), recovering 0.54 of the headroom to the best blend.
- [`research/chip-digits`](chip-digits) — weight and activation bit-width sweep of a 64-16-10 quantised MLP on the scikit-learn digits data (5 splits), with the MAC lane's gate count from Yosys and its RTL checked against NumPy: 4-bit weights and activations lose about 2 points of accuracy, 5 and 5 about 0.5, below 3 bits it collapses.
- [`research/chip-digits-pot`](chip-digits-pot) — Giving each neuron of the digits MLP its own power-of-two weight scale turns the per-channel rescale into one or two shift stages and keeps the lane integer-exact: at W2A4 the MSE-chosen exponents reach 70.5% (against 62% for per-tensor clipping), but at W3A4 they give 87.9%, below clipping's 90.8%.
- [`research/chip-digits-qat`](chip-digits-qat) — MSE-optimal weight clipping lifts the 3-bit-weight digits MLP from 81% to 91% test accuracy at the same MAC-lane hardware and integer-exact; per-channel scales and QAT help at 4 bits (95.7% at W4A4, within 1.1 points of float) but QAT fails at 2 bits.
- [`research/civ-levers`](civ-levers) — which interventions bring a polarized society back together? In an opinion-dynamics model with backfire swept from none to strong, bridging ties across camps never merges them and at a rejection threshold of 0.7 rewiring 10% of ties drives everyone to the extremes, while a ladder of committed voices within reach merges them in 24 of 25 settings (moderate broadcast 19, nudge 14, tolerance 8, bridging 2); interactive simulator included (tolerance and backfire are swept, not measured).
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant`](codesign-aero-cant) — does a rotor cant angle, co-designed with arm length and propeller size, beat the yaw limit that control-law changes could not move? In simulation yes: joint co-design with free cant (chosen at the 35° bound in 5 of 5 seeds) cuts held-out attitude error from 15.22° to 12.32°, at equal evaluations.
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant-bound`](codesign-aero-cant-bound) — does more rotor tilt keep helping once the 35° cap of codesign-aero-cant is lifted? In simulation no: with the bound at 35°, 50°, 65° and 80° the joint co-design reaches 12.3° held-out attitude error and picks 34–40° each time, so the optimum is interior and flat; an assumed tilt efficiency loss changes it by 0.1°.
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant-kappa`](codesign-aero-cant-kappa) — does the interior cant optimum survive a reaction torque that grows with propeller diameter (κ = c·D) instead of a fixed 0.016 m? In simulation yes: for c = 0.06, 0.126 and 0.25 the joint co-design reaches 12.5°, 12.3° and 11.8° held-out attitude error with the bound at 80°, picks 33–41° each time, and cant still beats no cant in 5 of 5 seeds; the gain is largest (−4.8°) for the weakest reaction torque.
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant-poshold`](codesign-aero-cant-poshold) — once the vehicle must hold position, does a canted-rotor quadrotor still win? In simulation cant still buys yaw (−6° RMS, 5 of 5 seeds) but not position, and the sideways force a tilted rotor makes under roll/pitch torque breaks designs tuned without it (1.1 m against 0.25 m position error).
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant-propdata`](codesign-aero-cant-propdata) — what is the reaction-torque-to-thrust ratio of real small propellers, and does the best cant angle survive it? Measured static data for 77 propellers of 3.15–9.85 in give c = CP/(2π·CT) = 0.065–0.13 (median 0.081); in simulation cant still beats no cant in 5 of 5 seeds at every c, by 2.9–4.7°, and the joint design picks 36–41°.
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant-twr`](codesign-aero-cant-twr) — does rotor cant still help once motor headroom is a design variable? With thrust-to-weight free in 1.5–4 (no cant picks the 4.0 ceiling), cant to 35° still beats no cant in 5 of 5 seeds at each of three measured propeller c values, by 1.6–3.4°; headroom alone recovers 2.5–3.5° of the 3.8–4.7° the parent credited to cant.
- [`research/codesign-aero-cant-twrceil`](codesign-aero-cant-twrceil) — does raising the TWR ceiling from 4 to 6 and 8 erase the cant advantage? No: cant to 35° still beats no cant in 5 of 5 seeds at three measured propeller c values, by 1.5–3.0° at ceiling 8 (1.6–3.4° at 4); the optimiser stops near TWR 5 without cant.
- [`research/codesign-aero-coupled`](codesign-aero-coupled) — does a residual network or analytic gyroscopic compensation beat per-axis PID on a coupled three-axis quadrotor? No: the residual is worse than PID in 26 of 30 pairs (+0.32° held out) and compensation changes the error by 0.001°; yaw authority sets the error.
- [`research/codesign-aero-mlp`](codesign-aero-mlp) — does a 31-parameter neural roll controller beat the three-gain PID on a randomised quadrotor? Not at equal evaluations (7.7° PID against 9.0°); with 10× the evaluations it only matches it (7.6°), and the airframe ranking is unchanged (Spearman 1.00).
- [`research/codesign-aero-skip`](codesign-aero-skip) — does a zero-initialised neural residual on the tuned PID beat the PID on a randomised quadrotor? No at equal evaluations (7.68° against 7.58°, better in 7 of 18 pairs); the airframe ranking is unchanged (Spearman 1.00).
- [`research/codesign-agri-pulloff`](codesign-agri-pulloff) — does the strawberry-gripper co-design of codesign-agri survive measured stem pull-off forces (3.17 N bending and pulling, 13.94 N pulling alone) instead of an assumed 3.5 N? Partly: the optimiser ranking holds in 8 of 10 cells, but the best achievable objective falls from 0.665 to 0.075 as the median pull-off rises from 3.17 to 13.94 N, and at 13.94 N no 40-evaluation run finds a gripper that picks anything
- [`research/codesign-agri-sweep`](codesign-agri-sweep) — does the optimiser ranking of codesign-agri survive a sweep of its 12 invented constants? Mostly: Bayesian optimisation beats CMA-ES beats random search in 82 of 100 worlds, but the best gripper has 2, 3 or 4 fingers depending on stem strength and the nominal winner loses 0.06 J or goes over budget in most worlds
- [`research/codesign-power`](codesign-power) — does the ranking of co-design methods survive 600 test scenes? No: re-scoring the 144 final designs of codesign-headroom gives 0.82 to 0.85 true success for every method, random search ties CMA-ES and Bayesian optimisation, and the rollout-log reader trails by 0.03 (interval includes 0)
- [`research/codesign-seeds`](codesign-seeds) — do more seeds separate the gripper co-design methods? Not yet: with 24 runs per method CMA-ES leads random search by 0.025 (interval -0.007 to +0.056), the rollout-log reader trails by 0.022, and a 0.02 gap would need about 140 runs per method
- [`research/eda-adaptci`](eda-adaptci) — `eda-adaptfixed` re-run with 40 splits per family and bootstrap intervals: for the linear model the spread beats random draws at every k (about 1 point of error at 3 to 8 designs, 2.5 at 2), but for ridge it loses at 3 designs and one family (sorter) reverses the sign, so the pooled interval is narrower than the real uncertainty.
- [`research/eda-adaptdesign`](eda-adaptdesign) — `eda-adaptci` re-run with a design-level bootstrap: the intervals widen by up to 2.5x, the spread's lead holds at 5 and 8 designs for both models (about 0.5 to 1.1 points) and at 2 for the linear model, but the linear k = 3 lead and the ridge k = 2 lead include zero, and the ridge k = 3 loss reported earlier is not established.
- [`research/eda-adaptplaced`](eda-adaptplaced) — the eda-adapt few-shot curves refitted to OpenROAD-placed area on 133 SKY130 designs: an unseen family's placed area is off by 39% (ridge), one placed design cuts it to 23%, about three reach 20%, as for cell counts.
- [`research/eda-biasdraw`](eda-biasdraw) — Four biased generators (20,000 vectors per target) catch none of the 26 eda-deepdraw survivors, so none contradicts a formal equivalence proof, and the del@122 mutant is confirmed as truncated output (90 rows for 100 vectors).
- [`research/eda-clocksweep`](eda-clocksweep) — the 40 MAC-engine designs mapped to SKY130 at six shared clock targets: cell count still ranks area (Spearman 0.995+), logic depth ranks delay at only 0.34 to 0.82, and with one shared clock the Pareto front equals the generic one, so the earlier front change came from per-design clocks.
- [`research/eda-delayproxy`](eda-delayproxy) — library-free delay proxies on the generic gate netlist of the 40 MAC engines: gate-type weights leave depth's Spearman 0.611 against SKY130-mapped delay unchanged (0.609), adding fan-out lifts it to 0.83 to 0.90, but gate count alone reaches 0.911, and no proxy recovers the true (area, latency) front.
- [`research/eda-goldenfree`](eda-goldenfree) — five golden-free properties (latency, done pulse, output hold, start-glitch and reset-abort invariance) kill 22 of the 66 killable eda-verify mutants, all control-flow bugs, and none of the 15 ROM or other datapath bugs; the 16 directed vectors catch all 22 too.
- [`research/eda-latencyhalve`](eda-latencyhalve) — a generic-gate cells &times; cycles screen puts the best SKY130 area &times; latency design first among 40 MAC engines; over random pools of 20, mapping 3 designs finds the pool's best 97% of the time, against 15% for random choice.
- [`research/eda-postplace`](eda-postplace) — the 40 MAC engines placed with the pip OpenROAD wheel: ABC area still ranks placed area (Spearman 0.997+), ABC delay only 0.61 to 0.80, wires add a median 36% to the period, and no design ABC calls feasible at 5 ns meets it after placement.
- [`research/eda-rankpayoff`](eda-rankpayoff) — across 207 cheap screens of 40 MAC engines, Spearman agreement with SKY130-mapped latency does not predict top-k screening payoff (&minus;0.13 at k = 1): the highest-Spearman screen (0.958) maps the best design first 32% of the time, gate count (0.551) 69%; for area &times; latency it does (0.60).
- [`research/eda-rungview`](eda-rungview) — searching generic-gate objectives instead of SKY130-mapped ones (RMS error 0.036 on area, 0.123 on latency) costs within 0.01 of hypervolume on the 40 MAC-engine designs, and TPE's +0.037 lead over random at budget 20 is unchanged.
- [`research/eda-screenplaced`](eda-screenplaced) — a screen with cost models trained on other families finds the placed (OpenROAD, SKY130) area-period front in 3 to 6 placements where random reaches 0.92 only at 16, but the true fronts hold only 1 to 2 designs, so the test is easy.
- [`research/eda-sky130rung`](eda-sky130rung) — the 40 MAC-engine designs mapped to the SKY130 HD cell library: generic cell count ranks mapped area at Spearman 0.995 but logic depth ranks mapped delay at only 0.611, so the (area, cycles) Pareto front is unchanged while the (area, latency) front loses one design and gains another.
- [`research/eda-smtbmc`](eda-smtbmc) — z3 bounded model checking via yosys-smtbmc decides none of the 10 undecided eda-deepdraw survivors at the 24-cycle depth a full inference needs; its cost passes from about 3 s at depth 10 to over 240 s at depth 12.
- [`research/eda-systolicclock`](eda-systolicclock) — the 24 systolic-array and MAC-lane designs of chip-systolic mapped to SKY130 at six shared clocks: the MAC-lane engine keeps the best area x latency at every feasible target (arrays 1.61 to 1.66x worse), though the clock removes many arrays and changes the Pareto front.
- [`research/energy-cell-dfn`](energy-cell-dfn) — re-scoring 49 designs from energy-cell under PyBaMM's full DFN model instead of SPMe: rank correlation 0.86 at cycle 100, the SPMe winner is 3rd of 45 and still +38% over the reference cell (+31% under SPMe), DFN reports about 19 Wh/kg more on average, and SPMe fails to cycle 3 designs DFN handles (modelled, stack level)
- [`research/energy-lcos-realised`](energy-lcos-realised) — how hard does a least-cost zero-emission German grid cycle its storage? Batteries about 1,700&ndash;1,900 h a year (near the assumed 2,000), cavern hydrogen only 719 h, which more than doubles its LCOS to $864/MWh.
- [`research/energy-lcos-sourced`](energy-lcos-sourced) — which storage technology is cheapest at each duration once costs are sourced (NREL ATB 2024, PyPSA technology-data)? Li-ion to 4 h, cavern compressed air from 6 h; at 8 h pumped hydro 52%, air 27%, Li-ion 22% of scenarios; hydrogen's assumed 500 h win does not survive.
- [`research/energy-mix-sourced`](energy-mix-sourced) — what does a zero-emission German power system cost once `energy-mix`'s assumed costs are replaced by sourced ones? PyPSA on 2019 hourly demand with PyPSA technology-data costs (Danish Energy Agency et al.): EUR 222/MWh with a steel hydrogen tank, EUR 144 with caverns, EUR 75 unconstrained; the storage type moves the answer more than the cost year.
- [`research/energy-mix-years`](energy-mix-years) — which weather year sets the hydrogen store of a zero-emission German power system? The same sourced-cost PyPSA model on 2015 to 2019: EUR 203 to 271/MWh with a steel tank, EUR 141 to 155 with caverns, and a cavern store of 24 to 36 TWh depending on the year.
- [`research/energy-price-residual`](energy-price-residual) — does residual load explain German day-ahead prices on a year the model never saw? Yes: correlation 0.87 and RMSE 9.6 EUR/MWh on 2019 against 23.0 for the flat merit-order price; the cheap-quarter mean is within EUR 4&ndash;9/MWh; the rest is the fuel-price level.
- [`research/energy-store-cop`](energy-store-cop) — does a weather-dependent heat-pump COP change the sand-store verdict? The table's 3.6 equals a 35 C floor-heating sink; at 45 to 55 C the store's weekly flip multiplier rises 8 to 46%, and 2018 still favours the pump.
- [`research/energy-store-flip`](energy-store-flip) — how far must capex move to flip the sand-store vs heat-pump verdict? Weekly cycling flips on a 2 to 40% store-capex change (the cost table's own vintage step is 26%); daily cycling holds to 2 to 3.5 times.
- [`research/energy-store-hx`](energy-store-hx) — how much can the sand store's heat exchanger cost? Weekly cycling survives about 120 to 270 EUR per kW in four of five price years; daily cycling survives 460 to 900; 2018 never wins.
- [`research/energy-store-modelprice`](energy-store-modelprice) — does the sand-store vs heat-pump verdict survive a modelled price? Mostly: 32 of 40 cells agree with real prices (21 for the flat merit-order price), but weekly cycling, whose margin is under EUR 0.011/kWh, is wrong in sign in 5 of 5 years; as a charge scheduler the model costs under EUR 0.0011/kWh.
- [`research/energy-thermal-cost`](energy-thermal-cost) — how much cheaper must electricity be for a sand heat store to beat a heat pump? Sourced costs (PyPSA technology-data) on the `energy-thermal` model: a daily-cycled store needs the pump's electricity about 2 times dearer, weekly 5, monthly 16, seasonal 120 (house); the heat lost in standby, not the sand, rules out seasonal storage.
- [`research/energy-thermal-hourly`](energy-thermal-hourly) — when does a sand heat store beat a heat pump on hourly prices? With 189 GW solar and 137 GW wind and a modelled German price series, a daily or weekly store wins in all 5 years (EUR 0.027/kWh against 0.071); a levy of 0.10/kWh or a surplus-hour price above about 20&ndash;35 EUR/MWh removes the win.
- [`research/energy-thermal-real`](energy-thermal-real) — on real German day-ahead prices, does a sand heat store beat a heat pump? Yes at wholesale: daily cycling wins in all 5 years (EUR 0.045&ndash;0.062/kWh against 0.070&ndash;0.074) and weekly in 4; the earlier merit-order model was flat at EUR 54.6/MWh and wrong; a EUR 0.10/kWh levy removes the win.
- [`research/energy-tou-lp`](energy-tou-lp) — how much is a home battery worth on a time-of-use tariff with optimal dispatch? An LP breaks even at $867/kWh for 10 kWh behind 6 kW PV, against $674 for the best fixed rule ($742 with a 4,000-cycle life); a rolling 36 h plan matches the year optimum (`energy-tou` with an optimiser).
- [`research/energy-tou-lp-forecast`](energy-tou-lp-forecast) — how much of an optimal home-battery controller's gain survives a realistic forecast? With yesterday's weather or a 7-day mean as the forecast, a rolling LP saves $726 or $691 a year against the fixed rule's $694 (perfect forecast: $892); 84&ndash;101% of the gain is lost (`energy-tou-lp` with forecasts).
- [`research/energy-tou-lp-replan`](energy-tou-lp-replan) — how much of the home-battery optimiser's forecast loss does re-planning every few hours recover? On PVGIS typical-year data for three US sites, re-planning the LP every 3&ndash;12 h instead of daily adds $10&ndash;36 a year and closes at most 17 points of the gap to a perfect forecast (10 kWh, assumed tariff); `energy-tou-lp-tmy` with receding-horizon re-planning.
- [`research/energy-tou-lp-tmy`](energy-tou-lp-tmy) — does a clear-sky-index forecast rescue the optimal home-battery controller on real weather? On PVGIS typical-year data for three US sites, a rolling LP fed yesterday's weather or a clear-sky-index forecast keeps 25&ndash;55% of the perfect-forecast gain over the fixed rule at Phoenix and Seattle and none of it at Golden (10 kWh, assumed tariff); `energy-tou-lp-forecast` on measured-derived irradiance.
- [`research/free-buckreal`](free-buckreal) — does a physics-shaped twin of a real buck converter beat plain ARX? Only partly: all models sit at 0.5 to 0.9 V free-run error against a 0.06 V quantisation floor, and the winner flips with which half of the 20 ms record is fitted. One 20 ms record; natural frequency fitted, no component values.
- [`research/free-delaymargin`](free-delaymargin) — exact: a fixed-step twin under-reports a PD loop's delay margin by 18.6% at dt = 0.1 s and 1.0% at 5 ms, because the largest stable delay is the largest n with (n + 1/2) dt below the true margin (25 of 25 cases)
- [`research/free-integperiod`](free-integperiod) — how wrong is a simulator's pendulum period at its default timestep? Exact elliptic-integral period against Euler, semi-implicit Euler, Verlet and RK4: Euler fails (14% period error at 2 ms), the symplectic pair is 0.75 ppm, and releasing from rest hides the symplectic error.
- [`research/free-keplerdrift`](free-keplerdrift) — how many fixed steps per orbit does a twin need before an eccentric orbit stops drifting? Against the exact Kepler solution RK4 needs 64 to 4096 steps per orbit (e = 0 to 0.9), the symplectic pair 8 to 16 times more, and at e = 0.9 every fixed step fails.
- [`research/free-lorenzhorizon`](free-lorenzhorizon) — how long does a digital twin of a chaotic system stay accurate? On Lorenz-63 each e-fold of smaller state error buys 1.12 time units (1/lambda = 1.11); a parameter error buys 0.85. Mean over 40 starts, one tolerance.
- [`research/free-missedpd`](free-missedpd) — how many missed samples can a PD loop tuned on a fixed-period twin survive? With samples lost independently and the last command held, a double-integrator loop with twin poles at 0.5 is mean-square stable only up to a 29% loss rate but almost surely stable up to 61%; a deadbeat twin tolerates 7% in mean square.
- [`research/free-modelcost`](free-modelcost) — does an Opus agent run cost more than a Sonnet run on the same kind of work? Matched on date and track, 6.3× the cost (CI 4.7–8.0), 3.2× the tool calls and 2.0× per call; 1.4× per self-estimated human hour. 46 Opus vs 958 Sonnet runs, preliminary.
- [`research/free-pendmiss`](free-pendmiss) — on a linearised pendulum (L = 0.5 m) a fixed twin-tuned gain survives any miss probability with slow poles unless the plant is very unstable per sample (limit q = 0.21 at ωh₀ = 1.4), a deadbeat tuning fails by q = 0.03 to 0.10, and a gain recomputed from the actual interval was stable at every q in all 15 cases. Linear, noise-free, simulated.
- [`research/free-pgsstack`](free-pgsstack) — how many solver iterations does a physics twin need to hold a stack of boxes still? A projected Gauss-Seidel solver at 60 Hz needs 16 for 4 boxes, about 128 for 6 and over 2048 for 16 or more, and extra iterations do not reliably help.
- [`research/free-sprtbias`](free-sprtbias) — exact: a twin that fails 10% less often than reality lifts the chance of certifying a 5%-failure policy from 0.049 to 0.087 (fixed test, 1% vs 5%), 20% less often to 0.147, and guarding against a 1.5x optimism costs 2.2 times the rollouts
- [`research/free-sprtcut`](free-sprtcut) — Follow-up to free-sprtcap: re-tuning a capped SPRT's boundaries and final cut so both errors stay at or below 5% exactly costs, for 1% vs 5% (fixed test 181 rollouts), a mean of 107 rollouts at a cap equal to the fixed size and 90 at twice it, against 90 to 95 for the plain capped test that overshoots the error; the fixed test needs 181.
- [`research/free-sprtminimax`](free-sprtminimax) — Follow-up to free-sprtopt: an exact linear programme gives the capped certification test with the smallest worst-case mean over failure rates between p1 and p0; for 1% vs 5% at a cap of 362 it needs 123.8 rollouts at worst against 131.0 for the average-optimal test and 181 for the fixed test, at the price of 3 to 6% more rollouts at the two design rates.
- [`research/free-sprtopt`](free-sprtopt) — Follow-up to free-sprtcut: backward induction gives the optimal capped test, 98.9 rollouts on average for 1% vs 5% at a cap of 181 (proved within 0.2% by a dual bound), against 107.3 for the grid-tuned SPRT and 181 for the fixed test; the tuned SPRT is 3 to 9% above the optimum at every cap tried.
- [`research/free-tankci`](free-tankci) — is the overflow twin's lead over black-box models on a real two-tank rig statistically real? A 0.13 V RMSE gap over ARX 4/4 with a block-bootstrap 95% interval of 0.06 to 0.20 V; the iid bootstrap is about three times too narrow. Test-trace sampling only, not fit variance.
- [`research/free-tankfit`](free-tankfit) — does the overflow twin's lead over ARX survive refitting on a real two-tank rig? A 0.13 V gap in 100% of refits with 87.5% of the training data, but 0.07 V and 84 to 90% of refits with 50 to 75%. Overlapping windows, fixed optimiser start.
- [`research/free-tanktwin`](free-tanktwin) — does a physics digital twin beat black-box models on a real two-tank rig? An 8-parameter overflow twin gets 0.53 V test RMSE against 0.66 V for the best black box (9-parameter ARX); quadratic NARX models diverge in free run. One rig, one split, no confidence interval.
- [`research/hanger-3dradius`](hanger-3dradius) — no: with caps of 10 and 15 cm the whole-sheet error is 1.08 and 1.21 cm against 1.12 cm at 6 cm (8 seeds each, p = 0.77 and 0.57), so the optimum sat on a flat ridge. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-3dseeds`](hanger-3dseeds) — yes, by 0.26 cm on the whole sheet with hanger-fit's bounds (8 seeds each, p = 0.03) and by 0.68 cm once the end-radius and bow limits are widened (p < 0.001), but the 3D-designed hangers have a worse centre line. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-avoid`](hanger-avoid) — does a second laundry-hanging arm lose less if it picks a free garment instead of waiting? At 0.30 m clearance blind pinching recovers 9.5 of its 28 lost garments an hour (151 to 161), aiming about 2 of 10; at 0.45 m nothing. Stylised: clearance and spacing assumed.
- [`research/hanger-avoidpaired`](hanger-avoidpaired) — is a laundry-hanging arm's gain from picking a free garment real? Paired on 2000 loads at 0.30 m: aiming +1.25 an hour (0.60 to 1.92), sweeping +4.4, pinching +10.0; at 0.45 m aiming gains nothing. Stylised: inputs are placeholders.
