- [`research/hanger-foldrate`](hanger-foldrate) — how many dryers can a laundry-hanging arm serve when folding takes as long as published robots take? At SpeedFolding's 90–120 s per fold the learned-rule cell falls from 169 to 58–44 garments an hour; always hanging holds 146. Only the fold time is sourced.
- [`research/hanger-foldsrc`](hanger-foldsrc) — how many dryers can a laundry-hanging cell serve when its fold station runs at published robot speeds? Timed from the SpeedFolding paper (68 to 167 s per garment) rather than an assumed 25 s, one arm and one fold table serve 0.8 to 2.1 dryers instead of 5.1, and with two arms five dryers need 3 to 10 fold tables. Fold times are sourced; every other station time is still assumed.
- [`research/hanger-kes`](hanger-kes) — does "arm 1 cm wider than the half shoulder" hold on four fabrics with published KES-FB bending rigidity (0.0066–0.125) and weight (12.5–24.6 mg/cm²)? Best offset is +1 cm in all 12 cases (with stretch swept 40–800 N/m); heavy suiting at 40 N/m nearly ties with +2 cm. B and weight sourced; stretch swept; tee geometry assumed.
- [`research/hanger-kindlink`](hanger-kindlink) — does it matter that the garment a robot picks is also the garment it decides to hang or fold? Linking them moves a one-arm cell from 147 to 141 garments an hour, but two arms swing from 141 to 250 with an assumed kind-to-archetype map. Stylised.
- [`research/hanger-knit`](hanger-knit) — does the hanger that `hanger-fit`'s CMA-ES designs change for stretchy, heavy knits? The best arm width moves from +0.9 cm past the half shoulder (tee) to +1.5–1.8 cm (EA 40 N/m), but slope, bow and end radius are not pinned down at 150 scores; a tee hanger scores 1.06 cm on a soft double-weight knit against 0.52 cm for one designed for it. Fabric constants swept, not measured.
- [`research/hanger-lookahead`](hanger-lookahead) — should a laundry-hanging arm also steer clear of the other arm's next pick? No: paired on 1000 loads, lookahead loses to avoiding the current pick in 14 of 18 settings and ties in 4, by up to 11 an hour. Stylised: inputs are placeholders.
- [`research/hanger-sharedpile`](hanger-sharedpile) — what does it cost two laundry-hanging arms to empty one dryer pile and wait for each other? Blocking at 0.30 m clearance takes aiming from 201 to 191 garments an hour and blind pinching from 179 to 151; a third arm adds only 3 to 5. Stylised: clearance and spacing assumed.
- [`research/hanger-sweep`](hanger-sweep) — does `hanger-fit`'s "arm 1 cm wider than the half shoulder" survive 162 swept fabrics (bending rigidity 0.003–1, weight ×0.5–2, stretch 40–800 N/m, strip depth 10–30 cm)? Best offset is +1 cm in 151 and +2 cm in 11, all of them stretch 40 N/m; bending rigidity alone moves the best error under 10%. Constants swept, not sourced.
- [`research/hanger-yieldcost`](hanger-yieldcost) — should an arm yield to the other arm's next pick only when the clash would be long? Gating by predicted overlap removes most of lookahead's loss and gains +0.7 to +1.35 garments an hour for pinching at 0.30 m (paired, 1000 loads). Stylised: inputs are placeholders.
- [`research/heavy-hydraulics`](heavy-hydraulics) — research programme (proposal stage): pump-shaft energy of fixed open-centre, load-sensing and ideal independent-metering circuits on an illustrative excavator cycle; over 2,916 swept cases load-sensing uses a median 47% of open-centre energy and metering 80% of load-sensing, an ordering that holds by construction; on 20,000 random cycles in a machine envelope load-sensing is a median 44% of open-centre; no measured cycle, margin or price is sourced yet
- [`research/homebot`](homebot) — a 3D-printable 22-DOF bimanual mobile manipulator (two 7-DOF arms with geared elbows and parallel grippers, head, lift, omni base) on one $15 servo, $627–948 in parts, with a MuJoCo twin generated from the same parameters as the CAD: reach and pick policies trained on the exact twin ignore servo, friction and mass errors but fail at one 40 ms step of latency (reach 99→29%, pick 91→41%); randomisation fixes reaching almost for free but costs picking 14 points; an interference sweep over the printed solids caught an elbow range the twin allowed but the parts could not reach. Simulated only, not yet built.
- [`research/infra-cement`](infra-cement) — Mass, energy and CO2 balance of a tonne of cement: OPC 830 kg CO2, LC3-50 491 (41% lower, 39-43% over every swept input), 50% slag 441; the clinker can fall to 50% and keep class 42.5 only if the OPC reference has 55 MPa of strength, to 54% at 50 MPa.
- [`research/infra-chatter`](infra-chatter) — Zero-order milling stability lobes against semi-discretisation and time-domain simulation for a one-mode tool: exact at slotting, but at 10% up-milling the limit is up to 3.3 times too high at some speeds, and in down-milling the zero-order chart predicts no chatter while the tool goes unstable below 0.9 mm; the best spindle speed raises removal rate 5 to 18 times.
- [`research/infra-dcopf`](infra-dcopf) — DC against AC optimal power flow in pandapower on the IEEE 30-, 39- and 118-bus cases: the DC model's cost is 1 to 3 % below the AC optimum (it ignores losses); on the 118-bus case its dispatch is feasible but costs 0.7 to 1.7 % more than the AC optimum once losses are paid, and on the 39-bus case it breaks voltage limits (15 buses at 0.6 of load) at almost no cost difference (modelled benchmark, not a measured system; tightened-limit runs often had no solution). Feeding the AC losses back into the DC model (a loss-adjusted DC) brings the 118-bus cost estimate to within 0.6 to 1.2 % of the AC optimum, but repairs only 13 to 32 % of the dispatch's excess cost and none of case39's voltage violations.
- [`research/infra-grind`](infra-grind) — Ball-mill population balance (Austin form): at the same 100 um P80 a closed circuit at 278% circulating load uses 0.47 of the open-circuit energy, 1.8 to 3.7 times better across the swept mill and classifier inputs, far above Bond's 1.2 open-circuit factor and a vendor's ~10% for cement; the model's size exponent matches Bond's only at a selection exponent near 0.5.
- [`research/infra-hosting`](infra-hosting) — Stochastic PV hosting capacity of the IEEE 13-node feeder in OpenDSS on 300 random placements: median 1,015 kW at midday minimum load, voltage binds first in 75% of placements; a 2 V lower regulator set-point adds +310 kW, while inverter fixed power factor and volt-var add about nothing once the regulators respond (modelled, not a measured feeder).
- [`research/infra-hosting-feeders`](infra-hosting-feeders) — PV hosting capacity of the IEEE 34- and 123-node feeders in OpenDSS (200 random placements): on the 123-node feeder a 2 V lower regulator set-point adds +790 kW to a 1,160 kW median (every placement better), inverter volt-var +100 kW and fixed power factor +180 kW; the published 34-node case has no headroom at all because its source is set to 1.05 pu (modelled, not measured feeders); over a one-minute day the set-point trades overvoltage for undervoltage while inverter reactive power cuts tap operations about 30 %.
- [`research/infra-hvacmpc`](infra-hvacmpc) — Does MPC beat a thermostat for a heat pump on a time-of-use tariff? On real TMY3 weather with a deliberately wrong model and yesterday's weather as the forecast it pays 10.7% less in Greensboro (4.1% in cold Sand Point), saves more with more thermal mass (6.5% to 25%), and loses the gain at an 8 K forecast error; the envelope is from published values, not a fit to measured data.
- [`research/infra-leakpdd`](infra-leakpdd) — Net3 leak signals under demand-driven and pressure-driven demand (WNTR): identical at 1 to 1.25 times base demand and nearly so at 1.5 times; beyond that both models fail because the pumps cannot meet demand (simulated; one network).
- [`research/infra-n1screen`](infra-n1screen) — DC against AC N-1 screening on IEEE 14-, 30-, 39-, 57- and 118-bus cases (every line and transformer outage, modelled, ratings swept): at 25 % rating headroom the DC screen misses 13 to 40 % of the outages an AC power flow calls insecure; flagging at 0.8 of rating misses none, at the cost of 102 false alarms on case118 (186 outages).
- [`research/infra-netting`](infra-netting) — Does bilateral or multilateral netting, or gridlock resolution, save a payment system liquidity? A simulator fitted to Bech and Soramäki's Danish and Finnish bounds (34.7% and 10.1% of gross value against the published 37.2% and 10.7% for DK) shows reciprocal flows are what make netting work, and resolution cuts delay by 24–46% in the Danish-shaped system but 34–62% in the Finnish-shaped one, where the paper found about 10%.
- [`research/infra-plf`](infra-plf) — Nodal loss penalty factors (AC loss sensitivities) in a DC OPF on the IEEE 30-, 39- and 118-bus cases: on the 118-bus case they recover 23 to 38 % of plain DC's excess cost at 0.6 to 1.0 of load, but the iteration does not converge at 0.8 and above and at 1.1 of load the dispatch is worse than plain DC (modelled benchmark, not a measured system).
- [`research/infra-propulsion`](infra-propulsion) — Freight energy per tonne-km from first principles for truck, unit train, bulk carrier and 747 freighter, then diesel swapped for batteries or hydrogen at the same gross mass and range: at 250 Wh/kg a truck keeps 79% of its payload, a train 93%, a ship 55% and the freighter cannot fly the route; 90% needs ~510, 180, 990 and 5,600 Wh/kg (most inputs assumed and swept).
- [`research/infra-pumpdays`](infra-pumpdays) — Net3 pump schedules billed on PG&E B-19 over a month of varying days: with every day the same the best schedule saves 56 to 67 %, but the lowest feasible peak jumps from 63 kW to the 310 kW booster at only 1.5 % more demand, and one day at +20 % cuts the saving to 44 % (summer) and 19 % (winter, spring) (simulated; one network, list prices).
- [`research/infra-pumpdemand`](infra-pumpdemand) — Net3 pump scheduling billed on PG&E's published B-19 tariff with demand charges at a 15-minute step: the lake-pump schedules that idle the 310 kW booster cut the monthly bill 56 to 67 % (peak 373 to 63 kW), and the demand charge, 60 to 64 % of the shipped bill, does not move the optimal schedule at any size from 0 to 20 times (simulated; one network, list prices).
- [`research/infra-pumpsched`](infra-pumpsched) — Pump scheduling on EPANET's Net3 (WNTR): running the lake pump 21 h from 01:00 cuts pumping energy 52 % at a flat tariff because it idles the booster pump; the time-of-use tariff adds 3 to 16 % of cost at a ratio of 3 or less, and hourly on/off schedules (21,806 simulated, plus a GA) add almost nothing beyond one window once tank drawdown is priced (simulated, assumed tariff shapes).
- [`research/infra-routing`](infra-routing) — Solomon RC208 (100 customers) with a battery: keeping the battery-blind routes and adding charging stops costs 28% more distance at 150 range units, a charge-aware search brings that to 5%; the battery is harmless above 200 units.
- [`research/infra-sortbound`](infra-sortbound) — Follow-up to infra-sortvalue: an exact dynamic-programming bound on the best possible pick schedule (agreeable deadlines make arrival order optimal) shows highest-value-first dispatch reaches 96.5% of the clairvoyant optimum at 6 items/m and 93.9% at 10, FIFO 94.7% and 89.6%, and that the largest headroom (about 10 to 11%) sits at faster belts and slower arms, not at the loads where pick order helped most. A rolling-horizon rule that re-solves the schedule with a 1 s look ahead recovers most of it: 99.7% of the optimum at 6 items/m and 98.0% in the hardest cells.
- [`research/infra-sorting`](infra-sorting) — Single-robot conveyor pick cell fed with the class mix and object sizes of the 4,784 TACO litter annotations: with a 1 s pick and 1 m of reach, recovery of bottles and cans holds near 0.65 up to 0.5 m/s, falls to 0.48 at 1.0 m/s and is zero from 1.5 m/s (reach/pick-time is a hard limit); detector size threshold and false-call rate move purity and recovery more than the pick-order policy, which changes nothing.
- [`research/infra-sortvalue`](infra-sortvalue) — Follow-up to infra-sorting: with TACO's class mix, assumed item values (drink can = 4 plastic bottles) and 0.5 to 1.5 s of arm travel, picking the highest-value item first beats FIFO by only 0.1% at 1 item/m, 2.0% at 6/m and 4.8% at 10/m, and value-per-second picking matches it; belt speed and pick time matter far more than pick order.
- [`research/infra-transformer`](infra-transformer) — Distribution transformer ageing under heat-pump and EV growth: an IEEE C57.91-form thermal model on TMY weather and a standard load profile; loss of life stays under 0.01%/yr until ~50% of homes electrify, then 5.6%/yr at 100% with uncoordinated charging against 1.07%/yr with valley-filled charging (constants swept, not verified).
- [`research/infra-waterloss`](infra-waterloss) — Water-leak detection from pressure residuals on EPANET's Net3 (WNTR): at a 5% weekly false-alarm budget, 5 sensors and 10% demand error, CUSUM finds all leaks the sensors can see at 4% of demand but 8% at 1% (3% of all leaks); demand error, not sensor noise or detector choice, sets the limit (simulated leaks; BattLeDIM L-Town was unreachable).
- [`research/infra-yield`](infra-yield) — Cost per good die from five yield models, a dies-per-wafer count and sourced wafer prices and defect densities: a 600 mm² die costs about $179 on a 7nm-class and $359 on a 5nm-class wafer, the yield model moves the 5nm figure by 16%, and two chiplets beat one die from about 300 mm² of logic (range 126-728 mm² over packaging cost and defect density).
- [`research/llm-coding-inference`](llm-coding-inference) — research programme: valid inference from LLM-coded qualitative data under group shift and rare categories; on 44,485 real tweets a pooled PPI interval covers as little as 67% when the audit's language mix differs from the corpus, a language-stratified one 94 to 96%
- [`research/lowcomm-training`](lowcomm-training) — research programme (proposal stage): a predictive theory of DiLoCo-style low-communication training, testing on real small transformers the closed forms derived in about a dozen quadratic-model projects, with 16 references, hypotheses H1 to H6 and an ICML/NeurIPS workshop as the first target
- [`research/marine-hull`](marine-hull) — research programme (proposal stage): energy per survey kilometre of hull types for 100 to 500 kg autonomous boats; with a Michell wave term a catamaran out-ranges a monohull in 3.3% of 6,561 cases, none at equal length; with Slocum coefficients from the gliderflight package a glider never out-ranges a same-mass propeller AUV at equal hotel load (0 of 2,430 cases); it breaks even when the AUV's hotel load is about 5 times its own; a REMUS-100-class AUV's non-propulsion load implied by vendor endurance is 104 to 175 W, far above that, so the glider out-ranges it in every case on energy per metre (at a tenth of the speed); a Savitsky planing hull reaches at most 0.14 of the monohull range (0 of 48 cases); a SWATH (two submerged torpedoes on struts) out-ranges the monohull in none of 4,374 swept pairs; charging panel mass to the battery (2 to 10 kg/m2, swept) cuts the median solar range multiplier from 2.01 to 1.64 at worst, and the full deck stays the best array wherever range is finite; against ten years of daily NASA POWER insolation a threshold speed policy (slow or hold when the pack is under 30%) never raises km/day over a fixed 1 m/s but cuts pack-empty days, e.g. 25.4% to 1.5% for a 4 m boat at 25 N
- [`research/marine-path`](marine-path) — plain line-of-sight guidance leaves a steady cross-track offset of delta*tan(asin(c/U)) in a cross current c (simulation matches it to 1%: 3.77 m at 0.3 m/s, 12 m look-ahead); integral LOS removes it, with lower rms cross-track error in 136 of 144 cross-current cases and the 8 exceptions all at a 6 m look-ahead with integral gain 2, where it oscillates (rms about 2.3 m even in still water)
- [`research/mimic-anycontext-size`](mimic-anycontext-size) — does a bigger Qwen make a better human-vs-machine detector? Partly: plain-prompt AUROC rises from 0.278 (0.5B) to 0.329 (1.5B), but 1.5B is still below chance in 84% of family-by-prompt cells.
- [`research/mimic-codeloop`](mimic-codeloop) — a 1.5B code model rewrites the cursor generator against the mimic-cursor detector: only 25 to 29% of its candidates run, and the reference detector stays at AUROC 1.000, tied with the component search at the same 24-candidate budget (one seed, preliminary negative).
- [`research/mimic-generatorholdout`](mimic-generatorholdout) — does a prompt-free detector transfer to unseen generators? Mostly: char+word n-grams keep AUROC 0.97 to 0.98 on a held-out chat model and 0.94 across a greedy-to-sampling shift; function-word cues alone drop to 0.69 to 0.85.
- [`research/mimic-looptransfer`](mimic-looptransfer) — do the mimic-loop imitator prompts fool detectors they never saw? No: matched on length, all 26 testable prompts are still caught (n-gram AUROC 0.83 to 0.96, Binoculars-style 0.84 to 1.00); low raw scores come from text length.
- [`research/mimic-webcam-markov`](mimic-webcam-markov) — can a Markov chain over a person's frames fool a detector? No: chains with 8, 32 or 128 states, fitted on one person or on everyone, emitting state centres or real frames, are caught at AUROC 1.000 (one cell 0.999), where stitching the same real frames in 50-frame blocks scored 0.57 to 0.65.
- [`research/mimic-webcam-persongen`](mimic-webcam-persongen) — does fitting to one person help? Copying a person's own earlier windows is caught at AUROC 0.59 to 0.65 on their later windows; copying other people is 0.58 to 0.91, and 0.93 to 0.97 once the detector knows the person; a fitted parametric generator is caught at 1.000 either way.
- [`research/mimic-webcam-semimarkov`](mimic-webcam-semimarkov) — does giving a chain over a person's frames dwell times or second-order memory help? No: semi-Markov and second-order chains (8 or 32 states, own or pooled fit, centre or real-frame emission) are all caught at AUROC 0.999 to 1.000.
- [`research/osrobot-deadlineloss`](osrobot-deadlineloss) — replaces the hard deadline of osrobot-deadline with a 2% yield loss per leaf stage of delay (Knezevic et al. 2003): the best pipeline costs 1.06 to 1.20 times the best inline fleet at 0.72 ha and 1.49 to 1.51 at 0.18 ha, and at 1.62 ha inline is cheaper when the window closes by 8 h and the pipeline from 12 h
- [`research/osrobot-dofbom`](osrobot-dofbom) — what does a 2-joint feeding arm cost against a 6-joint one once the whole bill of materials is priced? About 1.8 times, not 3: $66 against $122 at the SO-ARM101 table's $13.89 servo, rising to 2.4 times at a $49 retail servo.
- [`research/osrobot-fieldreal`](osrobot-fieldreal) — re-running the `osrobot-field` weeding model with sourced laser-weeder constants (2 m swath, 0.73 s per weed, 99.2% kill) halves the time but keeps the ranking: inline treatment takes 71.7 min against 79.3 for scout-then-treat at 0.3 weeds/m² and 1 km/h, and scout never wins across densities, speeds and detector hit rates
- [`research/osrobot-mjface`](osrobot-mjface) — at the ISO/TS 15066 face stiffness (75 N/mm) the blocked-arm impact peak only doubles (7.56 N against 3.68 N at 0.10 m/s), and every camera-arm trial stays under the 65 N and 130 N face limits (worst peak 53 N), where the assumed 2 N limit had flagged most; contact damping is unsourced and swept (MuJoCo, simulation only)
- [`research/osrobot-mjlayer`](osrobot-mjlayer) — with tissue as a 5 to 20 mm layer bonded to bone at 4 to 45 kPa, the force that indents it 30 % is 0.05 to 9 N, 1.3 to 1000 times below the simulated 12.6 to 53 N trial forces, so the arm needs a newton-scale force cap, not 65 N (published bottom-effect corrections, no FE run)
- [`research/osrobot-mjmodulus`](osrobot-mjmodulus) — published in-vivo face and lip moduli are 4 to 45 kPa, below the 0.05 to 5 MPa that osrobot-mjpress swept; at them a 4 mm tip at 53 N would sink 48 to 241 mm in a Hertz model, so the model is out of range and tip radius or depth, not 65 N, is the design question (closed-form, simulation forces)
- [`research/osrobot-mjpress`](osrobot-mjpress) — a 4 mm feeding-arm tip that meets the 65 N face force limit still needs 0.5 to 2.7 cm² of contact to meet a 110 to 20 N/cm² pressure limit at its worst trial (53 N); the answer flips on an unsourced face modulus (MuJoCo, simulation only)
- [`research/osrobot-mjsoft`](osrobot-mjsoft) — replacing the 75,000 N/m spring with a bonded tissue layer (4 to 45 kPa, 5 or 15 mm) cuts simulated peaks to 1 to 3 N in most cells but still breaks the 30 % depth limit in about half the trials; a 0.2 to 0.5 N cap fixes steady pushes, slowing to 0.02 m/s fixes a 10 kPa, 15 mm lip, and nothing fixes a thin 4 kPa one by speed alone (MuJoCo dynamics, closed-form tissue force)
- [`research/osrobot-sourcedprices`](osrobot-sourcedprices) — re-runs osrobot-deadlineloss with a Holybro X500 V2 drone (609 USD) and an AgileX Scout Mini (4,500 USD) at list price: pipeline over inline cost moves from 1.49 to 1.54 at 0.18 ha and 1.07 to 1.15 at 1.62 ha, ties at a 4,500 USD ground platform, and flips with an unsourced loaded flight time
- [`research/osrobot-uavcal`](osrobot-uavcal) — does the osrobot-uav multirotor energy model match real aircraft? With the Mavic 3's published 9.4 in propellers (0.239 m) it predicts 1.3 to 1.6 times the published 45 min endurance, so it is optimistic; the Anafi UKR rotor size was not found, so that row remains a sweep.
- [`research/osrobot-weedpass`](osrobot-weedpass) — a laser-weeding robot that drives three fast passes (4 km/h) kills 0.780 of the weeds in 75 min, against 0.607 in 83 min for one slow pass (1 km/h), and treating on the move instead of stopping cuts time by 6% at 0.3 weeds/m² and 39% at 3 weeds/m²; the ranking survives 30% of weeds being invisible to the detector
- [`research/osrobot-weedsrc`](osrobot-weedsrc) — with a detector fitted to two published field detection rates (88.9% at 0.2 m/s, 86.2% at 0.425 m/s), two laser-weeding passes at 4 km/h kill 0.952 in 40 min against 0.875 in 68 min for one pass at 1 km/h; the ranking holds, and the share of weeds the detector never sees (3 passes cap at 0.897 with 10% hidden) now sets the ceiling
- [`research/qual-agent`](qual-agent) — how well does an LLM-style coder count negative emotion in real comments? The Claude session agent, coding 300 GoEmotions comments blind, flags 44% as negative against a human 23% (opposite error to the classical stand-ins); accuracy 0.71 matches naive Bayes and trails a word list, and prediction-powered inference stays valid but 29% wider than hand-coding. One reader, not an API model.
- [`research/qual-dialect`](qual-dialect) — does an automated coder's error differ across languages? On 44,485 real tweets in Hausa, Igbo, Nigerian Pidgin and Yoruba, an English word-list coder and an English-trained classifier read almost none of the negative sentiment in Hausa, Igbo and Yoruba (sensitivity 0.4–11%), flipping some language gaps' sign (Hausa–Yoruba +10.6 points true, −6.5 reported); PPI keeps coverage at 93–96% but is never narrower than hand-coding. Not an LLM (no key)
- [`research/qual-equivalence`](qual-equivalence) — how big must a hand-coded audit be before you can trust a coder's counts? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, a tight equivalence test never passed the coder and a loose one passed it too often (coverage 0.35 at 1,600 audited items). Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-features`](qual-features) — do a dozen codebook codes predict better than bag-of-words when labels are scarce? On GoEmotions, 12 word-list codes reached 0.65 AUC at 100 labels against 0.56 for bag-of-words, and still led at 1,600 (0.75 against 0.67). Word lists, not an LLM; no learned embedding tested.
- [`research/qual-features-outcomes`](qual-features-outcomes) — do LLM-style qualitative codes predict outcomes better than bag-of-words at the same labelling budget? On 5,313 GoEmotions comments, not for a reply-vs-top-level outcome: emotion codes (human, an upper bound) reach AUC 0.527 against 0.557 for words and 0.595 for LSA, and 5% coder error erases their gain. All sets are weak; not an LLM.
- [`research/qual-gapaudit`](qual-gapaudit) — does a hand-coded audit protect a group gap when it only checks the overall count? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, a coder with zero overall bias still distorted a short-vs-long gap by 0.20, and the overall-bias gate covered the gap 0.4% of the time at 1,600 audited items. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-gapneg`](qual-gapneg) — can you estimate a group gap from an audit that hand-codes only a few of the coder's negatives? Not usefully: 0.9-wide intervals at 5 items, and a plain random audit (0.23 wide) beats the design. Pooling the groups' negatives covers 3%. 3,188 GoEmotions comments, not an LLM.
- [`research/qual-gate`](qual-gate) — if a hand-coded audit finds no bias in a coder, can you trust its counts? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, an audit-then-trust rule covered the truth in 8% of draws for a coder 2.6 points off, while always applying the prediction-powered correction covered 94&ndash;97%. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-highrecall`](qual-highrecall) — does a low audit rate for coder-negatives pay when the coder has high recall? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments the best share falls with recall (50%, 20%, 2%), but the narrowest interval (0.115&ndash;0.119 at 200 items) only matches a random audit with no coder (0.1195). Not an LLM.
- [`research/qual-induce`](qual-induce) — how much of a human emotion codebook does a codebook induced from the text alone recover? On 5,313 GoEmotions comments, NMF and k-means codebooks agree with the human emotion label above chance but weakly (NMI 0.26 vs 0.12 for 27 codes); the best single code per emotion reaches AUC about 0.6. Topic models, not an LLM.
- [`research/qual-labelaudit`](qual-labelaudit) — should you hand-code only what the coder called positive, or sample the audit by the coder's label? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, a positives-only audit had 0 of 1,000 intervals covering the truth for every coder (it misses the coder's false negatives); stratifying by label barely helped. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-lowrate`](qual-lowrate) — can you audit the coder's negatives at a lower rate and weight them back? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, weighting removes the bias of a positives-only audit but the interval widens as the negative share falls (0.126 at 50%, 0.352 at 5%, 200 items) and under-covers below ~10%. Not an LLM; coders miss half the positives.
- [`research/qual-manygroups`](qual-manygroups) — with six groups, how many hand-coded items does it take for every group's prevalence to be right? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, six 95% intervals covered all six truths in only 71 to 77% of 120 to 240-item equal audits (Bonferroni: 89 to 94%); a random audit could not estimate a 1.5% group at all. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-negaudit`](qual-negaudit) — how many of the coder's negatives must you hand-code to recover the positives it missed? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, about 50: coverage was 0.29 with 2, 0.87 with 10 and 0.94 with 50, whatever the audit's total size. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-negint`](qual-negint) — which confidence interval is right when you hand-code only 5 to 20 of the coder's negatives? Not Wald: 0.63 coverage at 5 items against 0.94 for Wilson or Jeffreys on 3,188 GoEmotions comments. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/qual-rareerror`](qual-rareerror) — does equal-numbers stratified auditing still match the optimal split when the coder errs more in the rare group? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, injecting up to 30% extra label flips in the rarest 11% group moved the optimal audit share only from 0.53 to 0.56, and equal numbers stayed within 0.7% of optimal width. Not an LLM.
- [`research/qual-register`](qual-register) — does a coder's error depend on writing register, and can a hand-coded sample tell? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, error differs by 1.9 points (naive-Bayes) or none (lexicon) between formal and informal text, yet the naive register gap is three times too large; an audit with up to 800 hand-coded items flags it no more often than chance. Not an LLM (no key); register is a proxy, not a dialect.
- [`research/qual-stratified`](qual-stratified) — should the hand-coded audit for a group gap be stratified by group? On 3,188 GoEmotions comments, it made no difference for a 36% group and a random audit gave intervals 1.7 times as wide for an 11% group at 100 items. Equal numbers per group matched the optimal split. Not an LLM; one corpus.
- [`research/religion-origins`](religion-origins) — a heavily cited synthesis of the evidence on how religion developed, from the cognitive science of belief and Palaeolithic burials through Göbekli Tepe, the first states, the Axial Age, Israelite monotheism, the Big Gods debate, early Christianity and the East–West Schism to 2020s data, read through scientific, historical and Catholic and Orthodox theological lenses, with what replicated and what did not
- [`research/sensing-ba`](sensing-ba) — with 12 views, 0.5 px noise and every camera pose wrong, bundle adjustment cuts mean surface error from 0.0117 to 0.0014 object radii (the true-pose floor), 8× here and 73× at 0.2 radii of position error, but only 1.4× at 4 px of pixel noise and not at all with 4 views. Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-bafocal`](sensing-bafocal) — with 12 views, wrong poses and a focal length told 5% wrong, a fixed-focal adjustment costs 1.2× the true-focal error (2.7× at 20%); solving for the focal returns the floor with the focal within 0.3%, and for k1 = −0.3 distortion 0.0034 falls to 0.0015, but not at 4 views or above 2 px noise. Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-bak2`](sensing-bak2) — rendering with k1 = −0.2 and k2 swept from −0.2 to +0.4, a bundle adjustment solving for the focal and k1 only returns 0.0015 object radii, the same as one that also solves k2, against 0.0022–0.0030 with no distortion model; the lens parameters are not separately identified (k1 stays near −0.16, k2 sits about 0.16 low) and the gain vanishes by 2 px noise. Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-bak2wide`](sensing-bak2wide) — moving the camera in and shortening the focal length (800 to 400 px) raises the largest normalised radius from 0.34 to 0.85, and the spread of the fitted k2 over seeds falls from 0.33 to 0.02 with its mean within 0.01 of truth; the surface then needs k2 (0.0027 with k1 only against 0.0020 with both). Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-baoutlier`](sensing-baoutlier) — with 12 views and wrong camera poses, 2% of observations replaced by random pixels already raise bundle-adjustment surface error from 0.0014 to 0.079 object radii, and Huber or Cauchy loss from the usual all-view start only halve that; a per-point RANSAC start fixes 5% (0.0319) but only 24 views get back to the clean floor at 10% (0.0012). Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-bapp`](sensing-bapp) — a 10 px principal-point offset held at the image centre raises the surface error from 0.0019 to 0.0043 object radii on the wide rig (focal 3.5% off) against 0.0019 to 0.0015 fitted on the narrow one; fitting the point recovers it and removes the cost. Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-burstcap`](sensing-burstcap) — inverting the burst break-even formula: the largest sensor-area ratio a K-frame burst matches runs from √K in the dark to K in bright light, so 8 frames at 3 e⁻ read noise match a 4×-area frame from 4.5 e⁻ per pixel per frame, a 6× frame from 21 e⁻ and never an 8× frame; Monte Carlo agrees to 1% in variance at 18 settings. Model only, swept ranges not sourced to a device
- [`research/sensing-calibviews`](sensing-calibviews) — on OpenCV's 13 real checkerboard photos, random subsets of 3 views put the focal length within 2% of the 13-view value in 99% of draws (5–95% band 2.3% wide), 12 views give 0.5%; noise-only re-renders predict the band, and a one-term lens model doubles held-out error. One camera, one board
- [`research/sensing-epigate`](sensing-epigate) — an epipolar gate removes 93% of 10% random-pixel mismatches (5% of good observations lost) and takes 5% contamination from 0.168 to 0.0061 object radii, but a RANSAC start does as well at 10% and better at 20%; near-miss mismatches are mild for a robust loss already. Synthetic only
- [`research/sensing-gatecorr`](sensing-gatecorr) — when a bad feature is wrong by the same offset in more consecutive views, the gate's recall falls from 0.81 to 0.59 at a 10% wrong rate and its lead over a RANSAC start shrinks from 1.7x to a tie within seed noise, though typical errors stay at 2 to 3.5 times the clean floor; a single failed seed in 12 can dominate a mean
- [`research/sensing-gatemix`](sensing-gatemix) — with the mismatch rates and mixes measured from SIFT on a real stereo pair, a ratio test of 0.8 to 0.6 (10% to 5% wrong) leaves gate + Cauchy adjustment at 0.008 to 0.003 object radii against 0.013 to 0.004 for the RANSAC start, and the all-random-pixel assumption overstated plain Cauchy's error 1.3 to 59 times; with no ratio test (56% wrong) every method fails
- [`research/sensing-kploc`](sensing-kploc) — on 30 warped real photographs SIFT keypoints localise to 0.13 px per axis at zero noise and 0.32 px at noise sigma 10 grey levels, 0.59 px at 40, so sensing-ba's assumed 0.5 px sits at roughly sigma 28; large keypoints are worse, blur matters little
- [`research/sensing-matchmix`](sensing-matchmix) — with the mismatch rates and along/near/far mixes measured for SIFT, a ratio test of 0.8 or tighter (10% wrong or fewer) leaves gate and RANSAC-start adjustment near the clean floor (0.002 to 0.02 radii) while an all-view start is 0.005 to 0.39; at ratio 0.9 the RANSAC start wins and with no ratio test (56% wrong) everything fails. Synthetic rig, measured mixes
- [`research/sensing-matchreal`](sensing-matchreal) — on a real Middlebury stereo pair SIFT with no ratio test is 56% wrong and 84% of the wrong ones are far, but at a 0.7 ratio test 6% are wrong and 62% of those lie on the true epipolar line (chance: 1%); ten warped photographs give 40% wrong without and 2% with ratio 0.6
- [`research/sensing-mixgate`](sensing-mixgate) — feeding sensing-matchreal's measured SIFT mismatch mixes into the sensing-epigate trial, gate plus Cauchy beats a RANSAC start at ratio tests of 0.8 and tighter (0.0078 against 0.0207 object radii at 0.8) and loses only at a loose 0.9 ratio test, where all methods exceed 0.08; synthetic geometry, 6 seeds
- [`research/sensing-nees`](sensing-nees) — with a 1/f accelerometer bias, a filter with no bias state claims 2.9 m after a 300 s GNSS outage when the RMS is 151 m (NEES/dof 514 to 2,000 at 100 ug); a random walk read off the Allan curve at 1 s or 10 s claims 2.4 to 3.7 times the true error, and at 100 s close to honest (0.9 to 1.6). Synthetic bias, swept instability
- [`research/sensing-viomarg`](sensing-viomarg) — a Schur-complement prior in the sensing-vio planar smoother cuts rms drift on 30 s paths from 12.3 % to 2.5 % of path (8 seeds, baseline IMU), and IMU grade then orders the results (0.25x noise 2.1 %, 1x 2.5 %, 4x 6.5 %), unlike with the pinned prior; planar and synthetic, not EuRoC
- [`research/sim2real-inference`](sim2real-inference) — research programme (milestones 3 to 6 logged): valid, bias-agnostic sim-to-real evaluation of robot policies, consolidating about 130 twin projects into one question, with 13 checked references, hypotheses H1 to H6 and CoRL as the target venue
- [`research/society-allies`](society-allies) — On a ring where people meet partners within 2 sites (memory 9), evenly spread robots at 12% tip every crowd but blocks of 3 or more tip none; at 20% blocks up to 3 still tip, and the largest tipping block grows with the neighbourhood (2 at k = 1 or 2, 4 at k = 3 or 5, 8 at k = 10).
- [`research/society-allyfade`](society-allyfade) — how fast can a bloc on the robots' side dissolve? With 20% allies converting at rate c per round, the least grid robot share that tips half of 12 crowds rises from 0.02 (no fading) to 0.05, 0.10, 0.10, 0.15 and 0.20 at c = 0.001 to 0.1; tips form in about 50 rounds and persist once allies are gone. Stylised
- [`research/society-allyrecent`](society-allyrecent) — allies who fade by the share of R among only their last k partners (k = 1, 3, 5, 9) tip about as many crowds as the whole-memory rule: at c = 0.01 and robots 0.10, 11 to 12 of 12 against 10 for constant fading; at c = 0.03, robots 0.15, 12 of 12 against 10. Differences between k are one crowd of 12. Stylised
- [`research/society-allywatch`](society-allywatch) — allies who convert only when they have watched the crowd walk the other way. With 20% allies, fading at rate c times the share of R in their memory tips more crowds than a constant rate c once robots are 0.10 or more (12/12 vs 10/12 at c = 0.01, robots 0.10; 4/12 vs 0/12 at c = 0.03), but does nothing below 0.05. Stylised
- [`research/society-cheapcue`](society-cheapcue) — how much cheaper must an urgency cue be for the urgent before a door robot can rely on it? Urgent people must pay at most about half what normal people do; the crowd is worse off than under yield-all in every cell.
- [`research/society-doorcost`](society-doorcost) — at what collision cost does a door stop tempting robots to go first? With 30% robots and dithering cost 1, always-yielding robots are individually stable only above a collision cost of 2.80 (waiting cost 1); below it a single robot gains by going though yielding is best for the crowd. The threshold rises with dithering cost and robot share.
- [`research/society-doorway`](society-doorway) — when robots always yield at a door, do people learn to walk through first, and who pays? Always-yielding robots raise the share of people who go from 0.388 to 0.595 at 50% robots and cut the crowd's cost (1.064 to 0.968); always-going robots raise it to 1.373. Mean field and simulation agree within 0.003.
- [`research/society-enforcers`](society-enforcers) — how many enforcer robots hold a queue against line-cutting robots? About 5% of the crowd holds the norm against 30% cutters (memory 9, 10 people in view of each cut, stop chance 0.5), simulation agrees. Stylised: view size and stop chance are swept, not sourced.
- [`research/society-exposure`](society-exposure) — do stubborn walkers who convert after meeting robots let a smaller robot minority tip a crowd's walking side? At 24% robots every crowd tips for k = 30 meetings (median round 171) though the fixed block stops it; at 16-20% none tip.
- [`research/society-fakeurgency`](society-fakeurgency) — can a robot that yields to people in a hurry survive people faking a hurry? With 30% robots, no signal cost on a 0 to 1.2 grid lets a signal-reading fleet beat yield-all for the crowd, and urgent people keep about 6% of the gain they get from perfect reading.
- [`research/society-grid`](society-grid) — On a 16x16 torus (memory 9), robot patches up to about 2k+1 sites wide tip every crowd at robot share 0.25 and wider ones tip few or none; one 8x8 patch of all the robots tips none at any neighbourhood radius up to 5.
- [`research/society-handover`](society-handover) — is a hand-over cheaper than buying robots first? With 20% allies fading at rate c and 10% robots at the start, replacing half of fading allies with robots tips 10 of 12 crowds at c = 0.03 but ends with 0.19 robots, against 0.15 robots upfront in society-allyfade; below 5% initial robots only full replacement tips anything. Stylised
- [`research/society-learncue`](society-learncue) — do door robots that learn from their own costs keep honouring an urgency cue? They keep yielding to cue senders but stop going against everyone else, which erases most of the cue's gain to urgent people.
- [`research/society-learnrobots`](society-learnrobots) — do robots that learn at a door drift to going first when collisions are cheap? No: with 30% robots they settle on a mix whose go share falls smoothly with collision cost (0.51 at k = 1.5, 0.03 at k = 2.8), whatever they start from, at a higher crowd cost than a fixed yielding fleet for k of 1 or more.
- [`research/society-lineup`](society-lineup) — how many line-cutting robots break a queueing norm? Mean-field critical share 12.7% (memory 9, lone-cut objection 0.9), simulation agrees; depends mostly on how fast objection fades. Stylised: objection strength is swept, not sourced.
- [`research/society-milgramfit`](society-milgramfit) — do real objection rates keep a queue norm? People alone need to stop a lone cut at least 0.786 of the time; Milgram's field reactions (about 0.5, secondary source) imply 7.5% enforcer robots even with no cutters. Inputs not read from the paper; p0 is swept.
- [`research/society-peoplecost`](society-peoplecost) — does a signal-reading door fleet help people once robots are not counted? Only if the urgent cue costs them about a third of what it costs others, or a planner weights urgent waiting; an equal-cost cue hurts people at every weight tried.
- [`research/society-ringmf`](society-ringmf) — A deterministic local mean-field agrees with the society-allies simulation on tip/no-tip in 145 of 160 cells and is almost never too optimistic, but it overstates the share of evenly spread robots needed (0.20 against 0.12 at k = 2).
- [`research/society-ringnoise`](society-ringnoise) — Neither alone: a noisy memoryless map tips at 0.06 robot share for spread robots, the simulation at 0.10 and the deterministic mean-field at 0.18 (k = 2).
- [`research/society-sidefit`](society-sidefit) — Fitting the stubborn-bloc model to the side preference Moussaïd et al. (2009) measured for two head-on walkers (about 17.5% left, read from their Fig. 4) puts the mean-field tipping robot share at 0.075 to 0.096 for memory 9 across error rates 0.02 to 0.30, against 0.217 for an unbiased crowd; memory matters more (0.033 to 0.053 at 5, 0.133 to 0.149 at 17) and a stubborn-right bloc raises it.
- [`research/society-urgentdoor`](society-urgentdoor) — should a robot yield only to people in a hurry, or to everyone? With 30% robots and 20% urgent people, a fleet that yields only to the urgent cuts their cost by 40% (1.130 to 0.676) at a 3% cost to everyone else, is individually stable, and costs the robots 29%.
- [`research/swe-costdrift`](swe-costdrift) — on 1,053 priced run records (16 to 21 October 2026) the median research run grew about 4.9% a day in API-equivalent cost (95% interval +2.8 to +7.0) and 5.7% a day in tool calls, while cost per call stayed flat; ventures runs show no drift (preliminary, observational, six days)
- [`research/verifiable-training-mechanisms`](verifiable-training-mechanisms) — research programme: incentive-compatible verification of outsourced ML training; first measurement on real training finds a tolerance-tau referee passes a skip-steps cheat on 30% of steps at tau = 1e-2 while the held-out loss stays within one seed std, and the loss only suffers at tau about 10x the worst benign drift; milestone 4 prices contracts by linear programme: with a tolerance, audit-only cannot enforce full effort and the joint contract beats both checks only when the score is precise (4 of 45 cells); milestone 6: against a coordinating majority replication adds no deterrence beyond the audit and the stake is flat in the number of workers (140 cells); milestone 5: an agreement term between redundant workers is worth nothing beyond the audit once cheats copy one trajectory (0 of 1,349 random instances), and a proved one-sided bound max(C, g/p - S) holds in 2,755 instances but is vacuous for a noisy score
- [`research/verified-dse`](verified-dse) — milestones 1 to 3 done: on 10 designs x 60 ABC pass sequences, the generic-gate score does not rank like routed SKY130 area x delay (tau-b median -0.04), and liberty mapping mis-ranks too when the clock is tight (tau 0.59); at equal wall-clock a one-shot cheap-rung screen does not beat random or hill-climbing search on the cached table; successive halving across rungs does not beat random routed search on mean regret either (plain halving from the generic-gate rung is worst; a 6-point routed pilot to pick the start rung fixes most of that, H3), a 10-point routed pilot plus a conformal bound on the mapped rung keeps the routed optimum 0.99 of the time (nominal 0.90) at 42 to 57% of the routed runs, but on the generic-gate rung coverage fails (0.65 against 0.80, H4 refuted there); proof-success prediction (H5): pooled AUC 0.96 to 0.998 across 15 designs is just design size (an oracle knowing only each design's success rate scores the same), and inside the one design with variation it is 0.65 to 0.78, so H5 is not supported; milestone 6 proves that halving's routed regret is at most the sum of the cut rungs' largest ranking inversions (vacuous for the generic-gate rung, loose for the mapped ones); milestone 7a adds a distribution-free pilot quantile of pair inversions (16-point pilots hold the nominal 0.8 coverage in all 60 group-rung cells, median 0.32 of the maximum), whose summed bound is under 0.1 on 9 of 10 groups for the mapped rungs at a relaxed clock but is violated by realised halving regret in 1 of 40 cells, so it is a heuristic, not a guarantee
<!-- projects:end -->
- [`research/build-data`](build-data) — research programme (proposal stage): do open building-permit feeds from several US cities, on one schema, predict permit and build duration better than each city's own quantiles; feed probe so far found Chicago issued-only, San Francisco and Austin with completion dates; no model run yet
